\chapter{First-Order Logic}
\section{Terminology}
A \keyword{formal system} is an abstraction of deductive reasoning the same way arithmetic is an abstraction of counting.
Structurally, a formal system comprises of the following components:
\begin{enumerate*}[label=(\arabic*)]
    \item formal language;
    \item rules of inference.
\end{enumerate*}
\begin{equation*}
    \text{Formal System} = \text{Formal Language} + \text{Rules of Inference}
\end{equation*}
A formal language is a well-defined collection of symbolic strings called \keyword{formulas}.
A rule of inference is a precise rule for deriving a new formula from a collection of formulas.
A \keyword{formal theory} is any subcollection of formulas belonging to a formal language.
Each formula in a formal theory is called an \keyword{axiom} of the theory.
If \( T \) is a formal theory associated with a formal system \( \mathcal{F} \) and \( \varphi \) is a formula derived from \( T \) via recursive applications of
the rules of inference of \( \mathcal{F} \), then we say that \( \varphi \) is a \keyword{theorem} of \( T \).

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
Most mathematical statements are expressible using a class of formal systems known as \keyword{first-order logic}.
The formal languages associated with first-order logic are called \keyword{first-order languages}.
The goal of this chapter is twofold:
\begin{enumerate*}[label=(\arabic*)]
    \item define first-order logic mathematically,
    \item derive all the properties of first-order logic that are useable in the rest of mathematics.
\end{enumerate*}

\section{Philosophical Concerns}
It is known that most if not all of mathematics can be formulated within the framework of axiomatic set theory (ZFC), which itself is formulated as a
first-order theory\footnote{A formal theory associated with first-order logic.}.
Nevertheless, sets and logic cannot exist independently; that is, ideas pertaining to sets and logic are inherently connected.
For instance, the fundamental set-theoretic constructions (unions, intersections, negations) are really just logical operations in disguise.
Similarly, the semantics of first-order languages cannot be fully defined without invoking some notion of sets---i.e., it is not possible to capture the full meaning of
universally quantified statements without explaining it in terms of sets or collections\footnote{ By making statements like ``for all \( x \), \( P(x) \) is true,'' we are alluding to the existence of a (possibly empty) universal collection containing all things.}.

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
Immediately, we are presented with a philosophical problem. If sets and logic are conceptually dependent, how do we justify the use of first-order logic
in the formulation of axiomatic set theory (ZFC)?
After all, the theorems of ZFC are proven using logical principles that are based on the properties of sets;
and yet they are also sentences \emph{about} the properties of sets!

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
The solution to this problem is to develop a \keyword{metatheory} of first-order logic that exists outside of ZFC.
Within this metatheory, the intuitive properties of sets will be restated and subsequently used to justify the properties of
first-order logic. Since the properties of sets are now reformulated in the language of the metatheory rather than a language associated with first-order logic,
there is no circularity in the justification.

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
The metatheory of logic could, in principle, be a formal system of its own right.
The formal approach, however, comes with its own set of problems.
Suppose we pick a formal system as our meta-theoretic starting point.
Then what awaits us is a possibly endless recursion of justifications.
For instance, we would have to justify the formal metatheory itself
with a \emph{meta}-metatheory, and the meta-metatheory with a \emph{meta}-meta-metatheory and so on until
we decide that a particular formal system in the recursive chain is self-evident enough to not require any further justification.

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
Our preferred approach is to skip all the fanfare and just formulate the metatheory in plain English
and to do it in such a way that the resulting metatheory is self-evident.
Note that our choice in using natural language as the metalanguage does not preclude the use of symbolisms in the metatheory.
Symbolic notations will be used wherever convenient\footnote{It is more accurate to think
of the metatheory as a semi-formal system of reasoning rather than a purely informal one.}.

\section{Metatheory}
The following are considered \keyword{primitive notions} in our metatheory of logic: `true', `false',
`not', `and', `or', `if', `imply', `iff', `all', `every', `any', `some', `for all', `for any', `there exists', `conjunction', `disjunction', `negation',
`implication', `converse', `object', `collection', `aggregate', `symbol', `sentence', `rule', `identity', `same', `unique', `arbitrary', `universe'.
By this, we mean that these terms are assumed to be understood a priori and hence will not be explicitly defined.
The primitives are the basic building blocks upon which all other concepts in our metatheory are built.

\begin{dfn}[Statements]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-statement}
    A \keyword{statement} is any sentence in English that can be judged unambiguously
    to be either true or false.
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Statements are sometimes abbreviated symbolically for brevity and convenience. E.g., \( P \), \( Q \), \( R \), \( \dots \) etc.
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}[Expressions]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-expressions}
    An \keyword{expression} is any linguistic construct that, when interpreted, refers to a unique object in the universe.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Axioms]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-axioms}
    Self-evident statements are known as \keyword{axioms}.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Vacuous Truth]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-vacuous-truth}
    A statement of the form ``if \( P \), then \( Q \)'', ``\( Q \) if \( P \)'', or ``\( P \) implies \( Q \)''
    is \keyword{vacuously true} iff \( P \) is a false statement.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Placeholders]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-placeholders}
    A \keyword{placeholder} is a symbol that appears in an English sentence in place of a noun.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Predicates]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-predicate}
    A \keyword{predicate} is any sentence in English that contains at least one placeholder and whose truth can be unambiguously determined
    by replacing the placeholder(s) with objects in the universe.
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We use functional notation to denote predicates as well as expressions containing placeholders.
    For example, \( P(n) \) denotes a predicate with the symbol \( n \) representing its placeholder.
\end{notation}

\begin{rem}
    A predicate whose placeholders have been replaced with objects becomes a statement.
\end{rem}

\begin{notation}
    If \( A \) denotes an object in the universe and \( \varphi(x) \) denotes a predicate, then
    \( \varphi(A) \) denotes the statement obtained from \( \varphi(x) \) by replacing \( x \) with \( A \).
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Satisfaction]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-satisfaction}
    An object \( A \) \keyword{satisfies} a predicate \( \varphi(x) \) iff \( \varphi(A) \) is a true statement.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Equality]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-equality}
    Two objects are \keyword{equal} iff they have the same identity; i.e., they are the same object and are indistinguishable from each other.
    Two objects are \keyword{distinct} iff they are not equal.
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We write \( x = y \) and \( x \neq y \) to abbreviate the statements ``\( x \) and \( y \) are equal'' and
    ``\( x \) and \( y \) are distinct'' respectively.
\end{notation}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:equality-axioms}
    The following statements about equality are true for any two objects \( A \) and \( B \) in the universe:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( A = A \)
        \item If \( A = B \), then \( B = A \)
        \item If \( A = B \) and \( B = C \), then \( A = C \)
        \item If \( A = B \) and \( \varphi(x) \) is a predicate, then \( \varphi(A) \) iff \( \varphi(B) \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{dfn}[Uniqueness]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-unique-satisfaction}
    An object \( A \) is said to \keyword{uniquely} satisfy a predicate \( \varphi(x) \) iff the statement
    ``for all \( x \),  \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x = A \)'' is true.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Sets]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-sets}
    A \keyword{set} is an object formed by collecting or grouping together single objects into an aggregate.
    Constituents of a set are called the \keyword{elements} or \keyword{members} of the set.
    If an object is an element of a set, then it \keyword{belongs to} that set.
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We write \( x \in A \) to abbreviate the statements ``\( x \) is an element of the set \( A \),''
    ``\( x \) is a member of \( A \),'' or ``\( x \) belongs to \( A \).''
\end{notation}

\begin{rem}
    For most purposes, it suffices to treat sets as abstract containers that are constructible
    from the empty set by ``filling up'' the empty set with well-defined objects.
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}[Set-builder Notation]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-setbuilder-notation}
    If \( \varphi(x) \) is a predicate, then we write \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) to denote the set
    whose elements are exactly those objects that \aref{dfn:metatheory-satisfaction}{satisfy} \( \varphi(x) \);
    more specifically, we read \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) as ``the set of all \( x \) such that \( \varphi(x) \) is satisfied.''
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    The notation \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) can only be used if we can \emph{prove} the existence of the set it denotes.
    There certainly are predicates in which \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) do not exist\footnote{Russell's Paradox.}.
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}[Subsets]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), we say that \( A \) is a \keyword{subset} of \( B \) iff
    the statement ``for all \( x \), if \( x \in A \), then \( x \in B \)'' is true.
    Furthermore, we say that \( A \) is a \keyword{proper subset} of \( B \) iff:
    \begin{enumerate*}[label=(\arabic*)]
        \item \( A \) is a subset of \( B \),
        \item there exists an element in \( B \) that is not an element of \( A \).
    \end{enumerate*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We write \( A \subseteq B \) and \( A \subset B \) to abbreviate the statements ``\( A \) is a subset of \( B \)'' and
    ``\( A \) is a proper subset of \( B \)'' respectively.
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Singleton]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-singleton}
    A \keyword{singleton} is any set that contains only a single element.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Family of Sets]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-family-of-sets}
    A \keyword{family} of sets is a set whose elements are also sets.
\end{dfn} 

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:extensionality}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), \( A = B \) iff
    ``for all \( x \), \( x \in A \) iff \( x \in B \)'' is a true statement.
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:existence-of-empty-set}
    There exists a set that does not contain any elements.
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:pairing}
    For any two objects \( A \) and \( B \) in the universe, there exists a set whose elements are exactly \( A \) and \( B \).
    We call such a set an \keyword{unordered pair} containing \( A \) and \( B \).
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:union}
    For any family of sets \( S \), there exists a set whose elements are exactly the object(s) belonging to some element of \( S \).
    We call such a set the \keyword{union} of \( S \).
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:power-set}
    For any set \( S \), there exists a set whose elements are exactly the subsets of \( S \).
    We call such a set the \keyword{power set} of \( S \).
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:specification}
    For any set \( S \) and any predicate \( \varphi(x) \), there exists a set whose elements are exactly the elements of \( S \) that satisfy \( \varphi(x) \).
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:regularity}
    For any non-empty family of sets \( S \), there exists a \( y \in S \) such that for all \( x \), \( x \notin S \) or \( x \notin y \).
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{rem}
    Note that we have simply restated the axioms of \( ZFC \) in our metalanguage (plain English) so that we can reference them in the metatheory without circularity.
\end{rem}

\begin{meta-rule}[Non-Contradiction]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:law-of-non-contradiction}
    For any statement \( P \), \( P \) is a true statement iff not \( P \) is a false statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Excluded Middle]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:law-of-excluded-middle}
    For any statement \( P \), the statements ``\( P \) or not \( P \)'' and ``not \( P \) or \( P \)'' are true.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Conjunction Introduction]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:conjunction-introduction}
    If \( P \) and \( Q \) are both true statements, then ``\( P \) and \( Q \)'' and ``\( Q \) and \( P \)'' are both true statements.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Conjunction Elimination]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:conjunction-elimination}
    If ``\( P \) and \( Q \)'' is a true statement, then both \( P \) and \( Q \) are true statements.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Disjunction Introduction]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:disjunction-introduction}
    If \( P \) is a true statement, then ``\( P \) or \( Q \)'' and ``\( Q \) or \( P \)'' are both true statements for any statement \( Q \).
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Disjunction Elimination]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:disjunction-elimination}
    If ``if \( P \) then \( Q \)'', ``if \( R \) then \( Q \)'', and ``\( P \) or \( R \)'' are all true statements, then \( Q \) must also be true statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[De Morgan's Laws]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:de-morgans-laws}
    For any statements \( P \) and \( Q \):
    \begin{enumerate*}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item ``\( P \) and \( Q \)'' is false iff ``not \( P \) or not \( Q \)'' is true;
        \item ``\( P \) or \( Q \)'' is false iff ``not \( P \) and not \( Q \)'' is true.
    \end{enumerate*}
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Material Implication]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:material-implication}
    For any two statements \( P \) and \( Q \), the statement ``if \( P \) then \( Q \)'' is true iff ``not \( P \) or \( Q \)'' is true.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Modus Ponens]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:modus-ponens}
    If \( P \) and ``if \( P \) then \( Q \)'' are both true statements, then \( Q \) must also be a true statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Modus Tollens]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:modus-tollens}
    If \( Q \) is a false statement and ``if \( P \) then \( Q \)'' is a true statement, then \( P \) must be false statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Disjunctive Syllogism]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}
    If ``\( P \) or \( Q \)'' is a true statement and \( P \) is a false statement, then \( Q \) must be a true statement.
    Similarly, if ``\( P \) or \( Q \)'' is a true statement and \( Q \) is a false statement, then \( P \) must be a true statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Hypothetical Syllogism]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:hypothetical-syllogism}
    For any statements \( P \), \( Q \), and \( R \), if ``\( P \) implies \( Q \)'' and ``\( Q \) implies \( R \)'' are both true statements, then
    ``\( P \) implies \( R \)'' must be a true statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Constructive Dilemma]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:constructive-dilemma}
    If ``\( P \) implies \( Q \)'', ``\( R \) implies \( S \)'', ``\( P \) or \( R \)'' are all true statements, then ``\( Q \) or \( S \)'' is also
    a true statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Reductio ad Absurdum]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:reductio-ad-absurdum}
    If \( P \) and ``if \( Q \) then not \( P \)'' are both true statements, then \( Q \) must be a false statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Universal Generalization]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an \emph{arbitrary} object in the universe and \( \varphi(x) \) denote a predicate.
    If \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement, then ``for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \)'' is also a true statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Universal Instantiation]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:universal-instantiation}
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an \emph{arbitrary} object in the universe and \( \varphi(x) \) denote a predicate.
    If ``for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \)'' is a true statement, then \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is also a true statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{meta-rule}[Existential Generalization]
    \phantomsection \label{meta-rule:existential-generalization}
    Let \( \varphi(x) \) denote a predicate. If \( A \) denotes an object in the universe and \( \varphi(A) \) is a true statement,
    then ``there exists an \( x \) such that \( \varphi(x) \)'' is also a true statement.
\end{meta-rule}

\begin{rem}
    These inference rules are accepted as valid without justification.
    In more philosophical terms, we say that they are a form of \keyword{a priori} knowledge---known by virtue of our rational faculties as opposed to our experience.
    The reader should have no difficulty convincing himself of their validity.
\end{rem}

\noindent
We now utilize \crefrange{meta-axiom:equality-axioms}{meta-axiom:regularity}
and \crefrange{meta-rule:law-of-non-contradiction}{meta-rule:existential-generalization}
to derive some basic results of our metatheory.

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}
    Two sets are equal iff they are subsets of each other.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( A \) and \( B \) be any two sets.
    Suppose that \( A = B \).
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    If \( \alpha \in A \), then necessarily \( \alpha \in B \) by \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality,meta-rule:universal-instantiation}.
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we have for all \( x \), \( x \in A \) implies \( x \in B \) by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( A \subseteq B \) is true.
    Similarly, let \( \beta \) denote an arbitary object in the univese.
    If \( \beta \in B \), then necessarily \( \beta \in A \) by \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality,meta-rule:universal-instantiation}.
    Again, since \( \beta \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that for all \( x \), \( x \in B \) implies \( x \in A \),
    which is synonymous to \( B \subseteq A \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}.
    In other words, we have shown that if \( A = B \) is true, then \( A \subseteq B \) and \( B \subseteq A \) must both be true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    To prove the converse, suppose that \( A \subseteq B \) and \( B \subseteq A \) are both true.
    Now let \( \gamma \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \gamma \in A \). Since \( A \subseteq B \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets,meta-rule:universal-instantiation}
    that \( \gamma \in B \). In other words, \( \gamma \in A \) implies \( \gamma \in B \).
    Conversely, if \( \gamma \in B \), then \( \gamma \in A \) is necessarily true by virtue of \( B \subseteq A \) and \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation}.
    Thus, \( \gamma \in B \) implies \( \gamma \in A \).
    Since \( \gamma \in A \) and \( \gamma \in B \) imply each other, we conclude that \( \gamma \in A \) iff \( \gamma \in B \).
    With \( \gamma \) being arbitrary, \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} allows us to draw the conclusion that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in A \) iff \( x \in B \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}, this is synonymous to \( A = B \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-specification-subset}
    Let \( \varphi(x) \) be any predicate and \( S \) be any set.
    Then the set containing all the elements of \( S \) that satisfy \( \varphi(x) \) is unique.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( T \) and \( T^{\prime} \) be sets containing the elements of \( S \) that satisfy \( \varphi(x) \).
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in T \).
    Then \( \alpha \in S \) and \( \varphi(\alpha) \) are necessarily true statements by definition.
    But \( T^{\prime} \) is also a set containing all the elements of \( S \) that satisfy \( \varphi(x) \).
    This means that \( \alpha \in T^{\prime} \) is also true.
    In other words, \( \alpha \in T \) implies \( \alpha \in T^{\prime} \).
    The converse can be demonstrated to be true by the same argument.
    Thus, we conclude that \( \alpha \in T \) iff \( \alpha \in T^{\prime} \).
    With \( \alpha \) being arbitrary, we conclude that \( T = T^{\prime} \) by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} and
    \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}.
\end{proof}

\begin{notation}
    If we wish to impose an additonal condition upon the placeholder when using set-builder notation,
    then we can write the predicate (through which the desired condition is imposed) on the left side of the ``\( \vert \)'' symbol.
    We call this variant of the set-builder notation the \keyword{conditional} set-builder notation.
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Conditional Set-builder]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-conditional-set-builder-notation}
    Let \( \varphi(x) \) and \( \psi(x) \) be two predicates. Then \( \setbuilder{\phi(x)}{\varphi(x)} \)
    denotes the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\text{\( \phi(x) \) and \( \varphi(x) \)}} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{eg}
    The set whose uniqueness we have just proven in \cref{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-specification-subset} can then be denoted as
    \( \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \) with \( \phi(x) \) being the predicate ``\( x \in S \).''
\end{eg}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-restricted-comprehension-set-is-the-subset-of-an-existing-set}
    For any given set \( S \) and any given predicate \( \varphi(x) \), \( \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \subseteq S \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object. Suppose that \( \alpha \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \).
    By definition, \( \alpha \in S \) and \( \varphi(\alpha) \) are both true statements.
    In other words, \( \alpha \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \) implies \( \alpha \in S \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} yields
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( y \), \( y \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \) implies \( y \in S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, clearly \( \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \subseteq S \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set}
    For any predicate \( \varphi(x) \), if there exists a set \( S \) such that \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in S \) for all \( x \),
    then the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists and \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} = \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose there exists a set \( S \) such that \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in S \) for all \( x \).
    By \cref{meta-axiom:specification}, we know that the set \( \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \) exists.
    We now prove that \( x \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \) iff \( \varphi(x) \) for all \( x \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{ \varphi(x) } \).
    By definition, it follows that \( \alpha \in S \) and \( \varphi(\alpha) \) are both true.
    In other words, \( \alpha \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \) implies \( \varphi(\alpha) \).
    To prove the converse, suppose that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement.
    Since ``for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in S \)'' is a true statement, it follows from
    \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation,meta-rule:modus-ponens} that \( \alpha \in S \).
    With \( \varphi(\alpha) \) and \( \alpha \in S \) both being true, necessarily \( \alpha \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \).
    This means that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) implies \( \alpha \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We have thus shown that the statements \( \varphi(\alpha) \) and \( \alpha \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \) imply each other.
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    for all \( x \), \( x \in \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \) iff \( \varphi(x) \).
    In other words, \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} = \setbuilder{x \in S}{\varphi(x)} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-empty-set}
    The set without any elements is unique.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose that \( A \) and \( B \) are sets that do no contain any elements.
    Consider the following predicate,
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq x \in A \ \text{iff}\  x \in B
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Since \( A \) and \( B \) are empty sets, it follows that
    \( \alpha \notin A \) and \( \alpha \notin B \) are both true statements.
    In other words, \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is true for any arbitrary object \( \alpha \) in the universe.
    Thus, we infer from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that the statement ``for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \)'' is a true statement.
    It follows from \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality} that \( A = B \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-unique-satisfaction}, the empty set is indeed unique.
\end{proof}

\begin{notation}
    Since there exists only one empty set in the universe, we can denote it with a unique symbol \( \emptyset \).
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-empty-set}
    The set \( \emptyset \) is the unique set that does not contain any element.
    In other words, if \( S \) is a set, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{\( S = \emptyset \) iff for all \( x \), \( x \notin S \)}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-empty-set-is-the-subset-of-every-set}
    For any set \( S \), \( \emptyset \subseteq S \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Since \( \emptyset \) has no elements, if follows that \( \alpha \notin \emptyset \).
    Thus, the statement ``if \( \alpha \in \emptyset \), then \( \alpha \in S \)'' is \aref{dfn:metatheory-vacuous-truth}{vacuously true}.
    Given that \( \alpha \) denotes an arbitrary object in the universe, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:S-is-subset-of-empty-set}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \emptyset \) implies \( x \in S \)}
    \end{equation}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, necessarily \( \emptyset \subseteq S \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-subset-of-an-empty-set-must-be-empty}
    For any set \( S \), if \( S \subseteq \emptyset \), then \( S = \emptyset \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in S \) is true.
    Since \( S \subseteq \emptyset \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in S \) implies \( x \in \emptyset \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Applying \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to \eqref{eq:S-is-subset-of-empty-set} then yields
    \begin{equation*}
        \alpha \in S \ \text{implies} \ \alpha \in \emptyset
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \alpha \in S \), it follows from \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens} that \( \alpha \in \emptyset \), which
    contradicts \cref{dfn:metatheory-empty-set}.
    Therefore, the supposition that \( \alpha \in S \) must be false; that is, \( \alpha \notin S \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \notin S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-empty-set}, necessarily \( S = \emptyset \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-unordered-pair}
    Let \( A \) and \( B \) be objects in the universe. Then the unordered pair containing \( A \) and \( B \) is unique.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) and \( T \) be unordered pairs containing \( A \) and \( B \).
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe such that \( \alpha \in S \).
    Since \( S \) contains exactly \( A \) and \( B \), the statement ``\( \alpha = A \) or \( \alpha = B \)'' must be true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \alpha = A \), then \( \alpha \in T \) is true because \( T \) is also an unordered pair containing \( A \) and \( B \).
    Similarly, if \( \alpha = B \), then \( \alpha \in T \) is also true for the same reason.
    We now conclude by \cref{meta-rule:disjunction-elimination} that \( \alpha \in T \) must be a true statement.
    In other words, the implication ``if \( x \in S \), then \( x \in T \)'' is true.
    The converse can be established with a similar argument.
    Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        \alpha \in S \ \text{iff} \ \alpha \in T
    \end{equation*}
    where \( \alpha \) denotes an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Finally, we conclude \( S = T \) by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} and \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}.
\end{proof}

\begin{notation}
    We will write \( \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) or \( \mleft\{ B, A \mright\} \) to denote the unordered pair containing exactly \( A \) and \( B \).
    Note that if \( A \) and \( B \) are the same set, the unordered pair is really just the singleton \( \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) (or \( \mleft\{ B \mright\} \)).
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Unordered Pair]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-unordered-pair}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), the \keyword{unordered pair} containing \( A \) and \( B \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} = \mleft\{ B, A \mright\} = \setbuilder{x}{x = A \ \text{or} \ x = B}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-sufficient-conditions-for-singleton}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty set and \( A \) be an object in the universe.
    If \( x \in S \) implies \( x = A \) for all \( x \), then \( S = \mleft\{ A \mright\} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    It should be obvious that \( S \subseteq \mleft\{ A \mright\} \).
    Since \( S \) is non-empty, there must exists an \( \alpha \) such that \( \alpha \in S \).
    Applying \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to the statement ``for all \( x \), \( x \in S \) implies \( x = A \)''
    yields the statement ``\( \alpha \in S \) implies \( \alpha = A \)''.
    We thus conclude from \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens} that \( \alpha = A \).
    Now let \( \beta \) be an arbitrary object such that \( \beta \in \mleft\{ A \mright\} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-unordered-pair}, it follows that \( \beta = A \).
    By \cref{meta-axiom:equality-axioms}, we conclude that \( \alpha = \beta = A \).
    Since \( \alpha \in S \) and \( \alpha = \beta \), it follows from \cref{meta-axiom:equality-axioms} once again that \( \beta \in S \).
    In other words, \( \beta \in \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) implies \( \beta \in S \).
    Since \( \beta \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) implies \( x \in S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore, \( \mleft\{ A \mright\} \subseteq S \).
    Since \( S \) and \( \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) are subsets of each other, they must be equal by \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-union}
    Let \( S \) be any family of sets. Then the union of \( S \) is unqiue.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( U \) and \( V \) be the unions of \( S \). We must show that \( U = V \). Consider the predicate
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \ \text{\( x \in E \) for some \( E \in S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe. Suppose that \( \alpha \in U \).
    By \cref{meta-axiom:union}, it follows that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement.
    Let \( E^{\prime} \) denote the element of \( S \) such that \( \alpha \in E^{\prime} \).
    But \( V \) is also a union of \( S \), so necessarily \( \alpha \in V \) by \cref{meta-axiom:union}.
    In other words, \( \alpha \in U \) implies \( \alpha \in V \).
    The converse can be proven with a similar argument, and we thus have \( \alpha \in U \) iff \( \alpha \in V \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, the statement ``for all \( x \), \( x \in U \) iff \( x \in V \)'' is true by
    \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
    Therefore, \( U = V \) by \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}.
\end{proof}

\begin{notation}
    For any family of sets \( S \), we write \( \Union S \) to denote the union of \( S \). 
    If \( S \) is the unordered pair \( \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \), then we also use the notation
    \( A \union B \) to denote \( \Union S \).
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Union]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}
    For every family of sets \( S \), the \keyword{union} of \( S \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \Union S = \setbuilderBig{x}{\text{there exists an \( E \in S \) such that \( x \in E \)}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-power-set}
    For any set \( S \), the power set of \( S \) is unique.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( P \) and \( P^{\prime} \) be the power sets of \( S \).
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in P \). By \cref{meta-axiom:power-set}, it follows that \( \alpha \subseteq S \).
    Since \( P^{\prime} \) is also a power set of \( S \), \( \alpha \in P^{\prime} \) must also be true by
    \cref{meta-axiom:power-set}. The converse can also be established by a similar line of reasoning.
    Thus, \( \alpha \in P \) iff \( \alpha \in P^{\prime} \).
    Since \( \alpha \) denotes an arbitrary object in the universe, \( P = P^{\prime} \) by
    \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} and \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}.
\end{proof}

\begin{notation}
    For any given set \( S \), the unique power set of \( S \) will be denoted as \( \powerset{S} \).
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Power Set]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-power-set}
    For any set \( S \), the \keyword{power set} of \( S \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \powerset{S} = \setbuilder{x}{x \subseteq S}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-intersection-of-a-family-of-sets}
    For any non-empty family of sets \( S \), there exists a unique set whose elements are exactly those objects that belong to all elements of \( S \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty family of sets.
    Now consider the predicate
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \ \text{for all \( y \), \( y \in S \) implies \( x \in y \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( S \) is a non-empty family of sets, there must exists a set \( E \) such that \( E \in S \).
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement; that is,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( y \), \( y \in S \) implies \( \alpha \in y \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( E \) denotes an object in the universe, it follows immediately from \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} that \( E \in S \) implies \( \alpha \in E \).
    But \( E \in S \) is true, so \( \alpha \in E \) must also be true by \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens}.
    Therefore, we can validly conclude that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) implies \( \alpha \in E \) where \( E \) is an existing set.
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set}, the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists and is unique.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Intersection]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-the-intersection-of-a-non-empty-family-of-sets}
    For every non-empty family of sets \( S \), the \keyword{intersection} of \( S \) is the unique set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \Intersect S = \setbuilderBig{x}{\text{for all \( y \), \( y \in S \) implies \( x \in y \)}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    For any given non-empty system of sets \( S \), we write \( \Intersect S \) to denote the unique set whose elements are exactly those objects that belong to all
    elements of \( S \).
    If \( S \) is the unordered pair \( \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) where \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, then we also write \( A \intersect B \) to denote
    \( \Intersect S \).
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Disjoint]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-disjoint-sets}
    Two sets \( A \) and \( B \) are \keyword{disjoint} iff \( A \intersect B = \emptyset \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Pairwise Disjoint]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-pairwise-disjoint-sets}
    If \( S \) is a family of sets, then \( S \) is \keyword{pairwise disjoint} iff any two \emph{distinct} elements of \( S \) are disjoint.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Difference]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-the-difference-of-two-sets}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), the \keyword{set difference} between \( A \) and \( B \) (also called the \keyword{relative complement} of \( B \) in \( A \))
    is the set given by:
    \begin{equation*}
        A - B = \setbuilder{x \in A}{\text{\(x \notin B\)}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that the existence of the relative complement follows immediately from \cref{meta-axiom:specification}.
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-no-set-is-an-element-of-itself}
    For any set \( A \), \( A \notin A \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( A \) is a set, the \aref{dfn:metatheory-singleton}{singleton} \( \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) exists by \cref{meta-axiom:pairing}.
    Clearly, \( \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) is a non-empty family of sets.
    Thus, it follows from \cref{meta-axiom:regularity} that there exists a set \( S \in \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \notin S \) or \( x \notin \mleft\{ A \mright\} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( S \in \mleft\{ A \mright\} \), it follows that \( S = A \).
    By \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation}, we conclude that either \( A \notin A \) or \( A \notin \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) is true.
    The statement \( A \notin \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) is clearly false. Therefore, \( A \notin A \) by \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-asymmetry-of-membership-relation}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), if \( A \in B \), then \( B \notin A \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( A \) and \( B \) be two arbitrary sets such that \( A \in B \).
    Since \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, the unordered pair \( \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) exists and is a non-empty
    \aref{dfn:metatheory-family-of-sets}{family of sets}.
    Therefore, \cref{meta-axiom:regularity} applies and there must exist a set \( S \in \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) such that
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:x-not-in-S-or-x-not-in-unordered-pair}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \notin S \) or \( x \notin \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \)}
    \end{equation}
    Applying \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to \eqref{eq:x-not-in-S-or-x-not-in-unordered-pair} then yields
    \begin{equation*}
        A \notin S \ \text{or} \ A \notin \mleft\{ A, B \mright\}
    \end{equation*}
    But \( A \notin \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) cannot be true by \cref{dfn:metatheory-unordered-pair}.
    Therefore, \( A \notin S \) must be true by \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}.
    Since \( S \notin \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-unordered-pair} once again that
    \( S = A \) or \( S = B \).
    But \( S = B \) cannot possibly be true because it contradicts our initial assumption that \( A \notin B \)---i.e., since
    \( A \notin S \), then \( S = B \) must imply \( A \notin B \), a contradiction.
    Therefore, necessarily \( S = A \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Now apply \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to \eqref{eq:x-not-in-S-or-x-not-in-unordered-pair} once again, but using \( B \) this time.
    That is,
    \begin{equation*}
        B \notin S \ \text{or} \ B \notin \mleft\{ A, B \mright\}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( B \notin \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) is impossible, we infer that \( B \notin S \).
    Suppose that \( B \in A \) were true. Then \( B \in S \) must also be true by \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality} because \( S = A \) as previously shown.
    But it has already been established that \( B \notin S \), so our supposition that \( B \in A \) must be false.
    Thus, \( B \notin A \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-any-member-of-a-family-of-sets-is-a-subset-of-the-union-of-the-family-of-sets}
    If \( S \) is a family of sets and \( E \in S \), then \( E \subseteq \Union S \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty family of sets and \( E \) be any element of \( S \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object in the univese.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in E \).
    Since \( E \in S \) and \( \alpha \in E \), it follows from \cref{meta-rule:existential-generalization} that
    there exists an \( x \in S \) such that \( \alpha \in x \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, it follows that \( \alpha \in \Union S \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in E \) implies \( x \in \Union S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, we thus have \( E \subseteq \Union S \).
\end{proof}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:metatheory-A-and-B-are-both-subsets-of-A-union-B}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), \( A \subseteq A \union B \) and \( B \subseteq A \union B \).
\end{cor}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-union-of-a-set-and-its-subset-is-itself}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), if \( A \subseteq B \), then \( A \union B = B \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( A \) and \( B \) be any two sets such that \( A \subseteq B \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in A \union B \); that is, \( \alpha \in A \) or \( \alpha \in B \).
    But if \( \alpha \in A \), then \( \alpha \in B \) must also be true since \( A \subseteq B \).
    In other words, \( \alpha \in B \) is true in all possible cases.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \alpha \in A \union B \ \text{implies} \ \alpha \in B
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \( x \in A \union B \) implies \( x \in B \) for all \( x \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( A \union B \subseteq B \).
    We know from \cref{cor:metatheory-A-and-B-are-both-subsets-of-A-union-B} that \( B \subseteq A \union B \).
    This means that \( A \union B \) and \( B \) are subsets of each other.
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}, \( A \union B = B \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-intersection-of-two-sets-using-set-builder-notation}
    If \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, then \( A \intersect B = \setbuilder{x}{x \in A \ \text{and} \ x \in B} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \varphi(x) \) be the predicate defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq x \in A \ \text{and} \ x \in B
    \end{equation*}
    Clearly, \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in A \) for all \( x \).
    Thus, the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists by \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set}.
    Let \( S = \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \).
    The goal is to prove that \( A \intersect B = S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( x^{\prime} \in S \).
    Therefore, \( \varphi(x^{\prime}) \) is a true statement; i.e., \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( x^{\prime} \in B \) are both true.
    Let \( Z \) be an arbitrary element of \( \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-unordered-pair}, either \( Z = A \) or \( Z = B \).
    If \( Z = A \), then necessarily \( x^{\prime} \in Z \) because \( x^{\prime} \in A \) is true.
    Similarly, if \( Z = B \), then necessarily \( x^{\prime} \in Z \) because \( x^{\prime} \in B \) is true.
    Clearly, \( x^{\prime} \in Z \) is implied in both cases.
    Therefore, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:disjunction-elimination} that \( x^{\prime} \in Z \) must be true.
    Since \( Z \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( X \in \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \), \( x^{\prime} \in X \)}
    \end{equation*}
    It follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-intersection-of-indexed-family-of-sets} that \( x^{\prime} \in \Intersect \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) and
    thus \( x^{\prime} \in A \intersect B \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        x^{\prime} \in S \ \text{implies} \ x^{\prime} \in A \intersect B
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( x^{\prime} \), is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in S \) implies \( x  \in A \intersect B \)}
    \end{equation*}
    That is, \( S \subseteq A \intersect B \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, let \( y^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( y^{\prime} \in A \intersect B \); i.e., \( y^{\prime} \in \Intersect \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-intersection-of-indexed-family-of-sets}, it follows that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:y-prime-is-an-element-of-all-elements-of-unordered-pair-A-B}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) implies \( y^{\prime} \in x \) }
    \end{equation}
    Clearly, \( A \) is an object in the universe.
    Applying \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to \eqref{eq:y-prime-is-an-element-of-all-elements-of-unordered-pair-A-B} yields
    \begin{equation*}
        A \in \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \ \text{implies} \ y^{\prime} \in A
    \end{equation*}
    But \( A \in \mleft\{ A, B \mright\} \) is true, so \( y^{\prime} \in A \) must also be true by \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens}.
    A similar argument can be used to show that \( y^{\prime} \in B \).
    Since \( y^{\prime} \in A \) and \( y^{\prime} \in B \) are both true, it follows that \( \varphi(y^{\prime}) \) is a true statement
    and thus \( y^{\prime} \in S \).
    In other words, \( A \intersect B \subseteq S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( A \intersect B \) and \( S \) are subsets of each other, they must be equal sets by \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-intersection-between-a-set-and-its-subset-equals-the-subset}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), if \( A \subseteq B \), then \( A \,{\intersect}\, B = A \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( A \) and \( B \) be any two sets such that \( A \subseteq B \).
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( x^{\prime} \in A \,{\intersect}\, B \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-intersection-of-two-sets-using-set-builder-notation}, it follows that \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( x^{\prime} \in B \)
    are both true statements. Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        x^{\prime} \in A \intersect B \ \text{implies} \ x^{\prime} \in A
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( A \,{\intersect}\, B \subseteq A \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, suppose that \( x^{\prime} \in A \). Since \( A \subseteq B \), \( x^{\prime} \) must also be an element of \( B \).
    Thus, \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( x^{\prime} \in B \) are both true.
    In other words, \( x^{\prime} \in A \,{\intersect}\, B \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-intersection-of-two-sets-using-set-builder-notation}.
    \begin{equation*}
        x^{\prime} \in A \ \text{implies} \ x^{\prime} \in A \intersect B
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} once again
    that \( A \subseteq A \,{\intersect}\, B \). Since \( A \,{\intersect}\, B \) and \( A \) are subsets of each other, they are necessarily equal.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Functions}
We now define the notion of functions and then derive many of their familiar properties within our metatheory.

\begin{dfn}[Ordered Pair]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-ordered-pair-kuratowski}
    Let \( a \) and \( b \) be any two objects in the universe.
    The \keyword{ordered pair} \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \) is defined as the set
    \( \mleft\{ \mleft\{ a \mright\}, \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \mright\} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-equality-of-ordered-pairs}
    For any two ordered pairs \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \) and \( \orderedpair{c}{d} \),
    \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{c}{d} \) iff \( a = c \) and \( b = d \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose that \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{c}{d} \).
    By \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}, it follows that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:a-b-pair-equals-c-d-pair}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \) iff \( x \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \)}
    \end{equation}
    Clearly, both \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} \) and \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \) are elements of \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \) according
    to \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordered-pair-kuratowski}.
    There are only two cases to consider.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case:} \( a = b \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    In this case, clearly \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \mleft\{ \mleft\{ a \mright\} \mright\} = \mleft\{ \mleft\{ b \mright\} \mright\} \) because
    \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} = \mleft\{ a, a \mright\} = \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} = \mleft\{ b, b \mright\} = \mleft\{ b \mright\}  \).
    By \eqref{eq:a-b-pair-equals-c-d-pair}, it follows that \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \).
    By \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}, necessarily \( \mleft\{ c \mright\} = \mleft\{ a \mright\} \) and \( \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} = \mleft\{ a \mright\} \).
    From this, we draw two conclusions:
    \begin{enumerate*}[label=(\arabic*)]
        \item \( a = c \),
        \item \( a = b = d\).
    \end{enumerate*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case:} \( a \neq b \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Once again, \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \), which implies \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \) by \eqref{eq:a-b-pair-equals-c-d-pair}.
    This means that either \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} = \mleft\{ c \mright\} \) or \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} = \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \) is true.
    Suppose that \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} = \mleft\{ c \mright\} \) is true. Then necessarily \( a = b = c \) by \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}.
    But this contradicts the fact that \( a \neq b \); therefore, \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} = \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \) must be true by
    \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}.
    From this, we further deduce that \( c \neq d \); otherwise, \( a = b \), a contradiction.
    But \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} \) is also an element of \( \orderedpair{c}{d} \), so either \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} = \mleft\{ c \mright\} \) or
    \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} = \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \) is true. Note that \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} = \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \) is impossible;
    otherwise, \( c = a \) and \( d = a \) are both true, which contradicts the fact that \( c \neq d \).
    Since \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} \neq \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \), the other remaining possibility \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} = \mleft\{ c \mright\} \) must be true.
    Therefore, \( a = c \).
    Note that either \( b = c \) or \( b = d \) is true because \( b \in \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \) and \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} = \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \).
    Since \( a = c \) and \( a \neq b \), necessarily \( b \neq c \) and thus \( b = d \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Thus, \( a = c \) and \( b = d \) are both true in all possible cases. In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{c}{d} \), then \( a = c \) and \( b = d \) are both true}
    \end{equation*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    To prove the converse, suppose that \( a = c \) and \( b = d \) are both true.
    Let \( e \) be an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( e \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \); that is, either \( e = \mleft\{ a \mright\} \) or \( e = \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \).
    If \( e = \mleft\{ a \mright\} \), then clearly \( e = \mleft\{ c \mright\} \) and thus \( e \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordered-pair-kuratowski}.
    If \( e = \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \), then \( e \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \) is also true because \( a = c \), \( b = d \),
    and \( \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \).
    Thus, \( e \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \) implies \( e \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \).
    Conversely, suppose that \( e \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \); that is, either \( e = \mleft\{ c \mright\} \) or \( e = \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \).
    If \( e = \mleft\{ c \mright\} \), then clearly \( e \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \) because \( a = c \) and \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \).
    Similarly, if \( e = \mleft\{ c, d \mright\} \), then \( e \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \) is also true because \( a = c \), \( b = d \),
    and \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \).
    Clearly, \( e \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \) and \( e \in \orderedpair{c}{d} \) imply each other.
    Since \( e \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} and \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality} that
    \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{c}{d} \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( a = c \) and \( b = d \) are both true, then \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{c}{d} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Having established the implication in both directions, we conclude that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{\( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{c}{d} \) iff \( a = c \) and \( b = d \)}
        \qedhere
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-ordered-pair-must-belong-to-power-set-of-power-set-of-a-set}
    If \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \) is an ordered pair such that \( a, b \in S \) for some existing set \( S \),
    then \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in \powerset{\powerset{S}} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Clearly, \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} \) and \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \) are subsets of \( S \) since \( a, b \in S\).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-power-set}, it follows that \( \mleft\{ a \mright\} \in \powerset{S} \) and \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \in \powerset{S} \).
    But this means that \( \mleft\{ \mleft\{ a \mright\}, \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \mright\} \subseteq \powerset{S} \) and thus
    \( \mleft\{ \mleft\{ a \mright\}, \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \mright\} \in \powerset{\powerset{S}} \) as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-components-of-ordered-pair-must-belong-to-union-of-union-of-a-family-of-set}
    If \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in S \) where \( S \) and \( \Union S \) are families of sets, then \( a \in \Union \mleft( \Union S \mright) \)
    and \( b \in \Union \mleft( \Union S \mright) \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We begin our line of reasoning with the assumption that \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in S \).
    But \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \in \orderedpair{a}{b} \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordered-pair-kuratowski},
    which means that there exist elements of \( S \) containing \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \) as an element.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, it follows that \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \in \Union S \).
    But \( a \) and \( b \) are both elements of \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-unordered-pair}.
    Since \( \Union S \) is a family of sets and \( \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \) is a set, it follows that there exists a set in \( \Union S \)
    containing both \( a \) and \( b \) are elements. In other words, \( a, b \in \Union \mleft( \Union S \mright) \) as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-cartesian-products}
    Let \( A \) and \( B \) be any two sets.
    Let the following predicates be defined:
    \begin{align*}
        \phi(a, b)    &\coloneq \text{\( a \in A \) and \( b \in B \)}\\
        \psi(x, a)    &\coloneq \text{there exists a \( b \) such that \( x = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) and \( \phi(a, b) \)}\\
        \varphi(x)    &\coloneq \text{there exists an \( a \) such that \( \psi(x, a) \)}
    \end{align*}
    Then the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \varphi(p) \) is a true statement;
    that is, \( \psi(p, a) \) is a true statement for some \( a \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \ \text{and} \ \phi(a, b) \ \text{for some \( b \)}
    \end{equation*}
    But for \( \phi(a, b) \) to be a true statement, \( a \in A \) and \( b \in B \) must both be true.
    Thus, \( a \in A \union B \) and \( b \in A \union B \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-ordered-pair-must-belong-to-power-set-of-power-set-of-a-set}, it follows that
    \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in \powerset{\powerset{A \union B}} \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(p) \ \text{implies} \ p \in \powerset{\powerset{A \union B}}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( p \) denotes an arbitrary object, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \powerset{\powerset{A \union B}} \) }
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set},
    \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists.
\end{proof}

\begin{notation}
    Oftentimes, the standard set-builder notation becomes too clunky and unwieldy
    as seen in \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-cartesian-products} where the predicate \( \varphi(x) \)
    is complex.
    In such cases, we adopt a more compact form of set-builder notation for readability.
    The idea is to replace the placeholder on the left side of the ``\( \vert \)'' symbol with an \aref{dfn:metatheory-expressions}{expression}
    constructed from a placeholder or multiple placeholders.
    For instance, the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) in \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-cartesian-products} can be denoted as
    \begin{equation*}
        \setbuilderbig{\orderedpair{a}{b}}{a \in A \ \text{and} \ b \in B}
    \end{equation*}
    where the expression ``\( \orderedpair{a}{b} \)'' refers to the set \( \mleft\{ \mleft\{ a \mright\}, \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \mright\} \).
    In general, the number of placeholders in the expression on the left of the ``\( \vert \)'' symbol corresponds to the number of placeholders
    in the predicate on the right.
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Expressional Set-builder]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-expressional-set-builder-notation}
    Let \( \varepsilon(x) \) be an expression and \( \varphi(x) \) be a predicate.
    Then \( \setbuilder{\varepsilon(x)}{\varphi(x)} \) denotes the set
    \( \setbuilder{y}{\text{there exists an \( x \) such that \( y = \varepsilon(x) \) and \( \varphi(x) \)}} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    The expression used in expressional set-builder notation can have as many placeholders as required.
    The expression used to denote the set in \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-cartesian-products} has two placeholders;
    i.e., \( \varepsilon(a, b) \coloneq \orderedpair{a}{b} \) and \(  \setbuilderbig{\varepsilon(a, b)}{a \in A \ \text{and} \ b \in B} \) denotes
    \begin{equation*}
        \setbuilderbig{x}{\text{there exist objects \( a \) and \( b \) such that \( x = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) and \( a \in A \) and \( b \in B \)}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}[Cartesian Product]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), the \keyword{cartesian product} of \( A \) and \( B \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        A \times B = \setbuilderbig{\orderedpair{a}{b}}{a \in A \ \text{and} \ b \in B}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-ordered-pairs-satisfying-binary-predicates}
    Let \( \varphi(x, y) \) be any predicate with two placeholders \( x \) and \( y \).
    If \( A \) and \( B \) are sets and \( \varphi(x, y) \) implies \( x \in A \) and \( y \in B \) for all \( x \) and \( y \),
    then the set \( \setbuilder{\orderedpair{x}{y}}{\varphi(x, y)} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We begin by defining the following predicate:
    \begin{equation*}
        \psi(x) \coloneq \text{there exist objects \( a \) and \( b \) such that \( x = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) and \( \varphi(a, b) \) is satisfied}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \psi(\alpha) \) is a true statement.
    Thus, there exist objects \( a \) and \( b \) in the universe such that \( \alpha = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) and \( \varphi(a, b) \) is satisfied.
    Since \( \varphi(a, b) \) is satisfied, we have \( a \in A \) and \( b \in B \) by our initial assumption.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in A \times B \) and thus \( \alpha \in A \times B \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we apply \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} to conclude that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \psi(x) \) implies \( x \in A \times B \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set}, the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\psi(x)} \) exists.
    Using \aref{dfn:metatheory-expressional-set-builder-notation}{expressional set-builder notation}, we can then re-express
    \( \setbuilder{x}{\psi(x)} \) as \( \setbuilder{\orderedpair{x}{y}}{\varphi(x, y)} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-cartesian-product-is-empty-iff-at-least-one-of-the-sets-is-empty}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), \( A \times B = \emptyset \) iff \( A = \emptyset \) or \( B = \emptyset \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( A \) and \( B \) denote two arbitrary sets.
    Suppose that \( A \times B = \emptyset \).
    Now suppose that the statement
    \begin{equation*}
        A = \emptyset \ \text{or} \ B = \emptyset
    \end{equation*}
    is false. By \cref{meta-rule:de-morgans-laws}, it follows that \( A \neq \emptyset \) and \( B \neq \emptyset \) are both true.
    Since neither \( A \) nor \( B \) is empty, there exist objects \( a \) and \( b \) such that \( a \in A \) and \( b \in B \).
    Let \( p \) be the ordered pair formed using \( a \) and \( b \).
    \begin{equation*}
        p = \orderedpair{a}{b}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, it follows that \( p \in A \times B \).
    Since \( A \times B \) contains an element, it cannot be empty.
    The supposition that ``\( A = \emptyset \) or \( B = \emptyset \)'' is false leads to a contradiction.
    Therefore, the statement ``\( A = \emptyset \) or \( B = \emptyset \)'' has to be true if ``\( A \times B = \emptyset \)'' is true.
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:metatheory-empty-cartesian-product-implies-at-least-one-empty-set}
        \text{if \( A \times B = \emptyset \), then \( A = \emptyset \) or \( B = \emptyset \)}
    \end{equation}
    To prove the converse, suppose that \( A = \emptyset \) or \( B = \emptyset \) is true.
    We consider each case separately.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case:} \( A = \emptyset \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose that \( A \times B \) is not empty; that is, \( A \times B \) has an element.
    Let \( p \) denote said element.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, there exist objects \( a \) and \( b \) such that
    \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \), \( a \in A \), \( b \in B \).
    But \( a \in A \) contradicts the fact that \( A = \emptyset \).
    By \cref{meta-rule:reductio-ad-absurdum}, the supposition ``\( A \times B \neq \emptyset \)'' must be false.
    Thus, \( A \times B = \emptyset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case:} \( B = \emptyset \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that \( A \times B \) is not empty.
    Let \( q = \orderedpair{c}{d} \) denote an element of \( A \times B \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, necessarily \( c \in A \) and \( d \in B \), which contradicts \( B = \emptyset \).
    By \cref{meta-rule:reductio-ad-absurdum}, \( A \times B = \emptyset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( A \times B = \emptyset \) follows in both cases, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:metatheory-empty-set-implies-empty-cartesian-product}
        \text{if \( A = \emptyset \) or \( B = \emptyset \), then \( A \times B = \emptyset \)}
    \end{equation}

    \noindent
    From \eqref{eq:metatheory-empty-cartesian-product-implies-at-least-one-empty-set} and \eqref{eq:metatheory-empty-set-implies-empty-cartesian-product},
    it is clear that \( A \times B = \emptyset \) and \( A = \emptyset \) or \( B = \emptyset \) imply each other.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-subsets-form-cartesian-product-subsets}
    If \( S \) is a non-empty set, then the following statement is true for any two sets \( A \) and \( B \):
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{\( A \subseteq B \) iff \( A \times S \subseteq B \times S \)}
    \end{equation*}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose that \( A \subseteq B \) is true.
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    If \( p \in A \times S \), then \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) for some \( a \in A \) and \( b \in S \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}.
    Since \( A \subseteq B \), \( a \in A \) must imply \( a \in B \).
    This means that \( p \in B \times S \) is true.
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in A \times S \) implies \( B \times S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( A \times S \subseteq B \times S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, suppose that \( A \times S \subseteq B \times S \) is true.
    Since \( S \neq \emptyset \), it must contain at least one element, which we will denote as \( s \).
    Now let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in A \).
    Since \( \alpha \) and \( s \) are objects, the sets \( \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \) and \( \mleft\{ \alpha, s \mright\} \) exists
    by \cref{meta-axiom:pairing}.
    Since sets are themselves objects, we invoke \cref{meta-axiom:pairing} once again to conclude that the set
    \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{s} = \mleft\{ \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\}, \mleft\{ \alpha, s \mright\}  \mright\} \) exists.
    Since \( \alpha \in A \) and \( s \in S \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product} that
    \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{s} \in A \times S \).
    But \( A \times S \) is a subset of \( B \times S \), so \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{s} \) must also be an element of \( B \times S \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, necessarily \( \alpha \in B \).
    In other words, \( \alpha \in A \) implies \( \alpha \in B \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in A \) implies \( x \in B \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( A \subseteq B \).
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Function]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-function}
    A set \( f \) is a function iff the following statements are true:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item for all \( x \), \( x \in f \) implies \( x = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) for some \( a \) and \( b \)
        \item for all \( x \), \( y \), and \( z \), \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{x}{z} \in f \) implies \( y = z \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-a-set-of-ordered-pairs-and-its-union-are-families-of-sets}
    If \( S \) is a set of ordered pairs, then \( S \) and \( \Union S \) are families of sets.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object. Suppose that \( p \in S \).
    Thus, \( p \) must be an ordered pair by assumption; that is, \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \)
    for some \( a \) and \( b \) where \( a \) and \( b \) denote objects in the universe.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordered-pair-kuratowski}, \( p \) is the set \(  \mleft\{ \mleft\{ a \mright\}, \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \mright\} \).
    Clearly, \( p \) must be a set.
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in S \) implies \( x \) is a set}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( S \) is a family of sets.
    By \cref{meta-axiom:union}, \( \Union S \) exists.
    We must show that \( \Union S \) is also a family of sets.
    Let \( q \) be an arbitrary element of \( \Union S \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, \( q \in p \) for some \( p \in S \).
    Since \( S \) is a set of ordered pairs, necessarily \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) for some \( a \) and \( b \) where \( a \) and \( b \)
    denote objects in the universe.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordered-pair-kuratowski}, either \( q = \mleft\{ a \mright\} \) or \( q = \mleft\{ a, b \mright\} \).
    In either case, \( q \) is a set. Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \Union S \) implies \( x \) is a set}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( \Union S \) is indeed a family of sets.
\end{proof}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:metatheory-every-function-and-its-union-are-families-of-sets}
    If \( f \) is a function, then \( f \) and \( \Union f \) are families of sets since \( f \) is a set of ordered pairs.
\end{cor}

\begin{dfn}[Composition]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-composition-of-two-sets-of-ordered-pairs}
    If \( R \) and \( S \) are sets of ordered pairs, then the \keyword{composition} of \( R \) and \( S \)
    is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \composition{S}{R} \coloneq \setbuilderbig{\orderedpair{x}{y}}{\text{there exists a \( z \) such that \( \orderedpair{x}{z} \in R \) and \( \orderedpair{z}{y} \in S \) }}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-composition-of-two-sets-of-ordered-pairs}
    If \( R \) and \( S \) are sets of ordered pairs, then \( \composition{S}{R} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We begin by defining the predicate \( \varphi(x) \) as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \text{there exists an \( a \) and \( b \) such that \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in R \) and \( \orderedpair{c}{b} \in S \) for some \( c \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Note that the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) is simply \( \composition{S}{R} \) written in standard set-builder notation.
    Thus, it suffices to prove the existence of \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) on the basis of \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement.
    Thus, \( \alpha = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) such that
    \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in R \) and \( \orderedpair{c}{b} \in S \) for some \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-components-of-ordered-pair-must-belong-to-union-of-union-of-a-family-of-set},
    necessarily \( a \in \Union \mleft( \Union R \mright) \) and \( b \in \Union \mleft( \Union S \mright) \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, \( \alpha \in \Union \mleft( \Union R \mright) \times \Union \mleft( \Union S \mright) \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \Union \mleft( \Union R \mright) \times \Union \mleft( \Union S \mright) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore, the set \( \composition{S}{R} = \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists by \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-composition-of-functions-is-a-function}
    If \( f \) and \( g \) are functions, then \( \composition{g}{f} \) is also a function.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We must prove that the set \( \composition{g}{f} \) satisfies the two criteria stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.
    It should be clear from \cref{dfn:metatheory-composition-of-two-sets-of-ordered-pairs} that \( \composition{g}{f} \) is a
    set of ordered pairs. It remains to prove that \( \composition{g}{f} \) satisfies the second criterion stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \), \( y^{\prime} \), and \( y^{\prime\prime} \) denote arbitrary objects in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \composition{g}{f} \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \in \composition{g}{f} \)
    are both true statements.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-composition-of-two-sets-of-ordered-pairs}, it follows that
    \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{z^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) for some \( z^{\prime} \)
    and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime\prime}} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{z^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \in g \) for some \( z^{\prime\prime} \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, the fact that both \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime\prime}} \) belongs to
    \( f \) implies that \( z^{\prime} = z^{\prime\prime} \).
    But if \( z^{\prime} \) and \( z^{\prime\prime} \) are equal, then \( y^{\prime} \) and \( y^{\prime\prime} \) must also be equal since \( g \) is a function.
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{\( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \composition{g}{f} \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \in \composition{g}{f} \) implies
        \( y^{\prime} = y^{\prime\prime} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, \( \composition{g}{f} \) must be a function.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Domain]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}
    If \( f \) is a function, then the \keyword{domain} of \( f \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \dom f = \setbuilderbig{x}{\text{there exists a \( y \) such that \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f \)}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Range]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}
    If \( f \) is a function, then the \keyword{range} of \( f \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \ran f = \setbuilderbig{y}{\text{there exists an \( x \) such that \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f \)}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-domain-and-range-of-function}
    The domain and range of a function exist.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( f \) be an arbitrary function. Let \( \varphi(x) \) and \( \psi(y) \) denote the following predicates:
    \begin{align*}
        \varphi(x) &\coloneq \text{there exists a \( y \) such that \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f \)}\\
        \psi(y)    &\coloneq \text{there exists a \( z \) such that \( \orderedpair{z}{y} \in f \) }
    \end{align*}
    Now let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object such that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement; that is, there exists some \( \beta \) in the universe
    such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, \( f \) and \( \Union f \) are both families of sets by \cref{cor:metatheory-every-function-and-its-union-are-families-of-sets}.
    Thus, \cref{thm:metatheory-components-of-ordered-pair-must-belong-to-union-of-union-of-a-family-of-set} applies and we have
    \( \alpha \in \Union \mleft( \Union f \mright) \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:varphi-x-implies-union-union-of-f}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \Union \mleft( \Union f \mright) \)}
    \end{equation}
    Similarly, let \( \theta \) denote an arbitrary object such that \( \psi(\theta) \) is a true statement.
    Thus, there exists some \( \delta \) in the universe such that \( \orderedpair{\delta}{\theta} \in f \).
