    Again, \cref{thm:metatheory-components-of-ordered-pair-must-belong-to-union-of-union-of-a-family-of-set} guarantees that
    \( \theta \in \Union \mleft( \Union f \mright) \).
    By \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:psi-x-implies-union-union-of-f}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \psi(x) \) implies \( x \in \Union \mleft( \Union f \mright) \)}
    \end{equation}
    We can now apply \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set} to \eqref{eq:varphi-x-implies-union-union-of-f} and
    \eqref{eq:psi-x-implies-union-union-of-f} to infer the existences of
    \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) and \( \setbuilder{x}{\psi(x)} \), which are \( \dom f \) and \( \ran f \) respectively.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-function-with-empty-range-must-have-empty-domain}
    If \( f \) is a function and \( \ran f = \emptyset \), then \( \dom f = \emptyset \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in \dom f \).
    Then by \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \) for some \( \beta \) in the universe.
    But this would mean that \( \beta \in \ran f \), contradicting the fact that \( \ran f = \emptyset \).
    Therefore, the supposition that \( \alpha \in \dom f \) must be false.
    In other words, \( \alpha \notin \dom f \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \notin \dom f \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-empty-set}, it follows that \( \dom f = \emptyset \).
\end{proof}

\begin{rem}
    In fact, if \( \dom f = \emptyset \) or \( \ran f = \emptyset \), then \( f \) itself is necessarily the empty set.
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-function-equality}
    If \( f \) and \( g \) are functions, then \( f = g \) iff the following statements are true:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \dom f = \dom g \)
        \item for all \( x \in \dom f \), \( f(x) = g(x) \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( f \) and \( g \) be functions with the aforementioned properties.
    The goal is to prove that \( f \) and \( g \) are subsets of each other.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object such that \( p \in f \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, \( p \) must be an ordered pair.
    Let \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) where \( a \) and \( b \) are objects in the universe.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, \( a \in \dom f \).
    Since \( \dom f \) and \( \dom g \) are equal, this means that \( a \in \dom g \) is also true.
    Once again, we infer from \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function} that there exists some \( b^{\prime} \)
    such that \( \orderedpair{a}{b^{\prime}} \in g \); i.e., \( g(a) = b^{\prime} \).
    But \( f(a) = b = g(a) = b^{\prime} \), which means that \( b = b^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-of-ordered-pairs}, it follows that \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{a}{b^{\prime}} \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{a}{b^{\prime}} \in g \), it follows that \( p \) must also belong to \( g \).
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in f \) implies \( x \in g \)}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( f \subseteq g \). The converse relation \( g \subseteq f \) can be proven with a similar argument with the roles of \( f \) and \( g \)
    swapped.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-functions-in-terms-of-two-sets}
    If \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, then a set \( f \) is a \keyword{function} on \( A \) into \( B \) iff the following statements are true:
    \begin{enumerate*}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item \( f \) is a function,
        \item \( \dom f = A \),
        \item \( \ran f \subseteq B \).
    \end{enumerate*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We write \( \function{f}{A}{B} \) to abbreviate the statement ``\( f \) is a function on \( A \) into \( B \).''
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metateory-value-of-a-function}
    Let \( f \) be a function and \( x \) be an object in the universe such that \( x \in \dom f \).
    Then an object \( y \) is the \keyword{value} of \( f \) at \( x \) iff \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We write \( f(x) \) to denote the value of a function \( f \) at \( x \).
    The expression ``\( f(x) \)'' can be read as ``\( f \) of \( x \).''
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Compatibility]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-two-functions}
    If \( f \) and \( g \) are functions, then \( f \) and \( g \) are \keyword{compatible} iff for all \( x \in \dom f \intersect \dom g \),
    \( f(x) = g(x) \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Image]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}
    If \( f \) is a function and \( A \) is a set, then the \keyword{image} of \( A \) under \( f \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \imageSet{f}{A} \coloneq \setbuilderbig{f(x)}{x \in A}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that the \aref{dfn:metatheory-expressional-set-builder-notation}{expressional set-builder notation}
    was used in \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}.
    It is also possible to denote \( \imageSet{f}{A} \) using the standard set-builder notation if desired.
    \begin{equation*}
        \imageSet{f}{A} \coloneq \setbuilderbig{y}{\text{there exists an \( x \) such that \( x \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f \) }}
    \end{equation*}
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-function-is-subset-of-cartesian-product}
    If \( \function{f}{A}{B} \), then \( f \subseteq A \times B \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( p \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( p \in f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}, it follows that \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) where \( a \) and \( b \) are objects in the universe.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, it follows that \( a \in \dom f \) and thus \( a \in A \) because \( \dom f = A \)
    by \cref{dfn:metatheory-functions-in-terms-of-two-sets}.
    Additionally, we have \( b \in \ran f \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}.
    Since \( \ran f \subseteq B \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-functions-in-terms-of-two-sets}, it follows that \( b \in B \).
    In other words, \( p \in A \times B \).
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} permits us to conclude that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in f \) implies \( x \in A \times B \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, we conclude \( f \subseteq A \times B \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function-subset-of-the-range-of-the-function}
    If \( f \) is a function and \( A \) is a set, then \( \imageSet{f}{A} \subseteq \ran f \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \beta \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \beta \in \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    Thus, there must exists an \( \alpha \in A \) such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, necessarily \( \beta \in \ran f \).
    In other words, \( \beta \in \imageSet{f}{A} \) implies \( \beta \in \ran f \).
    Since \( \beta \) is arbitrary, we have \( \imageSet{f}{A} \subseteq \ran f \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-image-of-the-domain-of-a-function-under-the-function-equals-the-range-of-the-function}
    If \( f \) is a function, then \( \imageSet{f}{\dom f} = \ran f \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \beta \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \beta \in \imageSet{f}{\dom f} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, there exists
    an \( \alpha \in \dom f \) such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, necessarily \( \beta \in \ran f \).
    Conversely, suppose that \( \beta \in \ran f \).
    Then by \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, there exists a \( \gamma \) such that
    \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\beta} \in \ran f \).
    But by \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, \( \gamma \in \dom f \).
    This means that there exists a \( \gamma \in \dom f \) such that \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\beta} \in f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, \( \beta \in \imageSet{f}{\dom f} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We have shown that \( \beta \in \imageSet{f}{\dom f} \) and \( \beta \in \ran f \) imply each other for any arbitrary \( \beta \).
    By \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}, it follows that \( \imageSet{f}{\dom f} = \ran f \).
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Injection]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-injection}
    If \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, then a set \( f \) is an injection (or injective function) on \( A \) into \( B \) iff the following statements are true:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \function{f}{A}{B} \)
        \item for all \( x \in A \) and \( y \in A \), \( f(x) = f(y) \) implies \( x = y \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We write \( \injection{f}{A}{B} \) to abbreviate the statement ``\( f \) is an injection on \( A \) into \( B \).''
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Surjection]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-surjection}
    If \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, then a set \( f \) is a \keyword{surjection} or \keyword{surjective function} on \( A \) into \( B \) iff the following statements are true:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \function{f}{A}{B} \)
        \item \( \ran f = B \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We write \( \surjection{f}{A}{B} \) to abbreviate the statement ``\( f \) is a surjection on \( A \) into \( B \).''
\end{notation}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:metatheory-every-function-is-a-surjection-on-its-domain-into-its-range}
    For every function \( f \), \( \surjection{f}{\dom f}{\ran f} \).
\end{cor}

\begin{dfn}[Bijection]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-bijection}
    If \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, then a set \( f \) is a bijection between \( A \) and \( B \) iff
    \( \injection{f}{A}{B} \) and \( \surjection{f}{A}{B} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    We write \( \bijection{f}{A}{B} \) to abbreviate the statement ``\( f \) is a bijection between \( A \) and \( B \).''
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Family of Functions]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-family-of-functions}
    A set \( F \) is a \keyword{family of functions} iff for all \( f \in F \), \( f \) is a function.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Compatibility]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-functions}
    A family of functions \( F \) is compatible iff any two functions in \( F \)
    are \aref{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-two-functions}{compatible}.
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-a-family-of-functions-is-compatible-iff-its-union-is-a-function}
    A family of functions \( F \) is compatible iff \( \Union F \) is a function.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose that \( F \) is compatible.
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary element of \( \Union F \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, there exists a function \( f \in F \) such that \( p \in f \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-function} that \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \)
    for some \( a \) and \( b \) where \( a \) and \( b \) denote objects in the universe.
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:first-condition-for-a-set-to-be-a-function}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \Union F \) implies \( x = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) for some \( a \) and \( b \)}
    \end{equation}
    Now let \( \alpha \) and \( \beta \) be any two objects such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \Union F \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, there must exist some function \( f \in F \) such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    Suppose that \( \gamma \) is an object in the universe such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in \Union F \).
    Again, we infer from \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets} that there exists some function \( g \in F \) such that
    \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in g \). Clearly, either \( f = g \) or \( f \neq g \).
    If \( f = g \), then obviously \( \beta = \gamma \) because \( f \) is a function.
    If \( f \neq g \), then \( \beta = \gamma \) is also true because \( f \) and \( g \) are compatible and \( \alpha \in \dom f \intersect \dom g \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:second-condition-for-a-set-to-be-a-function}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( y \), and \( z \), \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in \Union F \) and \( \orderedpair{x}{z} \in \Union F \) implies \( y = z \)}
    \end{equation}
    From \eqref{eq:first-condition-for-a-set-to-be-a-function} and \eqref{eq:second-condition-for-a-set-to-be-a-function}, it is clear that
    the two conditions stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-function} are met. Therefore, \( \Union F \) is a function.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    To prove the converse, suppose that \( \Union F \) is a function.
    Now let \( f \) and \( g \) be arbitrary elements of \( F \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary element of \( \dom f \,{\intersect}\, \dom g \).
    Clearly, \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in g \) for some \( \beta \) and \( \gamma \)
    because \( \alpha \in \dom f \) and \( \alpha \in \dom g \).
    Since \( f \in F \) and \( g \in G \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets} that
    \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \Union F \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in \Union F \).
    But \( \Union F \) is a function, so necessarily \( f(\alpha) = \beta = \gamma = g(\alpha) \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \dom f \intersect \dom g \) implies \( f(x) = g(x) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-two-functions}, it follows that \( f \) and \( g \) are compatible.
    Again, given that \( f \) and \( g \) represent arbitrary elements of \( F \), we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( f \) and \( g \), \( f \in F \) and \( g \in F \) implies \( f \) and \( g \) are compatible}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-functions}, necessarily \( F \) is compatible.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-domain-of-a-compatible-family-of-functions-is-the-union-of-the-domains-of-each-function-in-the-family}
    If \( F \) is a compatible family of functions, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \dom \mleft( \Union F \mright) = \Union \setbuilderBig{\dom f}{f \in F}
    \end{equation*}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( F \) is a compatible family of functions, it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-a-family-of-functions-is-compatible-iff-its-union-is-a-function}
    that \( \Union F \) is a function.
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \dom \mleft( \Union F \mright) \).
    Thus, \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \Union F \) for some \( y^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-union-of-indexed-family-of-sets}, it follows that there exists some function
    \( f^{\prime} \in \ran F \) such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f^{\prime} \).
    Since \( f^{\prime} \) is a function, it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function} that
    \( x^{\prime} \in \dom f^{\prime} \).
    This means that if \( x^{\prime} \in \dom \mleft( \Union F \mright) \), then there exists an \( f^{\prime} \in F \) such that \( x^{\prime} \in \dom f^{\prime} \).
    In other words, \( \dom \mleft( \Union F \mright) \subseteq \Union \setbuilder{\dom f}{f \in F} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, let \( x^{\prime} \in \Union \setbuilder{\dom f}{f \in F} \).
    It follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets} that \( x^{\prime} \in Z \) for some \( Z \in \setbuilder{\dom f}{f \in F} \).
    Clearly, \( Z = \dom f^{\prime} \) for some \( f^{\prime} \in F \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} \in Z \) and \( Z = \dom f^{\prime} \), it follows that \( x^{\prime} \in \dom f^{\prime} \).
    Given that \( f^{\prime} \) is a function, it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function} that a
    unique \( y^{\prime} \) exists such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f^{\prime} \).
    Since \( F \) is a family of functions and \( f^{\prime} \in \ran F \), this means that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \Union F \).
    Therefore, \( x^{\prime} \in \dom \mleft( \Union F \mright) \).
    In other words, \( \Union \setbuilder{\dom f}{f \in F} \subseteq \dom \mleft( \Union F \mright)  \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( \dom \mleft( \Union F \mright) \) and \( \Union \setbuilder{\dom f}{f \in F} \) are subsets of each other, they must be equal by
    \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-a-subset-of-another-function-then-the-two-functions-are-compatible}
    For any two functions \( f \) and \( g \), if \( f \subseteq g \), then \( f \) and \( g \) are compatible.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( f \) and \( g \) be two arbitrary functions such that \( f \subseteq g \).
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \dom f \,{\intersect}\, \dom g \).
    Thus, there exist objects \( y^{\prime} \) and \( z^{\prime} \) such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \ \text{and} \ \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in g
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( f \subseteq g \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \), it follows that
    \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \).
    But \( g \) is a function, so if \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \)
    are both true, then necessarily \( f(x^{\prime}) = y^{\prime} = z^{\prime} = g(x^{\prime}) \).
    Given that \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \in \dom f \,{\intersect}\, \dom g \), \( f(x) = g(x) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-two-functions}, it follows that \( f \) and \( g \) are compatible. 
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Restriction]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}
    If \( f \) is a function and \( A \) is a set, then the \keyword{restriction} of \( f \) to \( A \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \restrictedfunction{f}{A} = \setbuilderbig{\orderedpair{x}{y}}{x \in A \ \text{and} \ \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-restriction-is-a-function}
    If \( f \) is a function and \( A \) is a set, then \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) is also a function.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We must prove that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) satisfies the two conditions stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.
    Let \( p \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe. Suppose that \( p \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}, there must exist an \( \alpha \in A \) and a \( \beta \) such that
    \( p = \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \). In other words, every element of \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) is an ordered pair.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( \alpha \), \( \beta \), and \( \gamma \) be objects such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \)
    and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}, it follows that \( \alpha \in A \) and that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \)
    and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in f\).
    But \( f \) is a function, so \( \beta \) and \( \gamma \) must be equal by \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunction{f}{A} \ \text{and} \ \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in \restrictedfunction{f}{A} \ \text{implies} \ \beta = \gamma
    \end{equation*}
    for all \( \alpha \), \( \beta \), and \( \gamma \), which is the second condition in \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-domain-of-restriction-of-f-to-A-is-the-intersection-between-dom-f-and-A}
    If \( f \) is a function and \( A \) is a set, then \( \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} = A \intersect \dom f \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( x^{\prime} \in \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, it follows that there exists some \( y^{\prime} \)
    such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \),
    then necessarily \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \), the latter of which
    implies \( x^{\prime} \in \dom f \).
    In other words, \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( x^{\prime} \in \dom f \) are both true
    and thus \( x^{\prime} \in A \,{\intersect}\, \dom f \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-intersection-of-two-sets-using-set-builder-notation}.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, x^{\prime} \in \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \ \text{implies} \ x^{\prime} \in A \intersect \dom f
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \subseteq A \,{\intersect}\, \dom f \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, suppose that \( x^{\prime} \in A \,{\intersect}\, \dom f \); that is, \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( x^{\prime} \in \dom f \)
    are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, there must exist some \( y^{\prime} \in \ran f \) such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    In other words, there exist objects \( x^{\prime} \) and \( y^{\prime} \) such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) and
    \( x^{\prime} \in A \).
    Thus, \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) and \( x^{\prime} \in \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude that \( A \,{\intersect}\, \dom f \subseteq \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) and \( A \,{\intersect}\, \dom f \) are subsets of each other, it follows from
    \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} that they are equal sets.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-range-of-a-restriction-is-the-image-of-the-function-under-the-restricted-domain}
    If \( f \) is a function and \( A \) is a set, then \( \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} = \imageSet{f}{A} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( f \) be an arbitrary function and \( A \) an arbitrary set in the universe.
    Let \( y^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( y^{\prime} \in \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, there exists an object \( x^{\prime}\) such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \), then necessarily
    \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}.
    It should be obvious from \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function} that \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        y^{\prime} \in \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \ \text{implies} \ y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( y^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \subseteq \imageSet{f}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, let \( y^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object in the universe such that \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, it follows that there exists an object \( x^{\prime} \) such that
    \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Immediately, we can infer \( y^{\prime} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    Thus, \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A} \) implies \( y^{\prime} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) for any arbitrary \( y^{\prime} \);
    i.e., \( \imageSet{f}{A} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    Since \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) and \( \imageSet{f}{A} \) are subsets of each other, they are necessarily equal sets.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-restriction-of-an-injection-is-also-an-injection}
    If \( f \) is an injective function and \( A \) is a set, then \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) is also an injection.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    It follows immediately from \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-is-a-function} that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) is a function.
    Let \( \alpha \), \( \beta \), and \( \gamma \) be objects in the universe such that
    \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) and \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\gamma} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \)
    are both true.
    It follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function} that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\gamma} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\gamma} \in f \)
    are both true.
    Since \( f \) is injective, it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-injection} that \( \alpha = \beta \).
    Therefore, \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) is also injective.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-image-of-a-proper-subset-under-an-injective-function-is-a-proper-subset-of-the-range}
    If \( f \) is a non-empty injective function and \( A \) is a set such that \( A \subset \dom f \), then \( \imageSet{f}{A} \subset \ran f \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    It follows immediately from \cref{thm:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function-subset-of-the-range-of-the-function} that \( \imageSet{f}{A} \subseteq \ran f \).
    It remains to prove that there exists a \( y \in \ran f \) such that \( y \notin \imageSet{f}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( A \subset \dom f \) and \( \dom f \neq \emptyset \), it follows that there exists an element of \( \dom f \) that does not belong to \( A \).
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) denote such an element; that is, \( x^{\prime} \in \dom f \) and \( x^{\prime} \notin A \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} \in \dom f \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function} that
    there exists some \( y^{\prime} \) such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Clearly, \( y^{\prime} \in \ran f \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Now let us suppose (for the sake of contradiction) that \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, it follows that there exists some \( x^{\prime\prime} \in A \) such that
    \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    But \( f \) is injective, which means that if both \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \)
    are true, then necessarily \( x^{\prime} = x^{\prime\prime} \).
    But if \( x^{\prime} \) and \( x^{\prime\prime} \) are the same object, then necessarily \( x^{\prime} \in A \) because \( x^{\prime\prime} \) is an element of \( A \).
    This contradict the established fact that \( x^{\prime} \notin A \).
    Therefore, \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A} \) cannot possibly be true.
    In other words, \( y^{\prime} \notin \imageSet{f}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( y^{\prime} \) is an element of \( \ran f \) that does not belong to \( \imageSet{f}{A} \), it follows that \( \ran f \subset \imageSet{f}{A} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-a-bijection-to-a-subset-of-the-domain-of-the-function}
    If \( \bijection{f}{A}{B} \) and \( S \subseteq  A \), then \( \bijection{\restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S}}{S}{\mleft( B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \mright)} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( f \) is injective, it follows immediately from \cref{thm:metatheory-the-restriction-of-an-injection-is-also-an-injection}
    that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \) is also injective.
    Moreover, since \( S \subseteq  A \), it follows from
    \cref{thm:metatheory-domain-of-restriction-of-f-to-A-is-the-intersection-between-dom-f-and-A,thm:metatheory-the-intersection-between-a-set-and-its-subset-equals-the-subset}
    that \( \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} = S \,{\intersect}\, \dom f = S \,{\intersect}\, A = S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It remains to prove that \( \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} = B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \).
    Let \( y^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object such that \( y^{\prime} \in \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \).
    Thus, there exists an \( x^{\prime} \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Clearly, \( y^{\prime} \in \ran f \) and thus \( y^{\prime} \in B \) since \( \ran f = B \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, we will show that \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A - S} \) leads to a contradiction.
    Suppose that \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A - S} \).
    Then according to \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, there must exist an object \( x^{\prime\prime} \)
    such that \( x^{\prime\prime} \in A \), \( x^{\prime\prime} \notin S \), and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \in f \).
    Since \( f \) is injective, necessarily \( x^{\prime} = x^{\prime\prime} \).
    Since \( x^{\prime\prime} \notin S \), this would imply that \( x^{\prime} \notin S \), which contradicts the fact that \( x^{\prime} \in S \).
    Since \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A - S} \) leads to a contradiction, it cannot possibly be true.
    In other words, \( y^{\prime} \in B \) and \( y^{\prime} \notin \imageSet{f}{A - S} \) are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-difference-of-two-sets}, we thus have \( y^{\prime} \in B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \).
    \begin{equation*}
        y^{\prime} \in \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \ \text{implies} \ y^{\prime} \in B - \imageSet{f}{A - S}
    \end{equation*}
    Given that \( y^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude that \( \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \subseteq B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, suppose that \( y^{\prime} \in B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \).
    Thus, \( y^{\prime} \in B \) and \( y^{\prime} \notin \imageSet{f}{A - S} \).
    Suppose (for the sake of contradiction) that \( y^{\prime} \notin \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \).
    Since \( y^{\prime} \in B \) and \( \ran f = B \), there must exist an \( x^{\prime} \) such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Since \( \dom f = A \), \( x^{\prime} \) must be an element of \( A \).
    Moreover, \( x^{\prime} \) cannot be an element of \( S \); otherwise, \( y^{\prime} \in \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \), which contradicts our initial supposition
    that \( y^{\prime} \notin \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \).
    But if \( x^{\prime} \in A \) and \( x^{\prime} \notin S \) are both true, then necessarily \( x^{\prime} \in A - S \).
    This means that \( x^{\prime} \) is an element of \( A - S \) such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, it follows that \( y^{\prime} \in \imageSet{f}{A - S} \), which is yet another contradiction
    because it has already been established that \( y^{\prime} \notin \imageSet{f}{A - S} \).
    Since \( y^{\prime} \notin \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \) inevitably leads to a contradiction, it cannot possibly be true.
    Therefore, \( y^{\prime} \in \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \).
    \begin{equation*}
        y^{\prime} \in B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \ \text{implies} \ y^{\prime} \in \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( y^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude that \( B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \subseteq \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Having proven that \( \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} \) and \( B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \) are subsets of each other, we conclude
    from \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} that \( \ran \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{S} = B - \imageSet{f}{A - S} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-restricted-function-equals-original-function-restricted-to-intersection}
    If \( f \) is a function and \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \restrictedfunction{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \mright) }{B} = \restrictedfunction{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B}
    \end{equation*}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( g \coloneq \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    We must show that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{B} \) and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \) are subsets of each other; that is,
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{B} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \)
    and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{B} \) are both true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object in the universe. Suppose that \( p \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{B} \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-is-a-function} that both
    \( g \) and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}B \) are functions.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}, \( p \) must be an ordered pair.
    Let \( \alpha \) and \( \beta \) be objects in the universe such that \( p = \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}, it follows that \( \alpha \in B \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in g \),
    the latter of which further implies \( \alpha \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \) because \( g = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \).
    Since \( \alpha \in A \) and \( \alpha \in B \) are both true, necessarily \( \alpha \in A \,{\intersect}\, B \).
    Thus, \( \alpha \in A \,{\intersect}\, B \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \) are true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}, it follows that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( p \in \restrictedfunction{g}{B} \), then \( p \in \restrictedfunction{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{B} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( q \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-is-a-function} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \) is also a function.
    Since \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \), it follows from
    \cref{dfn:metatheory-function} that \( q \) an ordered pair.
    Let \( \gamma \) and \( \delta \) be objects in the universe such that \( q = \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \).
    Since \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \), it follows that \( \gamma \in A \,{\intersect}\, B \)
    and \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in f \) are both true.
    But if \( \gamma \in A \,{\intersect}\, B \), then necessarily \( \gamma \in A \) and \( \gamma \in B \) are both true.
    Thus, we have \( \gamma \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}, it follows that \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) and thus
    \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in g \) by definition.
    But if \( \gamma \in B \) and \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in g \) are both true, then \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{B} \)
    by \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}.
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( q \in \restrictedfunction{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \), then \( q \in \restrictedfunction{g}{B} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( q \) is arbitrary, we concludefrom \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A \,{\intersect}\, B} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{B} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-union-of-compatible-functions-equals-the-union-of-restrictions}
    If \( f \) and \( g \) are \aref{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-two-functions}{compatible} functions and \( A \) is any set, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \restrictedfunction{\mleft(f \,{\union}\, g\mright)}{A} = \restrictedfunction{f}{A} \,{\union}\,\, \restrictedfunction{g}{A}
    \end{equation*}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( h \coloneq \Union \mleft\{ f, g \mright\} \).
    We must show that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \) are subsets of each other.
    We begin by noting that \( h \) is a function because \( f \) and \( g \) are compatible.
    Since \( h \) is a function, \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) must also be a function by \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-is-a-function}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( p \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( p \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \).
    Since \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) is a function, it follows that \( p \) must be an ordered pair.
    Let \( \alpha \) and \( \beta \) denote objects in the universe such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}, it follows that \( \alpha \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in h \) are both true.
    But if the latter is true, then either \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \) or \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in g \) by
    \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}.
    We look at each case in turn.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case:} \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Clearly \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) since \( \alpha \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \) are both true.
    Thus, there exists an element in \( \mleft\{ \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A}, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \mright\} \) that contains \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \).
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case:} \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in g \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Once again, we conclude that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \) because \( \alpha \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in g \)
    are both true.
    This means that there exists an element in \( \mleft\{ \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A}, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \mright\} \)
    that contains \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \).
    Thus, \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It is clear that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \) is true in either case.
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( p \in \restrictedfunction{h}{A} \), then \( p \in \restrictedfunction{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunction{g}{A} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It remains to prove that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) is also true.
    Let \( q \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, it follows that \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) or \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \)
    is true.
    Again, we consider each case separately and see what they imply.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case:} \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( f \) is a function, \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \) must also be a function by \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-is-a-function}.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}, it follows that \( q \) is an ordered pair.
    Let \( \gamma \) and \( \delta \) be objects in the universe such that \( q = \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \).
    Moreover, necessarily \( \gamma \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in f \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}.
    Clearly, \( f \in h \).
    Therefore, there exists an element of \( h \) that contains \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \).
    Given that \( \gamma \in A \) is also true, it should be obvious from \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function} that
    \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) and thus \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case:} \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \) must be a function because \( g \) is a function.
    Functions only contain ordered pairs, so \( q \) must be an ordered pair.
    Let \( \epsilon \) and \( \zeta \) be objects in the universe such that \( q = \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \).
    Given that \( q \in \restrictedfunction{g}{A} \), it follows readily from \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}
    that \( \epsilon \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \in g \) are both true.
    Since \( g \in \mleft\{ f, g \mright\} \), it follows that \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \in h \).
    In other words, \( \epsilon \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \in h \) are necessarily true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}, we conclude that \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \)
    and equivalently \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It is clear that \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) is implied in both cases. From this, we conclude that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \), then \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( q \) is arbitrary, we deduce from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \).
    Having shown that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \) are subsets of each
    other, we conclude from \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \) as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Inverse]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}
    If \( f \) is a function, then the \keyword{inverse} of \( f \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        f^{-1} = \setbuilderbig{\orderedpair{y}{x}}{\orderedpair{x}{y} \in f}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that \( f^{-1} \) is not necessarily a function.
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-inverse-of-a-function}
    For any function \( f \), \( f^{-1} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( f \) is a function, \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-domain-and-range-of-function} guarantees the existence of \( \dom f \) and \( \ran f \).
    Furthermore, \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-cartesian-products} guarantees the existence of sets \( \dom f \times \ran f \) and \( \ran f \times \dom f \).
    Now let \( \varphi(x) \) be the predicate defined as follows:
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \text{there exists an \( a \) and a \( b \) such that \( x = \orderedpair{b}{a} \) and \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in f \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \varphi(p) \) is a true statement;
    that is, there exists an \( \alpha \) and a \( \beta \) such that \( p = \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function,dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function} that
    \( \alpha \in \dom f \) and \( \beta \in \ran f \).
    Clearly, \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \in \ran f \times \dom f \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}.
    Thus, \( p \in \ran f \times \dom f \).
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \ran f \times \dom f \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set},
    the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} = \setbuilder{\orderedpair{y}{x}}{\orderedpair{x}{y} \in f} = f^{-1} \) exists.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-injective-then-inverse-is-a-function}
    Let \( A \), \( B \), and \( f \) be sets.
    If \( \injection{f}{A}{B} \), then \( \bijection{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A} \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    To prove that \( \bijection{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A} \), we must establish the following claims.
    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:f-inverse-is-a-function-on-image-of-A-under-f-into-A}
        \function{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A}
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    We must show that \( f^{-1} \) satisfies the conditions stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-functions-in-terms-of-two-sets}.
    We begin by showing that \( f^{-1} \) is indeed a function.
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( p \in f^{-1} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}, there exist objects \( \alpha \) and \( \beta \)
    such that \( p = \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in f^{-1} \) implies there exist objects \( a \) and \( b \) such that \( x = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) }
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( \gamma \), \( \delta \), and \( \epsilon \) denote arbitrary objects.
    Suppose that \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\delta} \in f^{-1} \) and \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\epsilon} \in f^{-1} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}, necessarily \( \orderedpair{\delta}{\gamma} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\gamma} \in f \).
    Since \( f \) is injective, necessarily \( \delta = \epsilon \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-injection}.
    Thus, \( f^{-1} \) is a function by \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We now prove that \( \dom f^{-1} = \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    Let \( \beta \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \beta \in \dom f^{-1} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, there exists some \( \alpha \) such that \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \in f^{-1} \).
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \). Since \( \function{f}{A}{B} \), we infer \( \alpha \in A \) and subsequently
    \( \beta \in \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    Conversely, suppose that \( \beta \in \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, there exists a \( \gamma \in A \) such that \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\beta} \in f \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\beta} \in f \), then necessarily \( \beta \in \dom f^{-1} \) since
    \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\gamma} \in f^{-1} \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}.
    Since \( \beta \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{ for all \( x \),  \( x \in \dom f^{-1} \) iff \( x \in \imageSet{f}{A} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}, \( \dom f^{-1} = \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Finally, we prove that \( \ran f^{-1} \subseteq A \).
    Let \( \delta \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \ran f^{-1} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, there exists some \( \xi \) such that \( \orderedpair{\xi}{\delta} \in f^{-1} \)
    and thus \( \orderedpair{\delta}{\xi} \in f \).
    But this would mean that \( \delta \in \dom f \), and since \( \dom f = A \), necessarily \( \delta \in A \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \ran f^{-1} \) implies \( x \in A \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, \( \ran f^{-1} \subseteq A \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:f-inverse-is-injective}
        \( f^{-1} \) is injective.
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Suppose that \( f^{-1}(a) = f^{-1}(b) \) for some arbitrary \( a \) and \( b \);
    that is, there exists some \( c \) such that \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in f^{-1} \) and \( \orderedpair{b}{c} \in f^{-1} \)
    are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}, it follows that \( \orderedpair{c}{a} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{c}{b} \in f \)
    are both true.
    But \( f \) is a function, so \( a \) and \( b \) must be equal by \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( f^{-1}(a) = f^{-1}(b) \), then \( a = b \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, \( f^{-1} \) must be injective by \cref{dfn:metatheory-injection}.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:range-of-f-inverse-is-A}
        \( \ran f^{-1} = A \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    The first claim itself already implies that \( \ran f^{-1} \subseteq A \).
    It remains to prove that \( A \subseteq \ran f^{-1} \).
    Let \( a \in A \) be arbitrary.
    Since \( \function{f}{A}{B} \), it follows that \( \dom f = A \).
    Thus, \( a \in \dom f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, there must exist a \( b \) such that
    \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in f \), which in turn implies that \( \orderedpair{b}{a} \in f^{-1} \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( a \in A \), then there exists a \( b \) such that \( \orderedpair{b}{a} \in f^{-1} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, necessarily \( a \in \ran f^{-1} \).
    Therefore,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( a \in A \), then \( a \in \ran f^{-1} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( a \) denotes an arbitrary object, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in A \) implies \( x \in \ran f^{-1} \) }
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, \( A \subseteq \ran f^{-1} \) as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-inverse-of-bijection-is-another-bijection}
    Let \( A \), \( B \), and \( f \) be sets.
    If \( \bijection{f}{A}{B} \), then \( \bijection{f^{-1}}{B}{A} \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( \bijection{f}{A}{B} \), it follows that \aref{dfn:metatheory-bijection}{\( \injection{f}{A}{B} \) and \( \surjection{f}{A}{B} \) are both true}.
    Since \( \injection{f}{A}{B} \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-injective-then-inverse-is-a-function} that
    \( \function{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A} \).
    Also, given that \( \surjection{f}{A}{B} \), it follows that \( \ran f = B \).
    But \( \dom f = A \), so by \cref{thm:metatheory-image-of-the-domain-of-a-function-under-the-function-equals-the-range-of-the-function},
    \( \imageSet{f}{A} = \imageSet{f}{\dom f} = \ran f = B \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \function{f^{-1}}{B}{A}
    \end{equation*}
    It remains to prove that \( f^{-1} \) is injective and that \( \ran f^{-1} = A \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) be objects in the universe such that \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in f^{-1} \) and \( \orderedpair{b}{c} \in f^{-1} \)
    are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}, it follows that \( \orderedpair{c}{a} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{c}{b} \in f \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, necessarily \( a \) and \( b \) are equal.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \injection{f^{-1}}{B}{A}
    \end{equation*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Finally, we prove that \( \ran f^{-1} = A \) by showing that \( A \subseteq \ran f^{-1} \) because \( \ran f^{-1} \subseteq A \) is already implied by
    \( \function{f^{-1}}{B}{A} \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary element of \( A \). Since \( A = \dom f \), it follows that \( \alpha \in \dom f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, there must exist a \( \beta \) such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \) is true, then necessarily \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \in f^{-1} \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}.
    Since \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \in f^{-1} \) and \( \beta \) is an existing object, necessarily \( \alpha \in \ran f^{-1} \) by
    \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}.
    Thus, \( A \subseteq \ran f^{-1} \) as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-composition-of-injective-functions-is-also-injective}
    If \( f \) and \( g \) are injections, then \( \composition{g}{f} \) is also an injection.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( f \) and \( g \) be injective functions. Since \( f \) and \( g \) are functions,
    it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-the-composition-of-functions-is-a-function} that \( \composition{g}{f} \)
    is a function.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \), \( x^{\prime\prime} \), and \( y^{\prime} \) be objects in the universe such that
    \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \composition{g}{f} \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \composition{g}{f} \)
    are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-composition-of-two-sets-of-ordered-pairs}, it follows that
    \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{z^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) for some \( z^{\prime} \)
    and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{z^{\prime\prime}} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{z^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) for some \( z^{\prime\prime} \).
    Since \( g \) is injective, the fact that \( \orderedpair{z^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) and \( \orderedpair{z^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \)
    implies that \( z^{\prime} = z^{\prime\prime} \).
    But \( f \) is also injective, so \( x^{\prime} = x^{\prime\prime} \) follows from the fact that \( z^{\prime} \) and \( z^{\prime\prime} \) are the same object.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \text{\( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \composition{g}{f} \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \) implies
        \( x^{\prime} = x^{\prime\prime} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( \composition{g}{f} \) is injective.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Indexed Family of Sets]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-indexed-family-of-sets}
    A set \( S \) is an \keyword{indexed family of sets} iff \( S \) is a function and \( \ran S \) is a family of sets.
    If \( S \) is an indexed family of sets, then we refer to \( \dom S \) as the \keyword{index} or \keyword{index set} of \( S \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Union]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-union-of-indexed-family-of-sets}
    If \( S \) is an indexed family of sets, then the union of \( S \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \Union S  = \setbuilderbig{x}{\text{there exists an \( i \in \dom S \) such that \( x \in S_{i} \) }}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( \Union S = \Union \ran S \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Intersection]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-intersection-of-indexed-family-of-sets}
    If \( S \) is an indexed family of sets, then the intersection of \( S \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \Intersect S = \setbuilderbig{x}{\text{for all \( i \), \( i \in \dom S \) implies \( x \in S_{i} \)}}
    \end{equation*}
    That is, \( \Intersect S = \Intersect \ran S \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    If \( S \) is an indexed family of sets and \( I = \dom S \),
    we also use the notations \( \Union_{i \,\in\, I}  S_{i} \) and \( \Intersect_{i \,\in\, I} S_{i} \) to denote \( \Union S \) and \( \Intersect S \) respectively.
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Exponentiation]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-set-exponentiation}
    If \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, the set \( B^{A} \) is defined as the set of all functions on \( A \) into \( B \). That is,
    \begin{equation*}
        B^{A} \coloneq \setbuilderbig{f}{\function{f}{A}{B}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Compatibility]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-indexed-families-of-functions}
    An indexed family of functions \( F \) is compatible iff for all \( i, j \in \dom F \), \( F_{i} \) and \( F_{j} \) are compatible.
\end{dfn}



\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-set-exponentiation}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), \( B^{A} \) exists and is unique.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( A \) and \( B \) are sets, the cartesian product \( A \times B \) exists by \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-cartesian-products}.
    Let \( \varphi(f) \) be a predicate defined as follows:
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(f) \coloneq \function{f}{A}{B}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( f^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \varphi(f^{\prime}) \) is a true statement; i.e., \( \function{f^{\prime}}{A}{B} \).
    Let \( p \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( p \in f^{\prime} \).
    Since \( f^{\prime} \) is a function, \( p \) must be an ordered pair;
    that is, there exists objects \( a \) and \( b \) such that \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function,dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, it follows that
    \( a \in \dom f^{\prime} \) and \( b \in \ran f^{\prime} \).
    Since \( \dom f^{\prime} = A \) and \( \ran f^{\prime} \), necessarily \( a \in A \) and \( b \in B \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, necessarily \( p \in A \times B \).
    Since \( f^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in A \times B \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set}, the set \( \setbuilder{f}{\varphi(f)} = B^{A} \) exists.
    The uniqueness of \( B^{A} \) follows readily from \cref{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-specification-subset}.
\end{proof}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:metatheory-set-exponentiation-is-the-subset-of-cartesian-product-and-element-of-power-set-of-cartesian-product}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), \( B^{A} \subseteq A \times B \) and thus \( B^{A} \in \powerset{A \times B} \).
\end{cor}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-exponentiation-with-empty-exponent}
    For any set \( A \), \( A^{\emptyset} = \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( f \) be an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( f \in A^{\emptyset} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-set-exponentiation}, it follows that \( \function{f}{\emptyset}{A} \).
    Suppose that \( f \neq \emptyset \). Then there exists some \( p \) such that \( p \in f \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, it follows that \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) for some \( a \) and \( b \).
    But by \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, this would imply that \( a \in \dom f \), which contradicts the fact
    that \( \dom f = \emptyset \).
    By \cref{meta-rule:reductio-ad-absurdum}, the supposition that \( f \neq \emptyset \) must be false.
    In other words, \( f = \emptyset \).
    
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, if \( f = \emptyset \), then the two conditions stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-function} are vacuously true.
    Moreover, \( \dom f = \emptyset \) and \( \ran f = \emptyset \).
    Since \( \emptyset \) is a subset of every set, it follows that \( \ran f \subseteq A \).
    In other words, \( \function{f}{\emptyset}{A} \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, f \in A^{\emptyset} \ \text{iff} \ f = \emptyset
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( f \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,meta-axiom:extensionality,meta-axiom:pairing}
    that \( A^{\emptyset} = \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-exponentiation-with-empty-base}
    For any set \( A \),
    \begin{equation*}
        \emptyset^{A} =
        \begin{cases}
            \ \emptyset \ &\text{if} \ A \neq \emptyset\\            
            \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \ &\text{if} \ A = \emptyset
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    If \( A = \emptyset \), the theorem follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-exponentiation-with-empty-exponent}.
    It remains to prove that \( \emptyset^{A} = \emptyset \) if \( A \neq \emptyset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose that \( A \neq \emptyset \).
    Let \( f \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( f \in \emptyset^{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-set-exponentiation}, necessarily \( \function{f}{A}{\emptyset} \);
    that is, \( f \) is a function with \( \dom f = A \) and \( \ran f \subseteq \emptyset \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-subset-of-an-empty-set-must-be-empty}, it follows that \( \ran f = \emptyset \).
    But if \( \ran f = \emptyset \), then necessarily \( \dom f = \emptyset \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-function-with-empty-range-must-have-empty-domain}.
    But this would mean that \( A = \dom f = \emptyset \), which contradicts our initial supposition that \( A \neq \emptyset \).
    Since \( f \in \emptyset^{A} \) leads to a contradiction, it must be false. In other words, \( f \notin \emptyset^{A} \).
    Since \( f \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \notin \emptyset^{A} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-empty-set}, necessarily \( \emptyset^{A} = \emptyset \).
\end{proof}



\subsection{The Counting Numbers}
Our metatheory is now sophisticated enough that we can build the counting numbers with it.
We will preface the construction of the counting numbers with the following definitions and axiom.

\begin{dfn}[Successor]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-successor}
    For any set \( x \), the set \( x \union \mleft\{ x \mright\} \) is called the \keyword{successor} of \( x \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Inductive Set]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}
    A set \( I \) is \keyword{inductive} iff the following statements are true:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt, label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \emptyset \in I \)
        \item for all \( x \), \( x \in I \) implies \( x \union \mleft\{ x \mright\} \in I \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:existence-of-inductive-set}
    An inductive set exists in the universe.
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-successor}
    For any two sets \( A \) and \( B \), \( A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} = B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \) iff \( A = B \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose that \( A = B \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary element of \( A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \).
    Thus, either \( \alpha \in A \) or \( \alpha = A \).
    If \( \alpha \in A \), then \( \alpha \in B \) must also be true since \( A = B \).
    On the other hand, if \( \alpha = A \), then \( \alpha = B \) by \cref{meta-axiom:equality-axioms}.
    By \cref{meta-rule:constructive-dilemma}, it follows that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( \alpha \in A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\}\), then either \( \alpha \in B \) or \( \alpha = B \)}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( \alpha \in A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) implies \( \alpha \in B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \subseteq B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \).
    We can prove the converse relation with a similar argument.
    Let \( \beta \) denote an arbitrary element of \( B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \).
    Again, we are left with only two possibilities: \( \beta \in B \) or \( \beta = B \).
    If \( \beta \in B \), then \( \beta \) is also an element of \( A \) since \( A = B \).
    If \( \beta = B \), then \( \beta = A \) by the transitive property of the equality relation as stated in \cref{meta-axiom:equality-axioms}.
    Thus, \( \beta \in B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \) implies \( \beta \in A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) for any arbitrary \( \beta \).
    In other words, \( B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \subseteq A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \).
    Since \( A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) and \( B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \) are subsets of each other, they must be equal sets.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \text{\( A = B \) implies \( A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} = B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \)}
    \end{equation*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    To prove the converse, suppose that \( A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} = B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \).
    We know that \( A \in A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \), which means that \( A \in B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \).
    In other words, either \( A = B \) or \( A \in B \). We consider each possibility in turn.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( A \in B \), then \( B \notin A \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-asymmetry-of-membership-relation}.
    But \( B \in B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \) by \cref{meta-axiom:union,dfn:metatheory-singleton}.
    Thus, it follows that \( B \in A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) since \( A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} \) and \( B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \) are equal sets;
    that is, either \( B \in A \) or \( B = A \).
    It has already been established that \( B \notin A \), so necessarily \( B = A \) by \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}.
    By \cref{meta-axiom:equality-axioms}, it follows that \( A = B \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Clearly, \( A = B \) is true in both cases.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} = B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \ \text{implies} \ A = B
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, the statements \( A = B \) and \( A \union \mleft\{ A \mright\} = B \union \mleft\{ B \mright\} \) imply each other.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-the-set-of-natural-numbers}
    The set \( \setbuilder{x}{\text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( x \in I \)}} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We start by defining the following predicate.
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \ \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( x \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    \cref{meta-axiom:existence-of-inductive-set} guarantees that there exists at least one inductive set, which we will denote with \( \mathcal{I} \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object such that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement; that is,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( \alpha \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since the set \( \mathcal{I} \) is inductive, it follows that \( \alpha \in \mathcal{I} \).
    In other words, \( \varphi(\alpha) \) implies \( \alpha \in \mathcal{I} \).
    But \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, so we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \mathcal{I} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \mathcal{I} \) is an existing set, it follows readilly from \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set} that the set
    \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists and is unique.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}
    \( \naturalset = \setbuilder{x}{\text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( x \in I \)}} \)
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    If \( n \in \naturalset \), then we denote the successor of \( n \) as \( n + 1 \).
    Additionally, we also write \( \naturalset^{+} \) to refer to the set \( \naturalset - \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \); i.e., the set of all
    non-zero (non-empty) natural numbers.
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-ordering-of-natural-numbers}
    For any two natural numbers \( m \) and \( n \), we say that \( m \) is \keyword{less than} \( n \) (denoted as \( m < n \))
    iff \( m \in n \).
    Similarly, we write \( m \leq n \) to abbreviate the statement ``\( m \in n \) or \( m = n \).''
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    It is not difficult to see that the set \( \naturalset \) contains exactly the counting numbers
    as long as we interpret the symbols for the counting numbers with the following scheme:
    \begin{align*}
        0 &\coloneq \emptyset = \mleft\{ \mright\} \\
        1 &\coloneq 0 \union \mleft\{ 0 \mright\} = \{0\} \\
        2 &\coloneq 1 \union \mleft\{ 1 \mright\} = \{0\} \union \{1\} = \{0,1\}\\
        3 &\coloneq 2 \union \mleft\{ 2 \mright\} = \{0,1\} \union \{2\} = \{0,1,2\}\\
        \vdots
    \end{align*}
    This scheme (courtesy of John von Neumann) essentially defines a natural number as a set that contains every other natural number less than it.
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}[Least Element]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-least-element-of-a-non-empty-subset-of-N}
    If \( S \) is a non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \), then an object \( \lambda \) is the \keyword{least element} of \( S \)
    iff the following conditions hold:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \lambda \in S \)
        \item for all \( x \in S \), \( \lambda \leq x \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Greatest Element]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-greatest-element-of-a-non-empty-subset-of-N}
    If \( S \) is  a non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \), then an object \( \gamma \) is the \keyword{greatest element} of \( S \)
    iff the following conditions hold:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \gamma \in S \)
        \item for all \( x \in S \), \( x \leq \gamma \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Lower Bound]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-lower-bound-of-a-subset-of-N}
    If \( S \subseteq \naturalset \), then an object \( \lambda \) is a \keyword{lower bound} of \( S \)
    iff the following conditions hold:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \lambda \in \naturalset \)
        \item for all \( x \in S \), \( \lambda \leq x \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Upper Bound]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-upper-bound-of-a-subset-of-N}
    If \( S \subseteq \naturalset \), then an object \( \mu \) is an \keyword{upper bound} of \( S \)
    iff the following conditions hold:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \mu \in \naturalset \)
        \item for all \( x \in S \), \( x \leq \mu \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Boundedness]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-boundedness-of-a-subset-of-N}
    A subset \( S \) of \( \naturalset \) is \keyword{bounded} iff either an upper bound of \( S \) or a lower bound of \( S \) exists.
    If \( S \) has an upper bound, then \( S \) is said to be \keyword{bounded from above}.
    Similarly, if \( S \) has a lower bound, then \( S \) is \keyword{bounded from below}.
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-antisymmetric-property-of-membership-relation-of-N}
    If \( n, m \in \naturalset \) such that \( n \leq m \) and \( m \leq n \) are both true,
    then \( n = m \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( n \leq m \), it follows that either \( n \in m \) or \( n = m \).
    Suppose that \( n \in m \).
    Then \( m \notin n \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-asymmetry-of-membership-relation}.
    Since \( m \leq n \) is trueand \( m \notin n \), we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism} that \( m = n \).
    But \( n \in m \), so \( m = n \) would imply \( n \in n \), which contradicts \cref{thm:metatheory-no-set-is-an-element-of-itself}.
    Since \( n \in m \) leads to a contradiction, it cannot be true.
    By \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}, necessarily \( n = m \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-least-element-of-a-non-empty-subset-of-N}
    Every non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \) has at most one least element.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) be an arbitrary non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( \lambda \) and \( \lambda^{\prime} \) are both least elements of \( S \).
    It thus follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-least-element-of-a-non-empty-subset-of-N} that \( \lambda \leq \lambda^{\prime} \)
    and \( \lambda^{\prime} \leq \lambda \) are both true.
    Since \( S \subseteq \naturalset \) and \( \lambda \) and \( \lambda^{\prime} \) are both elements of \( S \), 
    \cref{thm:metatheory-antisymmetric-property-of-membership-relation-of-N} applies, and we conclude that \( \lambda = \lambda^{\prime} \).
    Thus, the least element of \( S \), if it exists, must be unique.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-greatest-element-of-a-non-empty-subset-of-N}
    Every non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \) has at most one greatest element.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Same argument as in \cref{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-least-element-of-a-non-empty-subset-of-N}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-natural-numbers-is-a-subset-of-any-inductive-set}
    If \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( \naturalset \subseteq I \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( I \) be any inductive set.
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \). By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}, the following statement is true:
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( y \), if \( y \) is an inductive set, then \( \alpha \in y \) }
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( I \) is an object in the universe, \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} yields
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \alpha \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    But \( I \) is indeed inductive, so necessarily \( \alpha \in I \) by \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens}.
    In other words, we have proven that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \) implies \( \alpha \in I \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    for all \( x \), \( x \in \naturalset \) implies \( x \in I \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( \naturalset \subseteq I \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers-is-inductive}
    \( \naturalset \) is an inductive set.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( I \) denote an arbitrary set.
    Suppose that \( I \) is \aref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}{inductive}.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}, necessarily \( \emptyset \in I \).
    In other words, the implication ``if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \emptyset \in I \)'' is true.
    Since \( I \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}
    that \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object. Suppose that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \); that is,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \alpha \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( I^{\prime} \) denote an arbitary inductive set. By \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation}, it follows that
    \( \alpha \in I^{\prime} \). Since \( I^{\prime} \) is inductive and \( \alpha \in I^{\prime} \),
    \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set} guarantees that \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in I^{\prime} \).
    Since \( I^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we apply \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} to infer
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
