    But by \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}, this implies \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in \naturalset \).
    In other words, \( \alpha \in \naturalset \) implies \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in \naturalset \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} once again that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \naturalset \) implies \( x \union \mleft\{ x \mright\} \in \naturalset \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Given that \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \), \( \naturalset \) is necessarily inductive by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Finite Set]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-finite-set-and-cardinality}
    A set \( S \) is \keyword{finite} iff there exists an \( n \in \naturalset \) such that a \keyword{bijection} exists
    between \( n \) and \( S \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Countable Set]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-countable-set}
    A set \( S \) is \keyword{countable} iff \( S \) is finite or a bijection exists between \( \naturalset \) and \( S \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Sequence]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-sequence}
    A set \( f \) is a \keyword{sequence} iff it is a function such that \( \dom f \in \naturalset \) or \( \dom f = \naturalset \).
    A sequence is \keyword{finite} iff it is a finite set; i.e., \( \dom f \in \naturalset \).
    A sequence is \keyword{infinite} iff it is not finite.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Tuple]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-tuple}
    A finite sequence is also called a \keyword{tuple}.
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    If \( S \) is a sequence, then it is customary to denote the value of \( S \) at \( i \) as \( S_{i} \).
    To construct a new sequence from \( S \), we write \( \sequence{\epsilon_{n}}{n \in \dom S} \) where \( \epsilon_{n} \)
    is an \aref{dfn:metatheory-expressions}{expression} built using \( S_{n} \).
\end{notation}

\begin{eg}
    Obviously, \( \sequence{S_{n}}{n \in \dom S} \) is merely an alias for \( S \) itself.
    We can build more complicated sequences using this notational scheme, of course.
    For instance, if \( A \) and \( B \) are \aref{dfn:metatheory-indexed-family-of-sets}{indexed families of sets} such that
    \( \dom A = \dom B = n \) where \( n \in \naturalset \), then
    \( \sequence{A_{i} \,{\union}\, B_{i}}{i \in n} \) denotes the finite sequence of the unions of the sets from each family.
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}[Generalized Conjunction]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-generalized-conjunction}
    If \( \Phi \) is a finite sequence of statements (or predicates), then the \keyword{conjunction} of \( \Phi \)
    is the statement:
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( i \in \dom \Phi \), \( \Phi_{i} \) is a true statement}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Generalized Disjunction]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-generalized-disjunction}
    If \( \Phi \) is a finite sequence of statements (or predicates), then the \keyword{disjunction} of \( \Phi \)
    is the statement:
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{there exists an \( i \in \dom \Phi \) such that \( \Phi_{i} \) is a true statement}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    If \( \Phi \) is a finite sequence of statements, then we denote the conjunction and disjunction of \( \Phi \)
    as \( \conjunction{\Phi} \) and \( \disjunction{\Phi} \) respectively.
\end{notation}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-empty-set-is-the-zero-tuple-and-empty-sequence}
    An empty tuple or sequence is the empty set.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( T \) be a finite sequence of length \( 0 \).
    Since \( 0 = \emptyset \), it follows that \( \dom T = \emptyset \) and thus \( T = \emptyset \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-set-of-all-n-ary-products}
    Given any set \( A \), the set \( \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \) exists and is unique and non-empty.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let the predicate \( \varphi(x) \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \text{there exists an \( n \) such that \( n \in \naturalset \) and \( x = A^{n} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    There are two cases to consider.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case} \( A = \emptyset \):
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object in the universe such that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement; that is,
    \( \alpha = A^{k} \) for some \( k \in \naturalset \). Since \( A = \emptyset \) and \( k \in \naturalset \), it follows from
    \cref{thm:metatheory-exponentiation-with-empty-base} that either \( \alpha = \emptyset \) or \( \alpha = \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \).
    In other words, \( \alpha \in \mleft\{ \emptyset, \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \mright\} \). Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary
    and \( \mleft\{ \emptyset, \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \mright\} \) is an existing set, it follows from
    \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set} that the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} = \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \) exists
    and is unique by \cref{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-specification-subset}.
    To prove that \( \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \neq \emptyset \), it suffices to show that \( \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \in \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \).
    We know from \cref{thm:metatheory-exponentiation-with-empty-exponent} that \( \emptyset^{\emptyset} = \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \).
    Since \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \) and \( \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} = \emptyset^{\emptyset} = A^{\emptyset} \),
    it follows that \( \varphi(\mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\}) \) is a true statement.
    Thus, \( \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \in \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \).
    Since \( \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \) contains at least one element, it must be non-empty.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case} \( A \neq \emptyset \):
    Let \( \beta \) be an arbitrary object in the universe such that \( \varphi(\beta) \) is a true statement;
    that is, \( \beta = A^{j} \) for some \( j \in \naturalset \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-set-exponentiation}, it follows that
    \begin{equation*}
        \beta = \setbuilderbig{f}{\function{f}{j}{A}}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( f^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \beta \); i.e., \( \function{f^{\prime}}{j}{A} \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-function-is-subset-of-cartesian-product}, it follows that \( f^{\prime} \subseteq j \times A \).
    Since \( j \in \naturalset \), \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N} requires that \( j \subseteq \naturalset \).
    The fact that \( A \) is not empty means that \cref{thm:metatheory-subsets-form-cartesian-product-subsets} is applicable and we thus have
    \begin{equation*}
        f^{\prime} \subseteq j \times A \subseteq \naturalset \times A
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( f^{\prime} \in \powerset{\naturalset \times A} \). Since \( f^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \powerset{\naturalset \times A} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    where \( \powerset{\naturalset \times A} \) is an existing set.
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set}, the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} = \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \) exists
    and is unique by \cref{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-specification-subset}.
    Finally, we have \( A^{\emptyset} = \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-exponentiation-with-empty-exponent} so that
    \( \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \in \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \).
    In other words, \( \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \) is non-empty.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-set-of-all-finite-sequence}
    If \( A \) is a set, then \( \finiteseq{A} \) denotes the set \( \Union \sequence{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-the-set-of-all-finite-sequences}
    For any set \( A \), \( \finiteseq{A} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( A^{\prime} \) denote the set \( \setbuilder{A^{n}}{n \in \naturalset} \), which exists and is non-empty as implied by 
    \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-set-of-all-n-ary-products}.
    Let \( \varphi(x) \) be a predicate with the following definition.
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \text{there exists an \( n \in \naturalset \) such that \( x = \orderedpair{n}{A^{n}} \) }
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object in the universe such that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement.
    Thus, there exists some \( k \in \naturalset \) such that \( \alpha = \orderedpair{k}{A^{k}} \).
    From \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, we infer that \( \alpha \in k \times A^{\prime} \).
    Since \( k \in \naturalset \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N} that \( k \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Given that \( A^{\prime} \) is non-empty, we infer from \cref{thm:metatheory-subsets-form-cartesian-product-subsets} that
    \( k \times A^{\prime} \subseteq \naturalset \times A^{\prime} \).
    Since \( \alpha \in k \times A^{\prime} \), it thus follows that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \times A^{\prime} \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \naturalset \times A^{\prime} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    where \( \naturalset \times A^{\prime} \) is an existing set.
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set,thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-specification-subset},
    the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists and is unique.
    We now show that the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) is a function.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( S \coloneq \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \).
    If \( \beta \) is an arbitrary element of \( S \), then clearly \( \beta \) is an ordered pair of the form \( \orderedpair{k}{A^{k}} \) where \( k \in \naturalset \).
    Additionally, \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-set-exponentiation} guarantees the uniqueness of \( A^{k} \).
    Therefore, \( S \) is a function.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We now prove that \( \dom S = \naturalset \).
    Let \( \gamma \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \gamma \in \dom S \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, there exists a \( \Delta \) such that \( \orderedpair{\gamma}{\Delta} \in S \).
    In other words, \( \varphi(\orderedpair{\gamma}{\Delta}) \) is a true statement, so we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \gamma \in \naturalset \ \text{and} \ \Delta = A^{\gamma}
    \end{equation*}
    More importantly, we have shown that \( \gamma \in \dom S \) implies \( \gamma \in \naturalset \). Since \( \gamma \) denotes
    an arbitrary object, \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} yields the statement
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \dom S \) implies \( x \in \naturalset \)}
    \end{equation*}
    This allows us to conclude \( \dom S \subseteq \naturalset \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}.
    The converse relation is straightforward to prove. Let \( n \) denote any element of \( \naturalset \).
    Since \( n \) and \( A \) are both sets, \cref{thm:existence-of-set-exponentiation} guarantees the existence and uniqueness of the set
    \( A^{n} \).
    By definition, the ordered pair \( \orderedpair{n}{A^{n}} \) must satisfy \( \varphi(x) \) and be an element of \( S \), which then implies that
    \( n \in \dom S \).
    Given that \( \dom S \) and \( \naturalset \) are subsets of each other, they are necessarily equal sets.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    The next task would be to prove that \( \ran S \) is a family of sets; that is,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:range-of-S-is-a-family-of-sets}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \ran S \) implies \( x \) is a set}
    \end{equation}
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( x^{\prime} \in \ran S \); i.e., \( x^{\prime} = A^{m} \) for some \( m \in \naturalset \).
    Clearly, \( x^{\prime} \) is a set, so \eqref{eq:range-of-S-is-a-family-of-sets} is indeed a true statement.
    The existence of \( \finiteseq{A} = \Union_{n \,\in\, \naturalset} S_{n} = \Union \ran S \) follows from
    \cref{meta-axiom:union,dfn:metatheory-union-of-indexed-family-of-sets}.
\end{proof}



\begin{thm}[Weak Induction]
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}
    Let \( P(n) \) be a predicate where the following conditions hold:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( P(0) \) is a true statement
        \item for all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \)
    \end{enumerate}
    Then  \( \naturalset = \setbuilder{ n \in \naturalset }{P(n)} \).
    In other words, \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) denote the set \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \) whose existence is guaranteed by \cref{meta-axiom:specification}.
    We begin by proving that \( S \) is an inductive set.
    Note that \( P(\emptyset) \) is necessarily a true statement by the definition of \( P(n) \) itself.
    Furthermore, we know from \cref{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers-is-inductive} that \( \naturalset \) is inductive,
    which means that \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}.
    In other words, \( P(\emptyset) \) and \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \) are both true statements.
    Therefore, \( \emptyset \in S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Now let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in S \); that is, \( \alpha \in \naturalset \) and \( P(\alpha) \) are both true.
    But if \( P(\alpha) \) is true, \( P(\alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\}) \) must also be a true statement
    by the definition of \( P(n) \).
    Furthermore, \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in \naturalset \) is also true by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}
    since \( \naturalset \) is inductive.
    In other words, \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in \naturalset \) and \( P(\alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\}) \) are both
    true statements.
    Therefore, \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in S \).
    This means that \( \alpha \in S \) implies \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in S \) implies \( x \union \mleft\{ x \mright\} \in S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \emptyset \in S \) is also true, it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set} that \( S \) is an inductive set.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We already know from \cref{thm:metatheory-restricted-comprehension-set-is-the-subset-of-an-existing-set} that \( S \subseteq \naturalset \).
    It remains to show that \( \naturalset \subseteq S \).
    Let \( \beta \) denote an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \beta \in \naturalset \).
    Now consider the definition of \( \naturalset \) as stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}.
    Since \( \beta \in \naturalset \), it follows that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \beta \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation}, we further infer that if \( S \) is inductive, then \( \beta \in S \).
    But we have already shown that \( S \) is inductive. Thus, by \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens}, \( \beta \in S \) is necessarily true.
    In other words, \( \beta \in \naturalset \) implies \( \beta \in S \).
    Since \( \beta \) denotes an arbitrary object, \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} is applicable and we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), if \( x \in \naturalset \), then \( x \in S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( \naturalset \subseteq S \).
    Since \( \naturalset \) and \( S \) are subsets of each other, \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} guarantees that
    \( \naturalset = S = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{rem}
    The Principle of Weak Induction as stated in \cref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction} essentially says that if we have a
    predicate about the natural numbers such that \( P(0) \) is true and that \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \),
    then \( P(n) \) can be satisfied by all elements of \( \naturalset \).
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-base-case-and-inductive-step-induction-hypothesis}
    In any application of the \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Principle of Weak Induction} on a predicate \( P(n) \),
    the statement \( P(\emptyset) \) is known as the \keyword{base case} whereas
    the statement ``for all \( n \), if \( n \in \naturalset \), then \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \)''
    is known as the \keyword{inductive step}.
    The statement \( P(n) \) in the implication ``\( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1)\)'' is also known as the \keyword{induction hypothesis}.
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}[Strong Induction]
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-strong-induction}
    Let \( P(n) \) be any predicate such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( Q(n) \) implies \( P(n) \) }
    \end{equation*}
    where \( Q(n) \) is the predicate ``for all \( m < n \), \( P(m) \)''.
    Then \( \naturalset = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    By assumption, we know that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:Q-n-initial-assumption}
        \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( Q(n) \) implies \( P(n) \) }
    \end{equation}
    The statement \( Q(\emptyset) \) can be translated as
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( m < \emptyset \), \( P(m) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    But \( \emptyset \) is empty, so this statement is \aref{dfn:metatheory-vacuous-truth}{vacuously true}.
    This means that \( Q(\emptyset) \) is true, thus establishing the base case.
    To establish the inductive step, let \( n^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( Q(n^{\prime}) \) is a true statement; that is,
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:Q-n-prime-statement}
        \text{for all \( m < n^{\prime} \), \( P(m) \)}
    \end{equation}
    Let \( e \) denote an arbitrary element of \( n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} \union \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \), it follows that either \( e = n^{\prime} \) or \( e \in n^{\prime} \).
    If \( e = n^{\prime} \), then \( P(e) \) must be true because \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true as a result of applying
    \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to \eqref{eq:Q-n-initial-assumption}.
    If \( e \in n^{\prime} \), then \( P(e) \) is also true by \eqref{eq:Q-n-prime-statement}.
    Since \( e \) is an arbitrary element of \( n^{\prime} + 1 \), we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    that \( P(m) \) is true for all \( m < n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    In other words, \( Q(n^{\prime} + 1) \) must be true whenever \( Q(n^{\prime}) \) is true, which establishes the inductive step.
    Having established the base case and the inductive step, we can validly conclude by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:Q-n-true-for-all-natural-numbers}
        \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( Q(n) \)}
    \end{equation}
    Now let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \). From \eqref{eq:Q-n-initial-assumption} and \eqref{eq:Q-n-true-for-all-natural-numbers},
    it follows that \( P(\alpha) \) must be true.
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \). In other words,
    \( \naturalset \subseteq \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \).
    Furthermore, \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \subseteq \naturalset \) is also true
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-restricted-comprehension-set-is-the-subset-of-an-existing-set}.
    Since \( \naturalset \) and \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \) are subsets of each other, it follows from
    \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} that \( \naturalset = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( n \subseteq \naturalset \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We begin by defining the following predicate.
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:induction-predicate}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{\( n \) is a set and \( n \subseteq \naturalset \)}
    \end{equation}
    Now we will show by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} = \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}:
    Clearly, \( 0 = \emptyset \) is a set. Furthermore, \( \emptyset \) is a subset of any set by \cref{thm:metatheory-empty-set-is-the-subset-of-every-set}.
    Therefore, \( P(0) \) is a true statement.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: Let \( k \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( P(k) \) is a true statement; that is, \( k \) is a set and \( k \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Since \( k \) is a set, \( k + 1 = k \union \mleft\{ k \mright\} \) must also be a set by definition.
    Now let \( e \) be an arbitrary element of \( k + 1 \); that is, either \( e \in k \) or \( e = k \).
    Since \( k \in \naturalset \), \( e \) obviously belongs to \( \naturalset \) if \( e = k \).
    On the other hand, if \( e \in k \), then \( e \in \naturalset \) must also be true because \( k \subseteq \naturalset \) is true by the
    \aref{dfn:metatheory-base-case-and-inductive-step-induction-hypothesis}{induction hypothesis}.
    In other words, \( e \in \naturalset \) is true in either possibility.
    By \cref{meta-rule:disjunction-elimination}, we reason that
    \begin{equation*}
        e \in k + 1 \ \text{implies} \ e \in \naturalset
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( e \) is arbitrary, we can further conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in k + 1 \) implies \( x \in \naturalset \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, necessarily \( k + 1 \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Since \( k + 1 \) is a set and \( k + 1 \subseteq \naturalset \), it follows from \eqref{eq:induction-predicate} that
    \( P(k + 1) \) is a true statement.
    Since \( k \) denotes an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \), the inductive step follows readilly from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Having established both the base case and the inductive step, the theorem follows from \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-proper-subset-of-N}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( n \subset \naturalset \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N}, it follows that \( n^{\prime} \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Since \( \naturalset \) is inductive and \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}
    that \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \naturalset \).
    Moreover, \( n^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordering-of-natural-numbers,dfn:metatheory-successor}.
    But \( n^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) implies \( n^{\prime} + 1 \notin n^{\prime} \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-asymmetry-of-membership-relation}.
    In other words, \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) is an element of \( \naturalset \) that does not belong to \( n^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( n^{\prime} \subset \naturalset \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the theorem follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-contains-the-empty-set}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( n \neq \emptyset \) implies \( \emptyset \in n \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( P(n) \) be a predicate with the following definition.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq  \text{\( n \in \naturalset \) and if \( n \neq \emptyset \), then \( \emptyset \in n \)}
    \end{equation*}
    We will show by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak induction} that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(\emptyset) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Clearly \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \) because \aref{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers-is-inductive}{\( \naturalset \) is inductive}.
    Furthermore, the implication ``if \( \emptyset \neq \emptyset \), then \( \emptyset \in \emptyset \)'' is vacuously true because \( \emptyset = \emptyset \).
    Thus, \( P(\emptyset) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    If \( n^{\prime} = \emptyset \), then clearly \( \emptyset \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) because \( n^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} \union \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \).
    If \( n^{\prime} \neq \emptyset \), then \( \emptyset \in n^{\prime} \) since \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true by assumption.
    But if \( \emptyset \in n^{\prime} \), then necessarily \( \emptyset \in n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    In either case, \( \emptyset \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) follows.
    Thus, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) holds.
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the inductive step follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Having established both the base case and inductive step, the theorem follows from \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-the-set-of-all-elements-of-N-that-is-less-than-itself}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( n = \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{x < n} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary element of \( \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{x < n^{\prime}} \).
    Thus, \( \alpha < n^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordering-of-natural-numbers}, it follows that \( \alpha \in n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that \( \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{x < n^{\prime}} \subseteq n^{\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, let \( \beta \in n^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordering-of-natural-numbers}, it follows that \( \beta < n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N} that
    \( n^{\prime} \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Therefore, \( \beta \in n^{\prime} \) necessarily implies \( \beta \in \naturalset \).
    In other words, \( \beta \in \naturalset \) and \( \beta < n^{\prime} \) are both true.
    Thus, \( \beta \in \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{x < n^{\prime}} \).
    Since \( \beta \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that \( n^{\prime} \subseteq \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{x < n^{\prime}} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) and \( \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{x < n^{\prime}} \) are subsets of each other, they must be
    equal sets by \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the theorem follows immediately from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-every-n-plus-one-is-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers-less-than-or-equal-to-n}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( n + 1 = \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset }{x \leq n} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Let \( \varphi(x) \) denote the following predicate.
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq x \leq n^{\prime}
    \end{equation*}
    The existence of the set \( \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \) is implied by \cref{meta-axiom:specification}.
    The goal, then, is to show that \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) and \( \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \) are in fact equal sets.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( x^{\prime} \in \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \).
    It follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-conditional-set-builder-notation} that \( x^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) and \( \varphi(x^{\prime}) \)
    are both true statements.
    Since \( \varphi(x^{\prime}) \) is true, it follows from the definition of \( \varphi \) that \( x^{\prime} \leq n^{\prime} \);
    i.e., either \( x^{\prime} < n^{\prime} \) or \( x^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \) is true.
    Since \( x^{\prime}, n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), the membership relation and \( < \) relation are synonymous by \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordering-of-natural-numbers}.
    In other words, either \( x^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \) or \( x^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \) is true, which in turn implies \( x^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \) and thus \( x^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \) implies \( x \in n^{\prime} + 1 \)}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \subseteq n^{\prime} + 1 \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, let \( y^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary element of \( n^{\prime} + 1 \); that is, either \( y^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \) or \( y^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N} that \( n^{\prime} \subset \naturalset \)
    and \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \subset \naturalset \).
    From this, we deduce that \( y^{\prime} \in \naturalset \).
    Since \( \in \) and \( < \) are synonymous for natural numbers,
    it follows that \( \varphi(y^{\prime}) \) is true and thus \( y^{\prime} \in \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \).
    We have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) implies \( x \in \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    That is, \( n^{\prime} + 1 \subseteq \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) and \( \setbuilder{x \in \naturalset}{\varphi(x)} \) are subsets of each other, they must be equal sets
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-there-is-nothing-in-between-a-natural-number-and-its-successor}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \), there does not exists an \( m \in \naturalset \) such that \( n \in m \) and \( m \in n + 1 \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that there exists an \( m^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) such that \( n^{\prime} \in m^{\prime} \) and \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    Since \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-successor} that either \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \) or \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \).
    If \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \), then \( n^{\prime} \notin m^{\prime} \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-asymmetry-of-membership-relation}.
    If \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \), then \( n^{\prime} \notin m^{\prime} \) is also implied by \cref{thm:metatheory-no-set-is-an-element-of-itself}.
    Both cases imply \( n^{\prime} \notin m^{\prime} \), a contradiction.
    Therefore, our supposition that such an \( m^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) exists cannot possibly be true.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n}
    For all \( n, m \in \naturalset \), \( m \in n \) implies either \( m + 1 = n \) or \( m + 1 \in n \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{for all \( m \in \naturalset \), \( m \in n \) implies either \( n = m + 1\) or \( m + 1 \in n \)}
    \end{equation*}
    We will prove by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Establishing the base case is trivial since \( P(\emptyset) \) is vacuously true; i.e., \( \emptyset \) does not contain any element.
    Now let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary natural number such that \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \); that is, either \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \)
    or \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Subcase} \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \):

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true and \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \), it follows that either \( n^{\prime} = m^{\prime} + 1 \) or \( m^{\prime} + 1 \in n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} \union \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \), it is clear that \( m^{\prime} + 1 \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) is true in both possibilities.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Subcase} \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \):

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) and \( n^{\prime} \) are equal sets, their successors must equal due to the uniqueness of unions.
    Therefore, \( m^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} + 1 \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    By \cref{meta-rule:constructive-dilemma}, we conclude that either \( m^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} + 1 \) or \( m^{\prime} + 1 \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) is true
    for all arbitrary \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is true.
    The inductive step follows immediately \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}[Linear Ordering]
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers}
    For all \( n, m \in \naturalset \), if \( n \neq m \), then either \( n \in m \) or \( m \in n \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq n \in \naturalset \text{ and for all \( m \in \naturalset \), \( n \neq m \) implies either \( n \in m \) or \( m \in n \)}
    \end{equation*}
    We will prove by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(\emptyset) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary natural number such that \( \emptyset \neq m^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-contains-the-empty-set}, it follows that \( \emptyset \in m^{\prime} \).
    Thus, \( P(\emptyset) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Obviously, \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \naturalset \) since \( \naturalset \) is \aref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}{inductive}.
    Let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( m^{\prime} \neq n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    Since \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true, there are three possibilities: \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \), \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \), or \( n^{\prime} \in m^{\prime} \).
    If \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \) or \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \), then necessarily
    \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) since \( n^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} \union \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \).
    On the other hand, if \( n^{\prime} \in m^{\prime} \), then either \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} + 1 \) or \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in  m^{\prime} \)
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n}.
    But \( m^{\prime} \neq n^{\prime} + 1 \) by assumption, so necessarily \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m^{\prime} \).
    In other words, we have proven the following statement:
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( m^{\prime} \neq n^{\prime} + 1 \), then either \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) or \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m^{\prime} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \), we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( m \in \naturalset \), \( m \neq n^{\prime} + 1 \) implies either \( m \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) or \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m \)}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is true.
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the inductive step follows readily from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-less-than-relation-of-natural-numbers-is-equivalent-to-proper-subset-relation}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \) and \( m \in \naturalset \), \( n < m \) iff \( n \subset m \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq n \in \naturalset \ \text{and for all \( m \in \naturalset \), \( n < m \) iff \( n \subset m \)}
    \end{equation*}
    
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(0) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( \emptyset < m^{\prime} \).
    Then \( \emptyset \in m^{\prime} \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-ordering-of-natural-numbers}.
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is a set, it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-empty-set-is-the-subset-of-every-set} that
    \( \emptyset \subseteq m^{\prime} \).
    Furthermore, \( \emptyset \) is an object that is an element of \( m^{\prime} \) but not an element of \( \emptyset \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( \emptyset \subset m^{\prime} \).
    Thus, we conclude that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{\( \emptyset < m^{\prime} \) implies \( \emptyset \subset m^{\prime} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Conversely, suppose that \( \emptyset \subset m^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that there exists an object \( \alpha \)
    such that \( \alpha \in m^{\prime} \) and \( \alpha \notin \emptyset \).
    From this we can infer that \( m^{\prime} \neq \emptyset \) because it contains at least one element.
    Since \( m^{\prime} \neq \emptyset \) and \( m^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), it follows from
    \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-contains-the-empty-set} that \( \emptyset \in m^{\prime} \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{\( \emptyset \subset m^{\prime} \) implies \( \emptyset \in m^{\prime} \)}
    \end{equation*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We have thus shown that \( \emptyset \in m^{\prime} \) and \( \emptyset \subset m^{\prime} \) imply each other.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \emptyset \in m^{\prime} \ \text{iff} \ \emptyset \subset m^{\prime}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude the truth of \( P(0) \) from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( m^{\prime} < \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \); that is, \( m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-successor}, it follows that either \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \) or \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \).
    If \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \), then necessarily \( m^{\prime} \subset n^{\prime} \) since \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( m^{\prime} \subseteq n^{\prime} \) and
    there exists a \( \beta \) such that \( \beta \in n^{\prime} \) and \( \beta \notin m^{\prime} \).
    It should also be obvious that \( m^{\prime} \subseteq \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) since every element of \( m^{\prime} \) is an
    element of \( n^{\prime} \) (and thus an element of \( n^{\prime} + 1 \)).
    But if \( \beta \in n^{\prime} \), then \( \beta \) must also belong to \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) according to \cref{dfn:metatheory-successor}.
    In other words, \( \beta \) is an element of \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) that does not belong to \( m^{\prime} \).
    Given that \( m^{\prime} \subseteq \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \), necessarily \( m^{\prime} \subset \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \)
    by \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}.
    Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        m^{\prime} < \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \ \text{implies} \ m^{\prime} \subset \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright)
    \end{equation*}
    Conversely, suppose that \( m^{\prime} \subset \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \); that is, \( m^{\prime} \subseteq \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \)
    and there exists a \( \gamma \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) such that \( \gamma \notin m^{\prime} \).
    Clearly, there are only two possibilities: either \( \gamma = m^{\prime} \) or \( \gamma \neq m^{\prime} \).
    If \( \gamma = m^{\prime} \), then necessarily \( m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) because \( \gamma \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \).
    If \( \gamma \neq m^{\prime} \), then it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers} that \( m^{\prime} \in \gamma \)
    because \( \gamma \) does not belong to \( m^{\prime} \).
    Moreover, \( \gamma \) is either \( n^{\prime} \) or an element of \( n^{\prime} \) (because \( \gamma \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \)).
    If \( \gamma = n^{\prime} \), then clearly \( m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) because \( m^{\prime} \in \gamma \).
    If \( \gamma \in n^{\prime} \), then necessarily \( \gamma \subset n^{\prime} \) because \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Since \( m^{\prime} \in \gamma \), it follows that \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \) and thus \( m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \).
    Evidently, \( m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) is necessarily true in all possible scenarios.
    Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        m^{\prime} \subset \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \ \text{implies} \ m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright)
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) and \( m^{\prime} \subset \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) imply each other, we conclude that
    \begin{equation*}
        m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \ \text{iff} \ m^{\prime} \subset \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright)
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is true;
    that is, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) implies \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) for any arbitrary \( n^{\prime} \).
    Applying \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} again yields the inductive step.
    Thus, \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \) by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
\end{proof}



\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-is-a-successor-of-another-natural-number}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \), if \( n \neq 0 \), then there exists an \( s \in \naturalset \) such that \( n = s + 1 \).
    In other words, every non-zero natural number is a successor of a natural number.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We begin by defining the predicate \( P(n) \) as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{if \( n \neq 0 \), then there exists an \( s \in \naturalset \) such that \( n = s \union \mleft\{ s \mright\} \) }
    \end{equation*}
    The goal is to prove by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \);
    that is, \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} = \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(0) \) is vacuously true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be some arbitrary object such that \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) and that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    By definition, \( n^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} \union \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) and \( n^{\prime} + 1 \neq \emptyset \), it follows that \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is true.
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) implies \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the inductive step follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Having established both the base case and the inductive step, we can conclude the theorem by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}[Well-Ordering]
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-well-ordering-of-natural-numbers}
    If \( S \) is a non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \), then \( S \) has a least element; that is,
    there exists an \( x \in S \) such that \( x \leq y \) for all \( y \in S \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that \( S \) has no least element.
    Now let \( T \) be the set defined as follows.
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-T}
        T \coloneq \setbuilderbig{x \in \naturalset}{\text{for all \( y \in S \), \( x < y \)}}
    \end{equation}
    We now establish the following claims.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:empty-set-does-not-belong-to-S}
        \( \emptyset \notin S \)
    \end{claim}

    \noindent
    Suppose that \( \emptyset \in S \).
    Then any element of \( S \) must either be equal to \( \emptyset \) or less than \( \emptyset \)
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-contains-the-empty-set}, which contradicts our initial supposition.
    Therefore, \( \emptyset \notin S \).

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:T-is-inductive}
        \( T \) is inductive.
    \end{claim}

    \noindent
    Let \( y^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( S \).
    Since \( \emptyset \notin S \) by \cref{claim:empty-set-does-not-belong-to-S}, it follows that \( y^{\prime} \neq \emptyset \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-contains-the-empty-set}, necessarily \( \emptyset < y^{\prime} \).
    Since \( y^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( y \in S \), \( \emptyset < y \)}
    \end{equation*}
    It follows from \eqref{eq:definition-of-T} that \( \emptyset \in T \).
    Now let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object such that \( x^{\prime} \in T \).
    By \eqref{eq:definition-of-T}, it follows that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:x-prime-less-than-all-elements-of-S}
        \text{for all \( y \in S \), \( x^{\prime} < y \)}
    \end{equation}
    Let \( y^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( S \).
    Applying \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to \eqref{eq:x-prime-less-than-all-elements-of-S} yields
    \( x^{\prime} < y^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n}, it follows that
    either \( x^{\prime} + 1 = y^{\prime} \) or \( x^{\prime} + 1 < y^{\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose that \( x^{\prime} + 1 = y^{\prime} \); this means that \( x^{\prime} + 1 \in S \).
    Let \( z^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( S \).
    By \eqref{eq:x-prime-less-than-all-elements-of-S}, it follows that \( x^{\prime} < z^{\prime} \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} + 1 \in S \) and \( z^{\prime} \in S \) , there are two cases to consider: \( z^{\prime} = x^{\prime} + 1 \) or \( z^{\prime} \neq x^{\prime} + 1 \).
    If \( z^{\prime} \neq x^{\prime} + 1 \), then it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers} that either
    \( z^{\prime} < x^{\prime} + 1 \) or \( x^{\prime} + 1 < z^{\prime} \).
    But \( z^{\prime} < x^{\prime} + 1 \) is impossible because it implies \( x^{\prime} < z^{\prime} < x^{\prime} + 1 \),
    which contradicts \cref{thm:metatheory-there-is-nothing-in-between-a-natural-number-and-its-successor}.
    Therefore, either \( z^{\prime} = x^{\prime} + 1 \) or \( x^{\prime} + 1 < z^{\prime} \).
    Since \( z^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows that \( x^{\prime} + 1 \leq z \) for all \( z \in S \). 
    Since \( x^{\prime} + 1 \in S \), it follows that there exists an element of \( S \) that is less than or equal to all elements of \( S \), which contradicts
    our initial hypothesis that no such element exists.
    In other words, \( x^{\prime} + 1 = y^{\prime} \) cannot possibly be true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( x^{\prime} + 1 = y^{\prime} \) and \( x^{\prime} + 1 < y^{\prime} \) are the only two possibilities, we conclude that
    \( x^{\prime} + 1 < y^{\prime} \).
    Given that \( y^{\prime} \) is an arbitrary element of \( S \), we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( y \in S \), \( x^{\prime} + 1 < y \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \eqref{eq:definition-of-T}, it follows that \( x^{\prime} + 1 \in T \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we can apply \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} once again to conclude
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in T \) implies \( x + 1 \in T \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}, \( T \) is necessarily an inductive set.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:T-is-the-subset-of-N-minus-S}
        \( T \subseteq \mleft( \naturalset - S \mright) \)
    \end{claim}

    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \in T \).
    Clearly, \( x^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) by \eqref{eq:definition-of-T}.
    If \( x^{\prime} \) also belongs to \( S \), then \eqref{eq:definition-of-T} would require that \( x^{\prime} \in x^{\prime} \),
    which contradicts \cref{thm:metatheory-no-set-is-an-element-of-itself}.
    Thus, \( x^{\prime} \notin S \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} \in T \) implies \( x^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) and \( x^{\prime} \notin S \), it follows that
    \( x^{\prime} \in \mleft( \naturalset - S \mright) \). In other words, \( x \in T \) implies \( x \in \mleft( \naturalset - S \mright) \)
    for all \( x \). 
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( T \subseteq \mleft( \naturalset - S \mright) \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( T \) is inductive, necessarily \( \naturalset \subseteq T \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-natural-numbers-is-a-subset-of-any-inductive-set}.
    Additionally, we know from \eqref{eq:definition-of-T} that \( T \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Given that \( \naturalset \) and \( T \) are subsets of each other, they must be equal by \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.
    
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary object.
    By \cref{meta-rule:law-of-excluded-middle}, either \( x^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) or \( x^{\prime} \notin \naturalset \) is true.
    If \( x^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), then \( x^{\prime} \in T \) since \( T = \naturalset \).
    But \( T \subseteq \mleft( \naturalset - S \mright) \) by \cref{claim:T-is-the-subset-of-N-minus-S}, so necessarily \( x^{\prime} \notin S \).
    If \( x^{\prime} \notin \naturalset \), then \( x^{\prime} \notin S \) must follow from \cref{meta-rule:modus-tollens}
    since \( S \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Thus, \( x^{\prime} \notin S \) is true in both cases.
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows that \( x \notin S \) for all \( x \).
    In other words, \( S = \emptyset \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \text{if \( S \) has no least element, then \( S = \emptyset \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( S \neq \emptyset \) by assumption, the supposition that \( S \) has no least element cannot be true.
    In other words, \( S \) must have a least element.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-every-non-empty-subset-of-N-that-is-bounded-from-above-has-a-greatest-element}
    Every non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \) that is bounded from above has a greatest element.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) be any non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \). Let \( U \) be the set of all upper bounds of \( S \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:U-is-the-set-of-all-upper-bounds-of-S}
        U = \setbuilderbig{x \in \naturalset}{\text{for all \( z \in S \), \( z \leq x \)}}
    \end{equation}
    Clearly, \( U \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Since \( S \) has an upper bound, it follows that \( U \neq \emptyset \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-well-ordering-of-natural-numbers}, \( U \) must have a least element.
    Let \( \lambda \) be the least element of \( U \).
    We will now show that \( \lambda \) is necessarily an element of \( S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose that \( \lambda \notin S \).
    Then necessarily \( \lambda \neq \emptyset \); otherwise, the fact that \( \lambda \) is an upper bound of \( S \) and \( S \) cannot be empty
    would imply that \( S = \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \), which in turn implies that \( \emptyset \in S \) and thus \( \lambda \in S \), a contradiction.
    Since \( \lambda \in \naturalset \) and \( \lambda \neq \emptyset \),
    it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-is-a-successor-of-another-natural-number}
    that \( \lambda = k + 1 \) for some \( k \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( S \).
    Since \( \lambda \) is an upper bound of \( S \) and since no natural number exists in between
    \( k \) and \( \lambda = k + 1 \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-there-is-nothing-in-between-a-natural-number-and-its-successor},
    we deduce the following inequality:
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:k-is-sandwiched-between-x-prime-and-lambda}
        x^{\prime} \leq k < \lambda
    \end{equation}
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \in S \), \( x \leq k \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \eqref{eq:U-is-the-set-of-all-upper-bounds-of-S}, it follows that \( k \in U \).
    But if \( k \) is an element of \( U \), then \( \lambda \) cannot possibly be the least element of \( U \) since \( k < \lambda \) by
    \eqref{eq:k-is-sandwiched-between-x-prime-and-lambda}.
    In other words, the statement \( \lambda \notin S \) leads to a contradiction and must therefore be false.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \lambda \in S
    \end{equation*}
    We can then conclude from \cref{dfn:metatheory-greatest-element-of-a-non-empty-subset-of-N,dfn:metatheory-upper-bound-of-a-subset-of-N} that
    \( \lambda \) is the greatest element of \( S \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}[Generalized Weak Induction]
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-generalized-weak-induction}
    Let \( k \) be an element of \( \naturalset \) and \( P(n) \) be a predicate such that the following conditions hold:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( P(k) \) is true
        \item for all \( n \geq k \), \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \)
    \end{enumerate}
    Then \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{\text{\( n \geq k \) and \( P(n) \)}} = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \geq k} \);
    that is, \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \geq k \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n < k} \) and \( T = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \geq k \ \text{and} \ P(n)} \).
    We first show that \( \emptyset \in S \,{\union}\, T \).
    To do so, we must consider two cases: \( k = 0 \) or \( k \neq 0 \).
    If \( k = 0\), then necessarily \( \emptyset \in T \) because \( \emptyset \geq k \) and \( P(\emptyset) \) are both true.
    If \( k \neq 0 \), then \( \emptyset \in S \) because \( \emptyset \) is less than any non-zero natural number by
    \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-contains-the-empty-set}.
    In either case, \( \emptyset \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) is implied.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \emptyset \in \mleft( S \union T \mright)
    \end{equation*}
    \noindent
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in \mleft( S \union T \mright) \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, it follows that either \( \alpha \in S \) or \( \alpha \in T \).
    If \( \alpha \in S \), then \( \alpha \in \naturalset \) and \( \alpha < k \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n}, it follows that
    either \( \alpha + 1 = k \) or \( \alpha + 1 < k \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \alpha + 1 = k \), then \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in T \) follows because \( P(\alpha + 1) \) and \( \alpha + 1 \geq k \) are both true.
    Since \( \alpha + 1 \in T \), \( \alpha + 1 \) must also belong to \( S \union T \).
    On the other hand, if \( \alpha + 1 < k \), then \( \alpha + 1 \) must belong to \( S \) by definition.
    Thus, \( \alpha + 1 \) must belong to \( S \union T \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \text{if \( \alpha \in S \), then \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \union T \mright) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    If \( \alpha \in T \), then it follows from the definition of \( T \) that \( \alpha \geq k \) and \( P(\alpha) \) are both true.
    Applying \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to the second condition of the theorem yields
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( \alpha \geq k \), then \( P(\alpha) \) implies \( P(\alpha + 1) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \alpha \geq k \) and \( P(\alpha) \) are both true, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens} that \( P(\alpha + 1) \) is true.
    Moreover, \( \alpha \geq k \) also implies that \( \alpha + 1 > k \).
    By the definition of \( T \), it follows that \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in T \) and thus \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \text{if \( \alpha \in T \), then \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \union T \mright) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{meta-rule:disjunction-elimination}, it follows that \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) must be true.
    In other words, \( \alpha \in \mleft( S \union T \mright) \) implies \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \mleft( S \union T \mright) \) implies \( \mleft( x + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \union T \mright) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Given that \( \emptyset \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set} that \( S \,{\union}\, T \) is inductive.
    By \cref{thm:the-set-of-natural-numbers-is-a-subset-of-any-inductive-set}, we conclude that \( \naturalset \subseteq \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \).
    But \( S \,{\union}\, T \) is clearly also a subset of \( \naturalset \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \naturalset = \mleft( S \union T \mright)
    \end{equation*}
    Next, we note the fact that \( \naturalset - S = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \geq k} \) as a consequence of
    \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers}.
    But \( \naturalset = \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) as previously shown.
    Thus,
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:S-union-T-minus-S-equals-all-numbers-geq-k}
        \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) - S = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \geq k}
    \end{equation}
    Let \( \beta \) be an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \beta \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) - S \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-difference-of-two-sets}, it follows that \( \beta \in \mleft( S \union T \mright) \) and \( \beta \notin S \) are both true, which
    is only possible if \( \beta \in T \). Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{ if  \( \beta \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \), then \( \beta \in T \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Conversely, suppose that \( \beta \in T \).
    Clearly, \( \beta \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}.
    But \( S \) and \( T \) are disjoint due to \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers}, so \( \beta \in T \) must imply
    \( \beta \notin S \). In other words, \( \beta \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) and \( \beta \notin S \) are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-difference-of-two-sets}, \( \beta \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) - S \).
    Thus,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( \beta \in T \), then \( \beta \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) - S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \beta \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) and \( \beta \in T \) imply each other, we conclude that
    \begin{equation*}
        \beta \in \mleft( S \union T \mright) \ \text{iff} \ \beta \in T
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \beta \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) - S \) iff \( x \in T \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{meta-axiom:extensionality}, it follows that \( \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) - S = T \).
    Substituting into \eqref{eq:S-union-T-minus-S-equals-all-numbers-geq-k} then yields
    \( T = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \geq k \ \text{and} \ P(n)} = \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) - S = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \geq k} \)
    as needed.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}[Finite Induction]
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-finite-induction}
    Let \( k \) be an element of \( \naturalset \) and \( P(n) \)
    be a predicate that meets the following criteria:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( P(0) \) is true
        \item For all \( n < k \), \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \)
    \end{enumerate}
    Then \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \leq k \ \text{and} \ P(n)} = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \leq k} \);
    that is, \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \leq k \) (or, equivalently, for all \( n \in k + 1 \)).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{k < n} \) and \( T = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \leq k \ \text{and} \ P(n)} \).
    Observe, first and foremost, that \( \emptyset \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) since \( P(0) \) and \( \emptyset \leq k \) are both
    true regardless of which natural number \( k \) denotes.
    Now let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) is true.
    Then by \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, we have \( \alpha \in S \) or \( \alpha \in T \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \alpha \in S \), then it follows from the definition of \( S \) that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \) and \( k < \alpha \).
    But \( \alpha \in \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-successor}, so we have \( k < \alpha < \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright)  \).
    In other words, \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in S  \) and thus \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \alpha \in T \), then we infer from the definition of \( T \) that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \), \( \alpha \leq k \), and \( P(\alpha) \) are true statements.
    If \( \alpha < k \), then \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \leq k \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n}.
    Moreover, since \( \alpha < k \) and \( P(\alpha) \) are both true, the second criterion of \( P(n) \) requires \( P(\alpha + 1) \) to be true also.
    Since \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \naturalset \), \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \leq k \), and \( P(\alpha + 1) \) are all true, necessarily
    \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in T \) and \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \).
    If \( \alpha = k \) instead, then clearly \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) > k \), which means that \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in S \) and
    \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We have shown that \( \mleft( \alpha + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) is implied regardless of whether \( \alpha \in S \) or \( \alpha \in T \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) implies \( \mleft( x + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \emptyset \in \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) has also been shown to be true, it follows that \( S \,{\union}\, T \) is an inductive set.
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-natural-numbers-is-a-subset-of-any-inductive-set}, it follows that \( \naturalset \subseteq \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \).
    But \( \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \) is also a subset of \( \naturalset \) because both \( S \) and \( T \) are subsets of \( \naturalset \).
    This means that \( \naturalset \) and \( S \,{\union}\, T \) are subsets of each other and are thus equal sets by \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.
    \begin{equation*}
        \naturalset = \mleft( S \union T \mright)
    \end{equation*}
    Observe that \( \naturalset - S = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{n \leq k} \) due to \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers}.
    Since \( \naturalset = \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) \), it follows that \( \naturalset - S = \mleft( S \,{\union}\, T \mright) - S = T \)
    as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-union-of-N-is-N-itself}
    \( \Union \naturalset = \naturalset \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \) and \( \naturalset \) is inductive, it follows that \( \emptyset \union \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \) is also
    an element of \( \naturalset \).
    This means that there exists an element of \( \naturalset \) that contains \( \emptyset \) and thus \( \emptyset \in \Union \naturalset \)
    by \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \Union \naturalset \).
    Again, \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets} implies that there exists an \( m^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) such that
    \( n^{\prime} \in m^{\prime} \).
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is a natural number, it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N} that
    \( m^{\prime} \subseteq \naturalset \). This means that every element of \( m^{\prime} \) also belongs to \( \naturalset \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in m^{\prime} \), it follows that \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) and \( m^{\prime} \) are both natural numbers and \( n^{\prime} \in m^{\prime} \),
    \cref{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n} applies and 
    either \( n^{\prime} + 1 = m^{\prime} \) or \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m^{\prime} \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( n^{\prime} + 1 = m^{\prime} \), then obviously \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \Union \naturalset \) because \( m^{\prime} \) is a natural number that contains
    \( n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    If \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m^{\prime} \), then \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \mleft( m^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-successor}.
    Since \( m^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) and \( \naturalset \) is inductive, necessarily \( m^{\prime} + 1 \in \naturalset \).
    Once again, there exists a natural number that contains \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) as an element.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    In all possible cases, \( n^{\prime} \in \Union \naturalset \) implies \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \Union \naturalset \).
    From this we conclude that \( \Union \naturalset \) is an inductive set.
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-natural-numbers-is-a-subset-of-any-inductive-set}, it follows that \( \naturalset \subseteq \Union \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \Union \naturalset \).
    Thus, there exists an \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) such that \( x^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N} that
    \( n^{\prime} \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \), it follows that \( x^{\prime} \in \naturalset \).
    In other words, every element of \( \Union \naturalset \) is an element of \( \naturalset \); i.e., \( \Union \naturalset \subseteq \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( \naturalset \) and \( \Union \naturalset \) are subsets of each other, they must be equal sets by \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Recursion}

\begin{recursion-theorem}[Standard Form]
    \phantomsection \label{recursion-theorem:standard-form}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty set.
    If \( g \) is a function such that \( \function{g}{S \times \naturalset}{S} \) and \( \sigma \) is an element of \( S \),
    then there exists a unique function \( \function{f}{\naturalset}{S} \) such that:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( f(0) = \sigma \)
        \item for all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( f(n + 1) = g(f(n), n) \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{recursion-theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \omega(i, j, y) \), \( \theta(i, y) \), and \( \psi(n, y) \) be sequences of two predicates such that:
    \begin{alignat*}{2}
        &\omega_{0}(i, j) &&\coloneq i = j \union \mleft\{ j \mright\}\\
        &\omega_{1}(i, j, y) &&\coloneq \text{there exists a \( z \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{j}{z} \in y \) and
        \( \orderedpair{i}{g(z, j)} \in y \)}\\
        &\theta_{0}(i, y) &&\coloneq i = 0 \ \text{and} \ \orderedpair{i}{\sigma} \in y\\
        &\theta_{1}(i, y) &&\coloneq \text{there exists a \( j \in \naturalset \) such that \( \conjunction{\omega(i, j, y)} \) is a true statement}\\
        &\psi_{0}(n, y) &&\coloneq n \in \naturalset \ \text{and} \ \function{y}{n}{S}\\
        &\psi_{1}(n, y) &&\coloneq \text{for all \( i \in n \), \( \disjunction{\theta(i, y)} \) is a true statement}
    \end{alignat*}
    Our first task is to show that the set \( T = \setbuilder{\orderedpair{n}{y}}{\conjunction{\psi(n, y)}} \) exists.
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) and \( y^{\prime} \) denote arbitrary objects in the universe.
    Suppose that \( \conjunction{\psi(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) is a true statement.
    Thus, \( \psi_{0}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is necessarily true; that is, \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) and \( \function{y^{\prime}}{n}{S} \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-function-is-subset-of-cartesian-product}, it follows that \( y^{\prime} \subseteq n \times S \);
    and since \( n \subseteq \naturalset \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N} and \( S \) is a non-empty set,
    necessarily \( y^{\prime} \subseteq  n \times S \subseteq \naturalset \times S \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-subsets-form-cartesian-product-subsets}.
    By \cref{meta-axiom:power-set}, \( y^{\prime} \in \powerset{\naturalset \times S} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) and \( y^{\prime} \) are arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( n \) and \( y \), \( \conjunction{\psi(n, y)} \) implies \( n \in \naturalset \) and \( y \in \powerset{\naturalset \times S} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, \( T = \setbuilder{\orderedpair{n}{y}}{\conjunction{\psi(n, y)}} \) exists by \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-ordered-pairs-satisfying-binary-predicates}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, we prove that \( \function{T}{\naturalset}{\finiteseq{S}} \).
    To accomplish this, we first define the predicate \( P(n) \) as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{there exists a unique \( y \in \finiteseq{S} \) such that \( \orderedpair{n}{y} \in T \)}
    \end{equation*}
    If we can prove by induction that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \), then we would've proven that \( \function{T}{\naturalset}{\finiteseq{S}} \).
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    We now state the following hypothesis.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Induction Hypothesis}: for all \( i < n^{\prime} \), \( P(i) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    The goal, then, is to prove that the induction hypothesis implies \( P(n^{\prime}) \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), it follows that either \( n^{\prime} = \emptyset \) or \( n^{\prime} \neq \emptyset \).
    We must show that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true in either case.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose that \( n^{\prime} = \emptyset \).
    Let \( y^{\prime} = \emptyset \).
    It should be obvious that \( \psi_{0}(\emptyset, \emptyset) \) is true because \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \) and
    \( \function{\emptyset}{\emptyset}{S} \) are both true.
    Moreover, \( \psi_{1}(\emptyset, \emptyset) \) is also vacuously true because \( \emptyset \) has no elements.
    In other words, \( \conjunction{\psi(\emptyset, \emptyset)} \) is true and thus \( \orderedpair{\emptyset}{\emptyset} \in T \).
    Next, we show that \( y^{\prime} \) is unique; that is, if \( \orderedpair{\emptyset}{y^{\prime}} \) and \( \orderedpair{\emptyset}{z^{\prime}} \)
    are both elements of \( T \), then necessarily \( y^{\prime} = z^{\prime} \).
    This is fairly obvious because both \( y^{\prime} \) and \( z^{\prime} \) must be empty for \( \function{y^{\prime}}{\emptyset}{S} \)
    and \( \function{z^{\prime}}{\emptyset}{S} \) to be true.
    We have thus showed that if \( n^{\prime} = \emptyset \), then there exists a unique \( y \in \finiteseq{S} \) such that
    \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y} \in T \).
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose that \( n^{\prime} \neq \emptyset \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) and is non-empty, it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-is-a-successor-of-another-natural-number}
    that there exists some \( k \in \naturalset \) such that \( n^{\prime} = k + 1 = k \union \mleft\{ k \mright\} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, it follows that \( k \in n^{\prime} \) and thus \( k < n^{\prime} \).
    Therefore, \( P(k) \) is a true by the induction hypothesis.
    This means that there exists a unique \( y^{k} \in \finiteseq{S} \) such that \( \orderedpair{k}{y^{k}} \in T \).
    Since \( k \in \naturalset \), either \( k = \emptyset \) or \( k \neq \emptyset \).
    Now let \( y^{\prime} = y^{k} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{k}{v} \mright\} \) where
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:possible-options-for-v}
        v =
        \begin{cases}
            \sigma &\text{if} \ k = \emptyset\\
            g(y^{k}(j), j) &\text{if} \ k \neq \emptyset \ \text{and} \ k = j + 1
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    We must then show that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in T \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Subcase:} \( k = \emptyset \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( \orderedpair{k}{y^{k}} \in T \), \( \psi_{0}(k, y^{k}) \) must be a true statement.
    Thus, \( y^{k} \) is necessarily \( \emptyset \).
    By \eqref{eq:possible-options-for-v}, it follows that \( y^{\prime} = \emptyset \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{k}{\sigma} \mright\} \).
    We will now show that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in T \) by demonstrating that \( \psi_{0}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \)
    and \( \psi_{1}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) are both true.
    Since \( \sigma \in S \) and
    \( n^{\prime} = k \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ k \mright\} = \emptyset \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} = \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} = \mleft\{ k \mright\} \),
    it is obvious that \( \function{y^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}{S} \).
    Thus, \( \psi_{0}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is indeed true.
    Let \( i^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object such that \( i^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is the singleton \( \mleft\{ k \mright\} \), it follows that \( i^{\prime} = k = \emptyset \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{\sigma} = \orderedpair{k}{\sigma} \in y^{\prime} \), it follows that \( \theta_{0}(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is true.
    This means that \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) is true.
    Given that \( i^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we can infer the truth of \( \psi_{1}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
    Therefore, \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in T \).
