    To prove uniquness, we must show that for all \( z \), \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{z} \in T \) implies \( z = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{k}{\sigma} \mright\} \).
    Let \( z^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary object such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in T \).
    This means that \( \psi_{0}(n^{\prime}, z^{\prime}) \) is true and thus \( \function{z^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}{S} \).
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object such that \( p \in z^{\prime} \).
    Since \( z^{\prime} \) is a function, \( p \) must be an ordered pair.
    Let \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \).
    Clearly, \( a \in \dom z^{\prime} \).
    Since \( \dom z^{\prime} = n^{\prime} = \mleft\{ k \mright\} \), necessarily \( a = k = \emptyset = 0 \).
    Moreover, \( \disjunction{\theta(\emptyset, z^{\prime})} \) must be true since \( \emptyset \in n^{\prime} \).
    But \( \theta_{1}(\emptyset, z^{\prime}) \) can never be true because \( \emptyset \) is not the successor of any element of \( \naturalset \).
    For \( \disjunction{\theta(\emptyset, z^{\prime})} \) to still be true, \( \theta_{0}(\emptyset, z^{\prime}) \) must therefore be true.
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \in z^{\prime} \).
    Since \( z^{\prime} \) is a function with \( \orderedpair{0}{b} \in z^{\prime} \) and \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \in z^{\prime} \) both being true,
    \( b \) and \( \sigma \) must be equal.
    Thus, \( p = \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \).
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in z^{\prime} \) implies \( x = \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( z^{\prime} \neq \emptyset \), \cref{thm:metatheory-sufficient-conditions-for-singleton} applies and we conclude that
    \( z^{\prime} = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \), which completes the proof for uniqueness.
    Thus, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Subcase:} \( k \neq \emptyset \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( k \in \naturalset \) and \( k \neq \emptyset \), there must exists a \( j \in \naturalset \) such that \( k = j + 1 \).
    By \eqref{eq:possible-options-for-v}, we thus have \( y^{\prime} = y^{k} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{k}{g(y^{k}(j), j)} \mright\} \).
    Again, we must show that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in T \).
    We begin by proving that \( y^{\prime} \) is a function.
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary element of \( y^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-union-of-sets}, it follows that either \( p \in y^{k} \) or \( p = \orderedpair{k}{g(y^{k}(j), j)} \).
    If \( p \in y^{k} \), then \( p \) must be an ordered pair because \( \orderedpair{k}{y^{k}} \in T \) due to the induction hypothesis
    and thus \( \function{y^{k}}{k}{S} \).
    If \( p \in \mleft\{ \orderedpair{k}{g(y^{k}(j), j)} \mright\} \), then \( p = \orderedpair{k}{g(y^{k}(j), j)}\), obviously an ordered pair.
    We have thus proven that \( y^{\prime} \) is a set of ordered pairs.
    Now suppose that \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in y^{\prime} \) and \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in y{\prime} \) for any objects \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \).
    Once again, either \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in y^{k} \) or \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{k}{g(y^{k}(j), j)} \) is true.
    If \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in y^{k} \), then \( a \neq k \) since \( a \in \dom y^{k} \) and \( k \notin \dom y^{k} \).
    Since \( a \neq k \), it follows that \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in y^{k} \) is also true.
    Since \( y^{k} \) is a function, necessarily \( b = c \).
    On the other hand, if \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{k}{g(y^{k}(j), j)} \), then necessarily by
    \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-of-ordered-pairs}.
    But if \( a = k \), then it is impossible for \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \) to belong to \( y^{k} \) because \( k \notin \dom y^{k} \).
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{a}{b} = \orderedpair{k}{g(y^{k}(j), j)} = \orderedpair{a}{c} \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-of-ordered-pairs}, \( a \) and \( c \) must be the same object.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \text{for all \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \): \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in y^{\prime} \) and \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in y^{\prime} \) implies \( b = c \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}, \( y^{\prime} \) must be a function.
    Furthermore, \( \dom y^{\prime} = \dom y^{k} \union \mleft\{ k \mright\} = k \union \mleft\{ k \mright\} = n^{\prime} \).
    Also, \( \ran y^{\prime} \subseteq S \) because \( \ran g \subseteq S \).
    In other words, \( \function{y^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}{S} \) and thus \( \psi_{0}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is true.
    
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, we proceed to prove that \( \psi_{1}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is also true.
    Let \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{a} \) be an arbitrary ordered pair in \( y^{\prime} \).
    Since \( i^{\prime} = \dom y^{\prime} = n^{\prime} = k \union \mleft\{ k \mright\} \), it follows that either \( i^{\prime} \in k \) or \( i^{\prime} = k \).
    Observe also that
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:overlap-in-y-prime-and-y-k}
        y^{\prime}(i^{\prime}) =
        \begin{cases}
            y^{k}(i^{\prime}) \ &\text{if} \ i^{\prime} \in k\\
            g(y^{k}(j), j)  \ &\text{if} \ i^{\prime} = k
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    If \( i^{\prime} \in k \), then it follows from the induction hypothesis that \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, y^{k})} \) is true, which means that
    either \( \theta_{0}(i^{\prime}, y^{k}) \) or \( \theta_{1}(i^{\prime}, y^{k}) \).
    If \( \theta_{0}(i^{\prime}, y^{k}) \), then necessarily \( i^{\prime} = 0 \), \( a = \sigma \), and \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \in y^{k} \).
    By the definition of \( y^{\prime} \), it follows that \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{\sigma} \in y^{\prime} \) and thus \( \theta_{0}(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \)
    and \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) are both true.
    If \( \theta_{1}(i^{\prime}, y^{k}) \), then \( i^{\prime} = j^{\prime} + 1 \) for some \( j^{\prime} \) and \( \orderedpair{j^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in y^{k} \)
    and \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{g(z^{\prime}, j^{\prime})} \in y^{k} \) for some \( z^{\prime} \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{j^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in y^{k} \) and \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{g(z^{\prime}, j^{\prime})} \in y^{k} \)
    then \( \orderedpair{j^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \) and \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{g(z^{\prime}, j^{\prime})} \) must both also belong to \( y^{\prime} \) by the definition
    of \( y^{\prime} \).
    This means that \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( i^{\prime} = k \), then we know that \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{g(y^{k}(j), j)} \in y^{\prime} \).
    Since \( k = j + 1 \), it follows that \( j \in k \). Thus, \( y^{k}(j) = y^{\prime}(j) \) by \eqref{eq:overlap-in-y-prime-and-y-k}.
    Moreover, \( \orderedpair{j}{y^{k}(j)} \in y^{k} \) and thus \( \orderedpair{j}{y^{k}(j)} \in y^{\prime} \) because \( j \in k \).
    In other words, \( \omega_{0}(i^{\prime}, j) \) and \( \omega_{1}(i^{\prime}, j) \) are both true.
    Once again, this implies that \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) is implied in all possible cases and \( i^{\prime} \) is an arbitrary element of \( n^{\prime} \),
    it follows that \( \psi_{1}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is true. Since both \( \psi_{0}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) and \( \psi_{1}(n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \)
    are true, it follows that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in T \).
    In other words, for the subcase where \( k \neq \emptyset \), there exists a \( y \in \finiteseq{S} \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y} \in T \).
    Now we must show that such a \( y \) is unique.
    Let \( y^{\prime} \) and \( y^{\prime\prime} \) be two elements of \( \finiteseq{S} \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in T \)
    and \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \in T \).
    Let \( i^{\prime} \in k \) be arbitrary.
    Since \( k \subseteq n^{\prime} \), it follows that \( i^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \) and \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \) both belong to \( T \), it follows that
    \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) and \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, y^{\prime\prime})} \) are both true.
    It can be shown that in all possible cases, \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, \restrictedfunctionsmall{y^{\prime}}{k} )} \)
    and \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, \restrictedfunctionsmall{y^{\prime\prime}}{k})} \) are both true;
    that is, \( \orderedpair{k}{ \restrictedfunctionsmall{y^{\prime}}{k} } \in T \) and \( \orderedpair{k}{ \restrictedfunctionsmall{y^{\prime\prime}}{k} } \in T \).
    Since \( k < n^{\prime} \), the induction hypothesis guarantees uniqueness;
    that is, \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{y^{\prime}}{k} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{y^{\prime\prime}}{k} \).
    Furthermore, \( y^{\prime}(k) = g(y^{\prime}(j), j) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{y^{\prime}}{k}(j), j) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{y^{\prime\prime}}{k}(j), j )
    = g( y^{\prime\prime}(j), j ) = y^{\prime\prime}(k) \).
    Therefore, \( y^{\prime} \) and \( y^{\prime\prime} \) are equal functions, which establishes uniqueness.
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true for the subcase \( k \neq \emptyset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since the \( P(n^{\prime}) \) follows from the induction hypothesis in all possible cases, we conclude by \aref{thm:metatheory-strong-induction}{Strong Induction}
    that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \); that is, \( \function{T}{\naturalset}{\finiteseq{S}} \).
    Next, we prove the following useful claims.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:for-all-m-n-Tm-subset-of-Ts-if-m-less-than-n}
        For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( T_{m} \subseteq T_{n} \) for all \( m \in n \).
    \end{claim}

    \noindent
    We proceed with an inductive proof by defining the predicate \( P(n) \) as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{\( n \in \naturalset \) and \( T_{m} \subseteq T_{n} \) for all \( m \in n \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \). We now state the following induction hypothesis.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Induction Hypothesis}: \( P(i) \) is true for all \( i < n^{\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    The goal is to show that the induction hypothesis implies \( P(n^{\prime}) \). Once again, we consider two separate cases: \( n^{\prime} = 0 \)
    or \( n^{\prime} \neq 0 \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Subcase}: \( n^{\prime} = 0 \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is trivially true because no element of \( \naturalset \) is less than \( 0 \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Subcase}: \( n^{\prime} \neq 0 \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-is-a-successor-of-another-natural-number}, there exists a \( k \in \naturalset \) such that
    \( n^{\prime} = k + 1 = k \union \mleft\{ k \mright\} \).
    The foremost task is to show that \( \orderedpair{k}{ \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} } \in T \).
    For this, it suffices to prove that \( \psi_{1}(k, \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} ) \) is true.
    Since \( k \in \naturalset \), either \( k = 0 \) or \( k \neq 0 \). If \( k = 0 \),  then 
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{\emptyset} = \emptyset \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{\emptyset}{\emptyset} \in T \), it follows that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} \in T \) as needed.
    For \( k \neq 0 \), let \( i^{\prime} \in k \) be arbitrary. Since \( i^{\prime} \in k \) and \( n^{\prime} = k + 1 \), it follows that
    \( i^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( \psi_{1}(n^{\prime}, T_{n^{\prime}}) \) is true, it follows that \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, T_{n^{\prime}})} \) is true.
    If \( i^{\prime} = 0 \), necessarily \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{\sigma} \in T_{n^{\prime}} \).
    But \( i^{\prime} \in k \), so this must mean that \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{\sigma} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} \)
    and thus \( \theta_{0}(i^{\prime}, \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} ) \) is true.
    On the other hand, if \( i^{\prime} \neq 0 \), then \( \theta_{1}(i^{\prime}, T_{n^{\prime}}) \) must be true; that is, there exists a
    \( j^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) such that \( i^{\prime} = j^{\prime} + 1 \) and \( \orderedpair{j^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in T_{n^{\prime}} \)
    and \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{g(z^{\prime}, j^{\prime})} \in T_{n^{\prime}} \) for some \( z^{\prime} \in S \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{j^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in T_{n^{\prime}} \), then necessarily
    \(\orderedpair{j^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} \) because \( j^{\prime} \in i^{\prime} \in k \).
    By the same line of reasoning, we can infer that \( \orderedpair{i^{\prime}}{g(z^{\prime}, j^{\prime})} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} \).
    In other words, \( \theta_{1}(i^{\prime}, \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k}) \) is true.
    Hence, it has been shown that \( \disjunction{\theta(i^{\prime}, \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k})} \) is true for any arbitrary 
    \( i^{\prime} \in k \).
    In other words, \( \psi_{1}(k, \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k}) \) is true.
    Thus,
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:Tk-is-subset-of-Tn-prime}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{(T_{n^{\prime}})}{k} = T_{k} \subseteq T_{n^{\prime}}
    \end{equation}
    Now let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( n^{\prime} \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} = k \union \mleft\{ k \mright\} \), either \( m^{\prime} \in k \) or \( m^{\prime} = k \).
    If \( m^{\prime} \in k \), then \( T_{m^{\prime}} \subseteq T_{k} \subseteq T_{n^{\prime}} \) by the induction hypothesis
    and \eqref{eq:Tk-is-subset-of-Tn-prime}.
    If \( m^{\prime} = k \), then clearly \( T_{m^{\prime}} \subseteq T_{n^{\prime}} \) by \eqref{eq:Tk-is-subset-of-Tn-prime}.
    Thus, \( T_{m^{\prime}} \subseteq T_{n^{\prime}} \) is true in either case.
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( m \in n^{\prime} \), \( T_{m} \subseteq T_{n^{\prime}} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    \cref{claim:for-all-m-n-Tm-subset-of-Ts-if-m-less-than-n} follows readily from \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:the-values-of-all-Tn}
        For all \( n \in \naturalset \), if \( n \neq 0 \), then
        \begin{equation}
            \phantomsection \label{eq:the-values-of-Tn-at-i}
            T_{n}(i) =
            \begin{cases}
                \sigma \ &\text{if} \ i = 0 \\
                g(T_{n}(j), j) \ &\text{if} \ i \neq 0 \ \text{where} \ i = j + 1
            \end{cases}
        \end{equation}
        for all \( i \in n \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Proving \cref{claim:the-values-of-all-Tn} once again requires \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) be defined as follows.

    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{for all \( i \in n \), \( T_{n}(i) \) has the value defined by \eqref{eq:the-values-of-Tn-at-i}}
    \end{equation*}
    
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(\emptyset) \) is vacuously true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Observe that \( T_{n^{\prime}} \subseteq T_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) since \( n^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \) (see \cref{claim:for-all-m-n-Tm-subset-of-Ts-if-m-less-than-n}).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-a-subset-of-another-function-then-the-two-functions-are-compatible}, the two functions \( T_{n^{\prime}} \)
    and \( T_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) are therefore compatible.
    In other words,
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:obeys-the-required-condition-for-all-i-less-than-n-prime}
        \text{for all \( i \in n^{\prime} \), \( T_{n^{\prime}}(i) = T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(i) \)}
    \end{equation}
    Since \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true, then it follows that \( T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(i) \) is defined by \eqref{eq:the-values-of-Tn-at-i} for all \( i \in n^{\prime} \).
    It remains to show that \( T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) \) is also defined according to \eqref{eq:the-values-of-Tn-at-i}.
    Clearly, \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime} + 1}{T_{n^{\prime} + 1}} \in T \); this means that
    \( \psi_{1}(n^{\prime} + 1, T_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) is true and thus
    \( \disjunction{\theta(n^{\prime}, T_{n^{\prime} + 1})} \) must also be true because \( n^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( n^{\prime} = 0 \), then clearly \( T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) = g(\sigma, n^{\prime})\) so that \( \theta_{0}(n^{\prime}, T_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) can
    be satisfied.
    But \( T_{n^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}) = \sigma \) by \eqref{eq:the-values-of-Tn-at-i} (since \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true by assumption).
    Furthermore, \eqref{eq:obeys-the-required-condition-for-all-i-less-than-n-prime} also requires that \( T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) = T_{n^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}) = \sigma \).
    Thus, \( T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) = g(T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}), n^{\prime}) \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( n^{\prime} \neq 0 \), then we can also arrive at the same conclusion via the observation that
    \( T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) = g(T_{n^{\prime} + 1}(j^{\prime}), j^{\prime}) \) for some \( j^{\prime} \) such that \( n^{\prime} = j^{\prime} + 1 \);
    this is necessary so that \( \theta_{1}(n^{\prime}, T_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) can be satisfied.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Thus, the values of \( T_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) is defined by \eqref{eq:the-values-of-Tn-at-i} in all possible cases.
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) follows from \( P(n^{\prime}) \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude the inductive step from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
    The claim follows readily from \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Note that \( T \) is an indexed family of sets, so its union exists.
    Now let \( f = \Union T \).
    It remains to prove that \( f \) is indeed the infinite sequence we are searching for.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We begin by proving that \( \function{f}{\naturalset}{S} \).
    Let \( g \) and \( h \) be arbitrary elements of \( \ran T \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, there exist \( n, m \in \dom T \) such that \( \orderedpair{n}{g} \in T \) and \( \orderedpair{m}{h} \in T \).
    Note that \( \function{T}{\naturalset}{\finiteseq{S}} \), so \( \dom T = \naturalset \).
    In other words, \( n \) and \( m \) are elements of \( \naturalset \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers}, there are only three possible cases: \( n = m \), \( n \in m \), or \( m \in n \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We can then show that \( g \) and \( h \) are compatible in all three cases.
    If \( n = m \), then clearly \( g = h \) because \( T \) is a function. But if \( g = h \), then \( g \) and \( h \) must be compatible.
    If \( n \in m \), then \( g \subseteq h \) by \cref{claim:for-all-m-n-Tm-subset-of-Ts-if-m-less-than-n}.
    Thus, \( g \) and \( h \) are necessarily compatible by \cref{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-a-subset-of-another-function-then-the-two-functions-are-compatible}.
    If \( m \in n \), then \( h \subseteq g \) by \cref{claim:for-all-m-n-Tm-subset-of-Ts-if-m-less-than-n}.
    Once again, we conclude from \cref{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-a-subset-of-another-function-then-the-two-functions-are-compatible} that \( g \) and \( h \)
    are compatible.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since any two functions in \( \ran T \) are compatible, it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-compatibility-of-functions} that \( \ran T \)
    is a compatible family of functions. By \cref{thm:metatheory-a-family-of-functions-is-compatible-iff-its-union-is-a-function}, it follows
    that \( f = \Union T = \Union \ran T \) is a function.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( a \in \dom f \) be arbitrary.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, there exists a unique object \( b \) such that \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in f \).
    Since \( f = \Union T \), \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in f \) implies that \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in T_{n} \) for some \( n \in \naturalset \).
    In other words, \( a \in \dom T_{n} \)
    But the definition of \( T \) requires that \( \function{T_{n}}{n}{S} \); that is, \( \dom T_{n} = n \).
    Therefore, \( a \in n \).
    Since \( n \in \naturalset \) and \( n \subseteq \naturalset \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N},
    it follows that \( a \in \naturalset \).
    In other words, \( \dom f \subseteq \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Conversely, let \( n \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Now consider the function \( \function{T_{n+1}}{n+1}{S} \).
    If \( n = 0 \), then \( T_{n+1}(n) = \sigma \) by \cref{claim:the-values-of-all-Tn}, which means that \( \orderedpair{n}{\sigma} \in T_{k} \) for some \( k \in \naturalset \).
    Therefore, \( \orderedpair{n}{\sigma} \in f \) and thus \( n \in \dom f \).
    If \( n \neq 0 \), then \( n = j + 1 \) for some \( j \in \naturalset \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-is-a-successor-of-another-natural-number}.
    Moreover, we have \( T_{n+1}(n) = g(T_{n+1}(j), j) \) by \cref{claim:the-values-of-all-Tn}.
    In other words, there exists some \( k \in \naturalset \) such that \( \orderedpair{n}{g(T_{k}(j), j)} \in T_{k} \).
    Given that \( f = \Union T = \Union \ran T \), it follows that \( \orderedpair{n}{g(T_{k}(j), j)} \in f \) and thus \( n \in \dom f \).
    We have shown that in all possibilities, \( n \in \dom f \).
    Since \( n \) is arbitrary, we conclude that \( \naturalset \subseteq \dom f \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( \dom f \) and \( \naturalset \) are subsets of each other, it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( \dom f = \naturalset \).
    It is trivial to show that \( \ran f \subseteq S \); this follows readily from the fact that \( \ran T \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \).
    Taking all these facts into account, we arrive at the conclusion that \( \function{f}{\naturalset}{S} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, we prove that \( f \) has all the required properties stated in the theorem.
    Suppose that \( \orderedpair{0}{a} \in f \).
    Thus, there exists a \( k \in \naturalset \) such that \( \orderedpair{0}{a} \in T_{k} \).
    By \cref{claim:the-values-of-all-Tn}, necessarily \( a = \sigma \).
    In other words, \( f(0) = \sigma \).
    Now let \( n \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( \orderedpair{n+1}{b} \in f \); that is, \( \orderedpair{n+1}{b} \in T_{j} \) for some \( j \in \naturalset \).
    But \cref{claim:the-values-of-all-Tn} requires that \( b = g(T_{j}(n), n) = g(f(n), n) \).
    Therefore, \( T_{j}(n+1) = b = g(f(n), n) \).
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{n+1}{g(f(n), n)} \in T_{j} \).
    Since \( f = \Union T \), necessarily \( \orderedpair{n+1}{g(f(n), n)} \in f \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, f(n + 1) = g(f(n), n) \ \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \)}
    \end{equation*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Finally, we establish the uniqueness of any function that has the properties stated in \cref{recursion-theorem:standard-form}.
    Let \( f \) and \( h \) be any two such functions.
    We must therefore prove that \( f \) and \( g \) are necessarily equal.
    Clearly, \( \dom f = \dom h = \naturalset \).
    It remains to prove that \( f(n) = h(n) \) for all \( n \in \dom f \), which can be done by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} with
    \( P(n) \) defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq n \in \naturalset \ \text{and} \ f(n) = h(n)
    \end{equation*}
    Proving the base case is trivial since \( f(0) = \sigma = h(0) \).
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary natural number such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    We know that \( f(n^{\prime} + 1) = g(f(n^{\prime}), n^{\prime}) \) and \( h(n^{\prime} + 1) = g(h(n^{\prime}), n^{\prime})  \).
    Since \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true, it follows that \( f(n^{\prime}) = h(n^{\prime}) \), which in turn implies that
    \( f(n^{\prime} + 1) = g(f(n^{\prime}), n^{\prime}) = g(h(n^{\prime}), n^{\prime}) = h(n^{\prime} + 1) \).
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is true.
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that \( f(n) = h(n) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset\).
    Given that \( f \) and \( h \) have equal domains and have the same values every where, they must be equal functions.
    This suffices to establish uniqueness.
\end{proof}

\begin{recursion-theorem}[Sequence Form]
    \phantomsection \label{recursion-theorem:sequence-form}
    If \( S \) is a non-empty set and \( g \) is a function such that \( \function{g}{ \finiteseq{S} }{S} \),
    then there exists a unique function \( \function{f}{\naturalset}{S} \) such that
    \( f(n) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} ) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \).
\end{recursion-theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty set and \( g \) be a function on \( \finiteseq{S} \) into \( S \).
    Let \( \varphi(x, n, y) \) be a sequence of two predicates such that
    \begin{alignat*}{2}
        &\varphi_{0}(x, n, y) &&\coloneq \dom x = n \ \text{and} \ y = x \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n}{g(x)} \mright\} \\
        &\varphi_{1}(x, n, y) &&\coloneq \dom x \neq n \ \text{and} \ y = \emptyset
    \end{alignat*}
    Let \( \psi(x, n) \) be a predicate defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        \psi(x, n) \coloneq x \in \finiteseq{S} \ \text{and} \ n \in \naturalset
    \end{equation*}
    We then define a set \( G \) in terms of \( \psi(x, n) \) \( \varphi(x, n, y) \) like so.
    \begin{equation*}
        G \coloneq \setbuilderBig{\orderedpair{\orderedpair{x}{n}}{y}}{ \psi(x, n) \ \text{and} \, \disjunction{\varphi(x, n, y)} }
    \end{equation*}
    The first task is to prove that \( G \) is a function.
    let \( x^{\prime} \), \( n^{\prime} \), \( y^{\prime} \), and \( y^{\prime\prime} \) be arbitrary objects in the universe such that
    \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}}{y^{\prime}} \in G \) and \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \in G \)
    are both true.
    Clearly \( \psi(x^{\prime}, n^{\prime}) \) is true so that \( x \in \finiteseq{S} \) and \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \).
    We now consider the only two possibilities: either \( \dom x = n \) or \( \dom x \neq n \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \dom x = n \), then neither \( \varphi_{1}(x^{\prime}, n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) nor \( \varphi_{1}(x^{\prime}, n^{\prime}, y^{\prime\prime}) \) can be true.
    It follows that \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime}, n^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) and \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime}, n^{\prime}, y^{\prime\prime}) \) must be true in order for
    \( \disjunction{\varphi(x^{\prime}, n^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) and \( \disjunction{\varphi(x^{\prime}, n^{\prime}, y^{\prime\prime})} \) to be true.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, y^{\prime} = x^{\prime} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n}{g(x^{\prime})} \mright\} = y^{\prime\prime}
    \end{equation*}
    Similarly, if \( \dom x \neq n \), then \( y^{\prime} = \emptyset = y^{\prime\prime} \).
    In either possibility, \( y^{\prime} = y^{\prime\prime} \) follows.
    Thus, \( G \) is a function by \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.
    
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It is clear from the definition of \( G \) that \( \dom G = \finiteseq S \times \naturalset \).
    We now proceed to show that \( \ran G \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \).
    Let \( y^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \ran G \); that is, there exists an \( x^{\prime} \in \finiteseq{S} \)
    and an \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) such that \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}}{y^{\prime}} \in G \).
    Since \( x^{\prime} \in \finiteseq{S} \), it follows that \( \dom x^{\prime} \in \naturalset \). Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \),
    it follows that either \( n^{\prime} = \dom x^{\prime}  \) or \( n^{\prime} \neq \dom x^{\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( n^{\prime} = \dom x^{\prime} \), then necessarily \( y^{\prime} = x \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(x^{\prime})} \mright\}  \).
    Note that \( \function{x^{\prime}}{n}{S} \), so \( n^{\prime} \notin \dom x \).
    Moreover, \( \ran g \subseteq S \), \( g(x^{\prime}) \) is necessarily an element of \( S \).
    From this, we conclude that \( \function{y^{\prime}}{n^{\prime} + 1}{S} \) and thus \( y^{\prime} \in \finiteseq{S} \) because \( n^{\prime} + 1 \)
    is also an element of \( \naturalset \).
    On the other hand, if \( n^{\prime} \neq \dom x^{\prime} \), then necessarily \( y^{\prime} = \emptyset \).
    Since the empty sequence must be an element of \( \finiteseq{S} \), we conclude that \( y^{\prime} \in S \).
    In either case, \( y^{\prime} \in \finiteseq{S} \) follows. In other words,
    \( y^{\prime} \in \ran G \) implies \( y^{\prime} \in \finiteseq{S} \) for any arbitrary \( y^{\prime} \).
    Thus, \( \ran G \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    At this point, it has been established that \( \function{G}{\finiteseq{S} \times \naturalset}{\finiteseq{S}} \).
    Since \( \tuple{} \in \finiteseq{S} \), \aref{recursion-theorem:standard-form}{the standard form of the recursion theorem} guarantees
    the existence of a unique function \( \function{F}{\naturalset}{\finiteseq{S}} \) such that
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:values-of-F}
        \begin{aligned}
            F_{0}     = &\\
            F_{n + 1} = & \\
        \end{aligned}
        \begin{aligned}
            &\ \tuple{} \\
            &\ G(F_{n}, n) \ \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \)}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Note that \( F \) is an indexed family of functions with \( \naturalset \) as its index set.
    We now derive the properties of \( F \) in the form of the following claims.
    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:F-n-has-n-as-its-domain}
        For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( \dom F_{n} = n \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Let \( P(n) \) be the following predicate.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \dom F_{n} = n
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( P(k) \) is true for all \( k < n^{\prime} \) (induction hypothesis).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), it follows that there are two cases that we must consider:
    \( n^{\prime} = 0 \) or \( n^{\prime} \neq 0 \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( n^{\prime} = 0 \), then \( F_{0} = \tuple{} = \emptyset \) by \eqref{eq:values-of-F}.
    Clearly, \( \dom F_{n^{\prime}} = \dom F_{0} = \emptyset = 0 = n^{\prime} \).
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( n^{\prime} \neq 0 \), then \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-is-a-successor-of-another-natural-number} guarantees the existence
    of an \( i \in \naturalset \) such that \( n^{\prime} = i + 1 \).
    By \eqref{eq:values-of-F}, it follows that \( F_{n^{\prime}} = G(F_{i}, i) \); that is,
    \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{F_{i}}{i}}{F_{n^{\prime}}} \in G \).
    It follows from the definition of \( G \) that \( \disjunction{\varphi(F_{i}, i, F_{n^{\prime}})} \) is true.
    Observe that \( i < n^{\prime} \). The induction hypothesis therefore guarantees that \( \dom F_{i} = i \).
    This means that \( \varphi_{1}(F_{i}, i, F_{n^{\prime}}) \) cannot be true.
    Since \( \disjunction{\varphi(F_{i}, i, F_{n^{\prime}})} \) is true, it follows that \( \varphi_{0}(F_{i}, i, F_{n^{\prime}}) \) is necessarily true.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, F_{n^{\prime}} = F_{i} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{i}{g(F_{i})} \mright\}
    \end{equation*}
    From this, we can then infer \( \dom F_{n^{\prime}} = \dom F_{i} \union \mleft\{ i \mright\} = i \union \mleft\{ i \mright\} = i + i = n^{\prime} \).
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We have shown that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) follows from the induction hypothesis in all possible cases.
    We thus conclude from \aref{thm:metatheory-strong-induction}{Strong Induction} that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:Fn-proper-subset-of-Fm-for-all-n-less-than-m}
        For all \( n, m \in \naturalset \), \( m < n \) implies \( F_{m} \subset F_{n} \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    We begin our inductive proof by defining the predicate \( P(n) \) as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{For all \( m \in \naturalset \), \( m < n \) implies \( F_{m} \subset F_{n} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    The base case \( P(0) \) is vacuously true since no natural number is less than \( 0 \).
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be any element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary natural number such that \( m^{\prime} < n^{\prime} + 1 \); i.e., \( m^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \).
    Clearly, either \( m^{\prime} < n^{\prime} \) or \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( m^{\prime} < n^{\prime} \), then necessarily \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime}} \) because \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    By \eqref{eq:values-of-F}, we also have \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = G(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}) \). In other words,
    \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{F_{n^{\prime}}}{n^{\prime}}}{F_{n^{\prime} + 1}} \in G \) and thus
    \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\} \).
    Obviously, \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subseteq F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \). Furthermore, the ordered pair \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \)
    does not belong to \( F_{m^{\prime}} \) because \( \dom F_{m^{\prime}} = m^{\prime} \) (see \cref{claim:F-n-has-n-as-its-domain})
    and \( n^{\prime} \notin m^{\prime} \).
    In other words, \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \), then we have \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{m^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{m^{\prime}})} \mright\} \)
    according to \eqref{eq:values-of-F}.
    Again, it follows that \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) because \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subseteq F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) and
    \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{m^{\prime}})} \notin F_{m^{\prime}} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It has thus been shown that \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) is implied in all possible cases.
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is true.
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) implies \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) for any arbitrary \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \).
    The truth of \cref{claim:Fn-proper-subset-of-Fm-for-all-n-less-than-m} follows immediately from \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:F-is-a-compatible-family-of-functions}
        \( F \) is a compatible indexed family of function.
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Let \( n \) and \( m \) be arbitrary elements of \( \dom F \).
    Since \( \dom F = \naturalset \), it follows that \( n \) and \( m \) are both elements of \( \naturalset \).
    We consider the following two cases: \( n = m \) or \( n \neq m \).
    If \( n = m \), then the proof is trivial because \( F_{n} \) and \( F_{m} \) are necessarily compatible because they are equal functions.
    If \( n \neq m \), then either \( n < m \) or \( m < n \) according to \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers}.
    By \cref{claim:Fn-proper-subset-of-Fm-for-all-n-less-than-m}, it follows that either \( F_{n} \subset F_{m} \) or \( F_{m} \subset F_{n} \).
    In either case, \( F_{n} \) and \( F_{m} \) are compatible by
    \cref{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-a-subset-of-another-function-then-the-two-functions-are-compatible}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, we utilize the properties of \( F \) to construct the function \( f \).
    Let \( f = \Union_{n \,\in\, \naturalset} F_{n} \).
    It will be shown via the following claims that \( f \) has the properties stated in the theorem.
    \begin{claim}
        \( \function{f}{\naturalset}{S} \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Since \( F \) is a compatible indexed family of functions (\cref{claim:F-is-a-compatible-family-of-functions}),
    it follows immediately from \cref{thm:metatheory-a-family-of-functions-is-compatible-iff-its-union-is-a-function} that
    \( f = \Union_{n \,\in\, \naturalset} F_{n} = \Union \ran F \) is a function.
    Additionally, we have by \cref{thm:metatheory-the-domain-of-a-compatible-family-of-functions-is-the-union-of-the-domains-of-each-function-in-the-family}
    \begin{equation*}
        \dom f = \dom \mleft( \Union \ran F \mright) = \Union \setbuilderBig{\dom x}{x \in \ran F}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( T = \Union \setbuilder{\dom x}{x \in \ran F} \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object such that \( \alpha \in T \).
    Thus, \( \alpha \in \dom F_{n} \) for some \( n \in \naturalset \).
    But \( \dom F_{n} = n \) (\cref{claim:F-n-has-n-as-its-domain}), so necessarily \( \alpha \in n \).
    Since \( n \subseteq \naturalset \), it follows that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \).
    In other words, \( T \subseteq \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Clearly, \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) is also an element of \( \naturalset \) because \( \naturalset \) is inductive.
    Since \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \naturalset \) and \( \dom F = \naturalset \), it follows that
    \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \dom F \).
    In other words, there exists some \( y^{\prime} \in \finiteseq{S} \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime} + 1}{y^{\prime}} \in F \).
    Furthermore, \( \dom y^{\prime} = n^{\prime} + 1 \) due to \cref{claim:F-n-has-n-as-its-domain}.
    This means that \( n^{\prime} \in \dom y^{\prime} \) for some \( y^{\prime} \in \ran F \); that is, \( n^{\prime} \in T \).
    Clearly, \( \naturalset \subseteq T \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( \naturalset \) and \( T \) are subsets of each other, it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} that
    \( \dom f = \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    To prove that \( \ran f \subseteq S \), let \( y^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \ran f \).
    Thus, there exists some \( n^{\prime} \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Since \( f = \Union_{n \,\in\, \naturalset} F_{n} \), it follows that there exists some \( m \in \naturalset \)
    such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in F_{m} \).
    Since \( F_{m} \in \finiteseq{S} \), it follows that \( \ran F_{m} \subseteq S \).
    Therefore, \( y^{\prime} \in \ran F_{m} \) implies \( y^{\prime} \in S \).
    From this we infer that every element of \( \ran f \) is necessarily an element of \( S \).
    In other words, \( \ran f \subseteq S \).

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:f-restricted-to-n-is-equals-to-F-at-n}
        For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} = F_{n} \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    We prove this claim by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} = F_{n}
    \end{equation*}
    The base case is trivial since \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{0} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{\emptyset} = \emptyset = \tuple{} = F_{0} \).
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Observe that
    \begin{equation*}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime} + 1} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{f(n^{\prime})} \mright\}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( P(n) \) is true, it follows that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} = F_{n^{\prime}} \).
    Thus, the equality becomes
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:f-restricted-to-n-prime-plus-one}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{f(n^{\prime})} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \), it follows that \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \naturalset \) because \( \naturalset \) is inductive.
    We know that \( \function{F}{\naturalset}{\finiteseq{S}} \); that is, \( \dom F = \naturalset \).
    Since \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \naturalset \), it follows that \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \dom F \).
    In other words, \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) is a well-defined element of \( \finiteseq{S} \).
    Furthermore, we know from \cref{claim:F-n-has-n-as-its-domain} that \( \dom F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-successor}, it follows that \( n^{\prime} \in \dom F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).
    Thus, there exists a unique \( y^{\prime} \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).
    In other words, there exists a \( j \in \naturalset \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in F_{j} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-union-of-indexed-family-of-sets}, it follows that
    \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in \Union_{n \,\in\, \naturalset} F_{n} \) and thus \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Since \( y^{\prime} = f(n^{\prime}) = F_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) \),
    \eqref{eq:f-restricted-to-n-prime-plus-one} becomes
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:f-restricted-to-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{F_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime})} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    Note also that \( F_{\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright)} = G(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}) \) by \eqref{eq:values-of-F};
    that is, \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{F_{n^{\prime}}} \in G \).
    From the definition of \( G \), it follows that either \( \varphi_{0}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \)
    or \(  \varphi_{1}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) is true.
    Since \( \dom F_{n^{\prime}} = n^{\prime} \) as established by \cref{claim:F-n-has-n-as-its-domain}, it follows that
    \( \varphi_{1}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) cannot be true.
    Therefore, \( \varphi_{0}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) is true and thus
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:F-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form}
        F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    It should now be obvious from \eqref{eq:f-restricted-to-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form} and \eqref{eq:F-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime} + 1 } = F_{n^{\prime} + 1}  \).
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) follows from \( P(n^{\prime}) \) for any arbitrary \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \).
    \cref{claim:f-restricted-to-n-is-equals-to-F-at-n} follows readily from \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:the-value-of-f-at-n-is-the-value-of-g-at-f-restricted-to-n}
        For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( f(n) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} ) \)
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \), it follows that
    \( f(n^{\prime}) = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) \).
    Note that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime} + 1} \) and \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) are equal functions (see \cref{claim:f-restricted-to-n-is-equals-to-F-at-n}).
    Thus, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        f(n^{\prime}) = F_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime})
    \end{equation*}
    But \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = G(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}) \) according to \eqref{eq:values-of-F}.
    This means that either \( \varphi_{0}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) or
    \( \varphi_{1}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) is true.
    Since \( \dom F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = n^{\prime} \),
    it follows that \( \varphi_{1}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) cannot be true.
    Therefore, \( \varphi_{0}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) must be true
    and \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_n^{\prime})} \mright\} \).
    Clearly,
    \begin{equation*}
        F_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) = g(F_{n^{\prime}})
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( F_{n^{\prime}} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} \) by \cref{claim:f-restricted-to-n-is-equals-to-F-at-n}, the equation becomes
    \begin{equation*}
        f(n^{\prime}) = F_{n^{\prime} + 1}(n^{\prime}) = g(F_{n^{\prime}}) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} )
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, \cref{claim:the-value-of-f-at-n-is-the-value-of-g-at-f-restricted-to-n} follows from
    \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Last but not least, we have to prove that any function \( \function{f}{\naturalset}{S} \) such that \( f(n) = g(\restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n}) \)
    for all \( n \in \naturalset \) is necessarily unique.
    Let \( f^{\prime} \) and \( f^{\prime\prime} \) be two functions on \( \naturalset \) such that
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:f-prime-and-f-prime-prime-at-n-equals-g-at-the-function-restricted-to-n}
        \begin{aligned}
            f^{\prime}(n) &\\
            f^{\prime\prime}(n)&
        \end{aligned}
        \begin{aligned}
            &\ = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{n} )\\
            &\ = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{n} )
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    for all \( n \in \naturalset \).
    The goal, therefore, is to prove that \( f^{\prime}(n) = f^{\prime\prime}(n) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \)
    so that we can conclude that \( f^{\prime} = f^{\prime\prime} \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-function-equality}.
    To do this, it suffices to prove that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{n} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{n} \)
    for all \( n \in \naturalset \).
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq n \in \naturalset \ \text{and} \ \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{n} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{n}
    \end{equation*}
    The proof for the base case is trivial since \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{0} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{0} = \emptyset \).
    For the inductive step, let \( m \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(m) \) is true.
    Since \( m + 1 = m \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ m \mright\} \), it follows that
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:f-prime-and-f-prime-prime-restricted-to-m-plus-one-union-form}
        \begin{aligned}
            \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{m + 1} &\\
            \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{m + 1} &
        \end{aligned}
        \begin{aligned}
            &\ = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{m} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{m}{f^{\prime}(m)} \mright\}\\
            &\ = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{m} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{m}{f^{\prime\prime}(m)} \mright\}\\
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    By \eqref{eq:f-prime-and-f-prime-prime-at-n-equals-g-at-the-function-restricted-to-n}, we have
    \( f^{\prime}(m) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{m} ) \) and
    \( f^{\prime\prime}(m) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{m} )\).
    But \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{m}  \) and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{m} \) are the same set
    because \( P(m) \) is true. Since \( g \) is a function, it follows that \( f^{\prime}(m) = f^{\prime\prime}(m) \).
    It should now be obvious from \eqref{eq:f-prime-and-f-prime-prime-restricted-to-m-plus-one-union-form} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{m + 1} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{m + 1} \).
    In other words, \( P(m + 1) \) is implied by \( P(m) \).
    We then conclude by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:f-prime-restricted-to-n-and-f-prime-prime-restricted-to-n-have-equal-values-for-all-N}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime}}{n} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f^{\prime\prime}}{n} \ \ \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \)}
    \end{equation}
    Together, \eqref{eq:f-prime-and-f-prime-prime-at-n-equals-g-at-the-function-restricted-to-n}
    and \eqref{eq:f-prime-restricted-to-n-and-f-prime-prime-restricted-to-n-have-equal-values-for-all-N}
    imply that \( f^{\prime} = f^{\prime\prime} \), which completes the uniqueness proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{recursion-theorem}[Generalized Standard Form]
    \phantomsection \label{recursion-theorem:generalized-standard-form}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty set.
    If \( g \) is a function such that \( \dom g \subseteq  S \times \naturalset \) and \( \ran g \subseteq  S \) and \( \sigma \) is an element of \( S \), then
    there exists a unique function \( f \) such that:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( f(0) = \sigma \)
        \item \( f(n + 1) = g(f(n), n) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \) such that \( n + 1 \in \dom f \)
        \item either \( \dom f = \naturalset \) or \( \dom f = k + 1 \) where \( \orderedpair{f(k)}{k} \notin \dom g \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{recursion-theorem}

\begin{proof}
    By \cref{meta-rule:law-of-excluded-middle}, it follows that either \( \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \subseteq \dom g  \)
    or \( \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \nsubseteq \dom g \).
    If \( \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \subseteq \dom g \), then \( S \times \naturalset = \dom g \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}
    because \( \dom g \subseteq \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \) by assumption.
    But if \( \dom g = \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \), then the problem reduces to the case of \aref{recursion-theorem:standard-form}{standard recursion}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    On the other hand, if \( \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \nsubseteq \dom g \), then there must exist a \( p \in \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \) such that
    \( p \notin \dom g \). By \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}, it follows that \( p = \orderedpair{e}{m} \) for some \( e \in S \) and \( m \in \naturalset \).
    Let \( \psi(x, y) \) be a sequence of two predicates such that
    \begin{alignat*}{2}
        &\psi_{0}(x, y) &&\coloneq x \in \dom g \ \text{and} \ \orderedpair{x}{y} \in g\\
        &\psi_{1}(x, y) &&\coloneq x \notin \dom g \ \text{and} \ y = e
    \end{alignat*}
    Now let the set \( g^{\prime} \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        g^{\prime} \coloneq \setbuilderbig{\orderedpair{x}{y}}{\text{there exists \( a \in S \) and \( b \in n \) such that \( x = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) and
        \( \disjunction{\psi(x, y)} \) is true}}
    \end{equation*}
    It is trivial to prove that \( g^{\prime} \) exists.
    We now prove that \( \function{g^{\prime}}{\mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright)}{S} \).
    It is clear from the definition of \( g^{\prime} \) that \( g^{\prime} \) is a set of ordered pairs and that
    \( \dom g^{\prime} \subseteq \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \).
    Suppose that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g^{\prime} \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in g \) for objects \( x^{\prime} \), \( y^{\prime} \),
    and \( z^{\prime} \).
    Thus, \( x^{\prime} = \orderedpair{a}{b} \) for some \( a \in S \) and \( b \in \naturalset \).
    Furthermore, \( \disjunction{\psi(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) and \( \disjunction{\psi(x^{\prime}, z^{\prime})} \) are both true.
    We now consider the only two possibilities: \( x^{\prime} \in \dom g \) or \( x^{\prime} \notin \dom g \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( x^{\prime} \in \dom g \), then \( \psi_{1}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) and \( \psi_{1}(x^{\prime}, z^{\prime}) \) cannot be true, which means
    that \( \psi_{0}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) and \( \psi_{0}(x^{\prime}, z^{\prime}) \) must be true so that
    \( \disjunction{\psi(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) and \( \disjunction{\psi(x^{\prime}, z^{\prime})} \) can both be true.
    But if \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in g \) are both true, then necessarily
    \( y^{\prime} = z^{\prime} \) because \( g \) is a function.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( x^{\prime} \notin \dom g \), then \( \psi_{0}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) and \( \psi_{0}(x^{\prime}, z^{\prime}) \) cannot be true;
    therefore, \( \psi_{1}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) and \( \psi_{1}(x^{\prime}, z^{\prime}) \) hold.
    By the definition of \( \psi(x, y) \), it follows that \( y^{\prime} = e = z^{\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Thus, \( y^{\prime} = z^{\prime} \) is implied in all possible cases.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}, it follows that \( g^{\prime} \) is a function.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    To prove that \( \dom g^{\prime} = \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \), it suffices to prove that \( \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \subseteq \dom g^{\prime} \)
    since we already know that \( \dom g^{\prime} \subseteq \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \).
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary element of \( S \times \naturalset \);
    that is, there exist objects \( a \in S \) and \( b \in \naturalset \) such that \( p = \orderedpair{a}{b} \).
    Since \( \dom g \subseteq \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \) by assumption, it follows that either \( p \in \dom g \) or \( p \notin \dom g \).
    If \( p \in \dom g \), then there exists a \( y \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{p}{y} \in g \).
    This means that \( \psi_{0}(p, y) \) is true and thus \( \orderedpair{p}{y} \in g^{\prime} \).
    In other words, there exists a \( y \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{p}{y} \in g^{\prime} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, it follows that \( p \in \dom g^{\prime} \).
    On the other hand, if \( p \notin \dom g \), then it follows from the definition of \( g^{\prime} \) that \( \orderedpair{p}{e} \in g^{\prime} \).
    Therefore, \( p \) also belongs to \( \dom g^{\prime} \).
    Clearly, \( p \in \dom g^{\prime} \) is true in all possible cases.
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x  \), \( x \in \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \) implies \( x \in \dom g^{\prime} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, \( \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \subseteq \dom g^{\prime} \) as needed.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It is trivial to show that \( \ran g^{\prime} \subseteq S \) (both \( \psi_{0}(x, y) \) and \( \psi_{1}(x, y) \) implies \( y \in S \)).
    Since \( g^{\prime} \) is a function with \( \dom g^{\prime} = \mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright) \) and \( \ran g^{\prime} \subseteq S \),
    it follows that \( \function{g^{\prime}}{\mleft( S \times \naturalset \mright)}{S} \).
    Given that \( \sigma \in S \), it follows from \cref{recursion-theorem:standard-form} that there exists a unique function
    \( \function{h}{\naturalset}{S} \) such that
    \begin{align}
        h(0) &= \sigma \phantomsection \label{eq:h-is-sigma-at-0} \\
        h(n + 1) &= g^{\prime}(h(n), n) \ \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \)} \phantomsection \label{eq:recursive-properties-of-h}
    \end{align}
    Let \( T = \setbuilder{n \in \dom h}{\orderedpair{n}{e} \in h} \).
    Observe that since \( T \subseteq \naturalset \), \cref{thm:metatheory-well-ordering-of-natural-numbers} guarantees that \( T \) has a least element
    if \( T \neq \emptyset \).
    Now let \( f = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \union \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{D} \) where
    \begin{equation*}
        D =
        \begin{cases}
            \naturalset \ &\text{if} \ T = \emptyset\\
            \lambda \ &\text{if} \ T \neq \emptyset \ \text{and \( \lambda \) is the least element of \( T \)}
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    It should be obvious that \( f \) is a function.
    It remains to show that, in both scenarios, \( f(0) = \sigma \) and \( f(n + 1) = g(f(n), n) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \) such that \( n + 1 \in \dom f \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( T = \emptyset \), then \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{D} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\naturalset} = h \) because \( \dom h = \naturalset \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \in h \), it follows that \( \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \subseteq h \)
    and thus \( f = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \union \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{D} = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \union h = h \)
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-union-of-a-set-and-its-subset-is-itself}.
    It should be obvious that \( f(0) = h(0) = \sigma \).
    To prove that \( f(n + 1) = g(f(n), n) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \) such that \( \mleft( n + 1 \mright) \in \dom f \), we resort to
    \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) have the following definition.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{ if \( \mleft( n + 1 \mright) \in \dom f \), then \( f(n + 1) = g(f(n), n) \) }
    \end{equation*}
    
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(0) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( f = h \), we have \( \dom f = \dom h = \naturalset \). Clearly, \( 0 \in \dom f \) because \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \in f \).
    Therefore, \( 0 + 1 \in \naturalset \) because \( \naturalset \) is inductive.
    Since \( \dom h = \naturalset \), it follows that \( \mleft( 0 + 1 \mright) \in \dom h \).
    This means that \( \orderedpair{0 + 1}{y} \in h \) for some \( y \in S \).
    Moreover, we can also infer that \( y \neq e \) for \( T \) would not be empty otherwise.
    By \eqref{eq:recursive-properties-of-h}, it follows that \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{h(0)}{0}}{y} \in g^{\prime} \).
    Since \( h(0) = \sigma \), we conclude that \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}}{y} \in g^{\prime} \).
    This means that \( \disjunction{\psi( \orderedpair{\sigma}{0}, y )} \) is true.
    Since \( y \neq e \), \( \psi_{1}(\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}, y) \) cannot be true.
    Thus, \( \psi_{0}(\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}, y) \) must be true for \( \disjunction{\psi( \orderedpair{\sigma}{0}, y )} \) to be true.
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}}{y} \in g \) and thus \( f(0 + 1) = y = g(\sigma, 0) = g(f(0), 0) \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \)

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Clearly, \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) and \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1 \) are both elements of \( \naturalset \).
    Since \( \dom h = \naturalset \), it follows that \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1 \) belongs to \( \dom h \).
    Thus, there exists a \( y \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1}{y} \in h \).
    Furthermore, \( y \neq e \) because \( T \) would not be empty otherwise.
    By \eqref{eq:recursive-properties-of-h}, it follows that
    \( y = h(\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1) = g^{\prime}(h(n^{\prime} + 1), n^{\prime} + 1) \).
    Equivalently, \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}}{y} \in g^{\prime} \).
    But \( y \neq e \), so \( \psi_{1}( \orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}, y ) \) cannot be true.
    For \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}}{y} \) to be an element of \( g^{\prime} \),
    \( \psi_{0}( \orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}, y ) \) must therefore be true.
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}}{y} \in g \).
    Equivalently, \( f(\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1) =  y = g(h(n^{\prime} + 1), n^{\prime} + 1) = g(f(n^{\prime} + 1), n^{\prime} + 1) \).
    Thus, \( P(n^{\prime}) \) implies \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the inductive step follows immediately from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Having established both the base case and the inductive step, we conclude by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that
    \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \); that is, if \( T = \emptyset \), then \( f \) meets the criteria specified in the theorem.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( T \neq \emptyset \), then \( T \) must have a least element \( \lambda \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-well-ordering-of-natural-numbers}.
    We consider two cases: \( \lambda = 0 \) or \( \lambda \neq 0 \).
    If \( \lambda = 0 \), then \( f = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \union \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} =
    \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \union \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\emptyset}
    = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \union \emptyset = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \).
    Clearly, \( f(0) = \sigma \).
    Moreover, since \( 0 + 1 \notin \dom f \), the second criterion is vacuously true.
    If \( \lambda \neq 0 \), then \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-is-a-successor-of-another-natural-number} guarantees
    the existence of a \( k \in \naturalset \) such that \( \lambda = k + 1 \).
    Since \( \lambda \neq 0 \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-contains-the-empty-set} that
    \( 0 \in \lambda \) and thus \( 0 \in \dom \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \in h \) by \eqref{eq:h-is-sigma-at-0},
    it follows that \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \) because \( 0 \in \lambda \).
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \) belong to both \( \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \) and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \).
    Thus, \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \in f \) from which we can infer \( f(0) = \sigma \).
    Next, observe that \( \dom f = \lambda = k + 1 \).
    Let \( P(n) \) be a predicate defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{if \( \mleft( n + 1 \mright) \in \dom f \), then \( f(n + 1) = g(f(n), n) \)}
    \end{equation*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(0) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \mleft( 0 + 1 \mright) \notin \dom f \), then \( P(0) \) is vacuously true.
    If, however, \( \mleft( 0 + 1 \mright) \in \dom f \), then there must exists a \( y \in S \)
    such that \( \orderedpair{0 + 1}{y} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \)
    because \( f = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \,{\union}\, \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{0 + 1}{y} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \), then necessarily \( \orderedpair{0 + 1}{y} \in h \) by
    \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}; that is, \( y = h(0 + 1) \).
    By \eqref{eq:recursive-properties-of-h}, it thus follows that \( y = g^{\prime}(h(0), 0) \).
    Recall that \( h(0) = \sigma \) by \eqref{eq:h-is-sigma-at-0}.
    Therefore, \( y = g^{\prime}(\sigma, 0) \) or, in other words, \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}}{y} \in g^{\prime} \).
    By the definition of \( g^{\prime} \), it follows that \( \disjunction{\psi(\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}, y)} \) must be true.
    Recall that \( \lambda \) is the least element in \( \naturalset \) at which \( h \) has a value of \( e \).
    Since \( \mleft( 0 + 1 \mright) \in \dom f \) and \( \dom f = \lambda \), it follows that \( \mleft( 0 + 1 \mright) \in \lambda \)
    and thus \( \mleft( 0 + 1 \mright) < \lambda \).
    From this we can infer that \( y = h(0 + 1) \) cannot be \( e \); otherwise, \( \lambda \) would not be the least element of \( T \).
    Since \( y \neq e \), \( \psi_{1}(\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}, y) \) cannot possibly be true.
    Thus, for \( \disjunction{\psi(\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}, y)} \) to be true,
    \( \psi_{0}(\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}, y) \) must be true.
    By the definition of \( \psi \), it follows that \( \orderedpair{\sigma}{0} \in \dom g \) and \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{\sigma}{0}}{y} \in g \).
    In other words, \( f(0 + 1) = y = g(\sigma, 0) = g(f(0), 0) \) and thus \( P(0) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \) for all \( n < k \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( n^{\prime} < k \) and \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Since \( n^{\prime} < k \), then \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \leq k \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n}.
    Since \( \dom f = \lambda = k + 1 \), it follows that \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \dom f \).
    Note also that \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \neq 0 \).
    Since \( f = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \,{\union}\, \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \) and \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) \in \lambda \),
    it follows that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:value-of-f-at-n-prime-plus-one-equals-value-of-h-at-n-prime-plus-one}
        f(n^{\prime} + 1) = (\restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda})(n^{\prime} + 1) = h(n^{\prime} + 1)
    \end{equation}
    Suppose that \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1 \) also belongs to \( \dom f \).
    This means that there exists a \( y \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1 }{y} \in f \).
    Clearly, \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1 \neq 0 \), so the ordered pair \( \orderedpair{\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1 }{y} \) cannot be
    equal to \( \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \).
    Since \( f = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{\sigma} \mright\} \,{\union}\, \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \), it follows that
    \( \orderedpair{\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1}{y} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{\lambda} \).
    Since \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1 \in \dom f \) and \( \dom f = \lambda \), it follows that
    \( \orderedpair{\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1}{y} \in h \).
    By \eqref{eq:recursive-properties-of-h}, it follows that \( y = g^{\prime}(h(n^{\prime} + 1), n^{\prime} + 1) \).
    Equivalently, \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}}{y} \in g^{\prime} \).
    It thus follows from the definition of \( g^{\prime} \) that either
    \begin{equation*}
        \psi_{0}(\orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}, y) \ \text{or}
        \ \psi_{1}(\orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}, y)
    \end{equation*}
    is true.
    Since \( \mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1 \in \lambda \), \( y \) cannot possibly be equal to \( e \);
    otherwise, \( \lambda \) cannot possibly be the least element of \( T \).
    In other words, \( \psi_{1}(\orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}, y) \)
    is false, which means that \( \psi_{0}(\orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1}, y) \)
    must be true.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \orderedpair{ \orderedpair{h(n^{\prime} + 1)}{n^{\prime} + 1} }{y} \in g
    \end{equation*}
    Equivalently, \( y = g(h(n^{\prime} + 1), n^{\prime} + 1) \).
    By \eqref{eq:value-of-f-at-n-prime-plus-one-equals-value-of-h-at-n-prime-plus-one},
    this becomes
    \begin{equation*}
        f(\mleft( n^{\prime} + 1 \mright) + 1) = y = g(f(n^{\prime} + 1), n^{\prime} + 1)
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is implied by \( P(n^{\prime}) \) and \( n^{\prime} < k \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it the inductive step follows readily from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Having established both the base step and the inductive step, we conclude from \aref{thm:metatheory-finite-induction}{Finite Induction} that
    \( f \) has all the required properties if \( T \neq 0 \).
    The uniqueness of \( f \) can be established easily by \aref{thm:metatheory-finite-induction}{Finite Induction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{recursion-theorem}[Generalized Sequence Form]
    \phantomsection \label{recursion-theorem:generalized-sequence-form}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty set.
    If \( g \) is a function such that \( \dom g \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \) and \( \ran g \subseteq S \), then there exists a unique function
    \( f \) such that:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item for all \( n \in \dom f \), \( f(n) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} ) \)
        \item either \( \dom f = \naturalset \) or \( \dom f = k \) for some \( k \in \naturalset \) such that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{k} \notin \dom g \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{recursion-theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) be a non-empty set and \( g \) be a function such that \( \dom g \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \) and \( \ran g \subseteq S \).
    We now consider two cases.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case}: \( \finiteseq{S} \subseteq \dom g \).

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Clearly \( \dom g = \finiteseq{S} \), in which case
    \cref{recursion-theorem:sequence-form} implies the existence of a unique function \( \function{f}{\naturalset}{S} \) such that
    \( f(n) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} ) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case}: \( \finiteseq{S} \nsubseteq \dom g \).

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( \psi \) be a sequence of three predicates such that
    \begin{alignat*}{2}
        &\psi_{0}(x, n, y) &&\coloneq \ x \in \finiteseq{S} \ \text{and} \ n \in \naturalset \\
        &\psi_{1}(x, n, y) &&\coloneq \ x \in \dom g \ \text{and}\ \dom x = n\\
        &\psi_{2}(x, n, y) &&\coloneq \ y = x \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n}{g(x)} \mright\}
    \end{alignat*}
    Let \( G = \setbuilder{\orderedpair{\orderedpair{x}{n}}{y}}{\conjunction{\psi}} \) (the reader is highly encouraged to verify the existence of this set).
    We begin by proving that the set \( G \) is a function.
    Let \( x^{\prime} \), \( n^{\prime} \), \( y^{\prime} \), and \( z^{\prime} \) be objects in the universe such that
    \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}}{y^{\prime}} \in G \) and \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}}{z^{\prime}} \in G \) are both true.
    Since \( g \) is a function and the \aref{thm:metatheory-uniqueness-of-union}{the union of sets is unique}, it follows that \( y^{\prime} = z^{\prime} \).
    Therefore, \( G \) is a function with \( \dom G \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \times \naturalset \) and \( \ran G \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \) due to \( \psi_{0} \) and
    \( \psi_{2} \) respectively.
    This means that \cref{recursion-theorem:generalized-standard-form} applies and there exists a unique function
    \( F \) such that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:values-of-F-2}
        \begin{aligned}
            F_{0} &\\
            F_{n + 1} &
        \end{aligned}
        \begin{aligned}
            &= \tuple{}\\
            &= G(F_{n}, n)
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    for all \( n \in \naturalset \) such that \( n + 1 \in \dom F \) where \( \dom F \) is either \( \naturalset \) or \( j + 1 \) for some \( j \in \naturalset \)
    such that \( \orderedpair{F_{j}}{j} \notin \dom G \).
    Now we let \( f = \Union F  \) and proceed to prove that \( f \) has all the required properties posited in the theorem.
    To do this, it is crucial to establish the following claims.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:for-all-n-in-N-dom-F-n-is-n}
        For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( n \in \dom F \) implies \( \dom F_{n} = n \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    We prove this claim by induction.
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq n \in \naturalset \ \text{and} \ n \in \dom F \ \text{implies} \ \dom F_{n} = n
    \end{equation*}
    The base case is straightforward since \( 0 \in \naturalset \) and \( \dom F_{0} = \dom \emptyset = \emptyset = 0 \).
    For the inductive step, let \( n^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \) and \aref{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers-is-inductive}{\( \naturalset \) is an inductive set},
    it follows that \( n^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \) is also an element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \).
    Then \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = G(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}) \) by \eqref{eq:values-of-F-2}.
    This means that \( \psi_{2}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) is true and thus
    \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\} \).
    Clearly, \( \dom F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = \dom F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \).
    But \( \dom F_{n^{\prime}} = n^{\prime} \) because \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Therefore, \( \dom F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = n^{\prime} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} = n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    In other words, if \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true, then \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) follows.
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the inductive step follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
    Having established the base case and the inductive step, we conclude by \cref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction} that
    \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:if-n-less-than-m-then-F-n-is-proper-subset-of-F-m}
        For all \( n, m \in \dom F \), \( m < n \) implies \( F_{m} \subset F_{n} \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq n \in \naturalset \ \text{and for all \( m \in n \), \( F_{m} \subset F_{n} \) }
    \end{equation*}
    If \( \dom F = \naturalset \), then we can proceed by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} like so.
    First, we note that \( P(0) \) is vacuously true since the empty set contains no element (base case).
    For the inductive step, let \( n^{\prime} \) be an element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \);
    that is, either \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \) or \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \).
    If \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \), then \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime}} \) because \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Recall that \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = G(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}) \).
    This means that \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\} \) due to
    \( \psi_{2} \).
    Clearly, \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).
    If \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \), the same argument applies.
    Since \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \), necessarily \( F_{m^{\prime}} = F_{n^{\prime}} \) due to the fact that \( F \) is a function.
    Thus, \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\} = F_{m^{\prime}} \,{\union}\,
    \mleft\{ \orderedpair{m^{\prime}}{g(F_{m^{\prime}})} \mright\} \).
    Once again, it follows that \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( m \in n^{\prime} + 1 \), \( F_{m} \subset F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) }
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is implied by \( P(n^{\prime}) \).
    The inductive step is then justified via \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
    By \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}, we conclude that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \) in \( \dom F = \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \dom F = j + 1 \) for some \( j \in \naturalset \), the same inductive proof applies except that we only have to establish the inductive step for all \( n \in j \).
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( n^{\prime} < j \) and that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Note that \( n^{\prime} < j \) implies \( n^{\prime} + 1 \leq j \) by
    \cref{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n}.
    Since \( \dom F = j + 1 \) and \( n^{\prime} + 1 \leq j \), it follows that \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \).
    In other words, \( F \) is necessarily defined at \( n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    Let \( m^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary natural number such that \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} + 1 \); that is, either \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \)
    or \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \).
    If \( m^{\prime} \in n^{\prime} \), then \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime}} \) follows readily from the fact that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
