    Since \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \), we have \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = G(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}) \) by \eqref{eq:values-of-F-2}.
    We then infer from the definition of \( G \) that \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\} \).
    Since \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime}} \), necessarily \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).
    If \( m^{\prime} = n^{\prime} \), then clearly \( F_{m^{\prime}} = F_{n^{\prime}} \) because \( F \) is a function.
    Moreover, \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\}
    = F_{m^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{m^{\prime}}{g(F_{m^{\prime}})} \mright\}\).
    It follows from the definition of union that every element of \( F_{m^{\prime}} \) is an element of \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \);
    also, the ordered pair \( \orderedpair{m^{\prime}}{g(F_{m^{\prime}})} \) does not belong to \( F_{m^{\prime}} \) because
    \( \dom F_{m^{\prime}} = m^{\prime} \) and \( m^{\prime} \notin m^{\prime} \).
    From this, we conclude that \( F_{m^{\prime}} \subset F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).
    Since \( m^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is true; that is,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( n^{\prime} < j \), then \( P(n^{\prime}) \) implies \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( P(0) \) is true and \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows from \aref{thm:metatheory-finite-induction}{Finite Induction} that
    \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \) in \( \dom F = j + 1 \).

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:f-is-a-function}
        \( f \) is a function.
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Let \( F^{\prime} \) and \( F^{\ast} \) be arbitrary elements of \( \ran F \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, it follows that there exist \( n, m \in \dom F\)
    such that \( \orderedpair{n}{F^{\prime}} \in F \) and \( \orderedpair{m}{F^{\ast}} \in F \).
    Since \( \dom F = \naturalset \), it follows that \( n \) and \( m \) are both elements of \( \naturalset \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers}, there are only three possibilities: \( n = m \), \( n < m \), or \( m < n \).
    If \( n = m \), then obviously \( F^{\prime} \) and \( F^{\ast} \) are compatible.
    If \( n < m \), then  \( F^{\prime} \subseteq F^{\ast} \) follows from \cref{claim:if-n-less-than-m-then-F-n-is-proper-subset-of-F-m}
    and thus \( F^{\prime} \) and \( F^{\ast} \) are compatible by \cref{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-a-subset-of-another-function-then-the-two-functions-are-compatible}.
    If \( m < n \), the same argument applies with the roles of \( n \) and \( m \) swapped.
    Since any pair of functions in \( \ran F \) are compatible, it follows that \( \ran F \) is a compatible family of functions.
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-a-family-of-functions-is-compatible-iff-its-union-is-a-function}, \( f = \Union F = \Union \ran F \) must be a function.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:domain-of-f-is-either-N-or-k-where-k-is-an-element-of-N}
        Either \( \dom f = \naturalset \) or \( \dom f = k \) for some \( k \in \naturalset \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Since \( f = \Union F \), the domain of \( f \) depends on the domain of \( F \).
    It has been shown that either \( \dom F = \naturalset \) or \( \dom F = j + 1 \) for some \( j \in \naturalset \) where \( \orderedpair{F(j)}{j} \notin \dom G \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    First, we consider the case where \( \dom F = \naturalset \).
    Let \( m \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Since \( \naturalset \) is inductive, it follows that \( m + 1 \) is also an element of \( \naturalset \).
    Using \cref{claim:for-all-n-in-N-dom-F-n-is-n}, we deduce that \( \dom F_{m + 1} = \mleft( m + 1 \mright) \).
    Since \( m \in \mleft( m + 1 \mright) \), it follows that \( m \in \dom F_{m + 1} \). In other words, there exists a
    \( y \) such that \( \orderedpair{m}{y} \in F_{m + 1} \).
    By the definition of union, it follows that \( \orderedpair{m}{y} \in f \), which in turn implies that \( m \in \dom f \).
    Thus, \( m \in \naturalset \) implies \( m \in \dom f \).
    Since \( m \) is arbitrary, it follows that \( \naturalset \subseteq \dom f \).
    Conversely, let \( q \) be an arbitrary element of \( \dom f \); that is, \( \orderedpair{q}{r} \in f \) for some \( r \in S \).
    Again, the definition of union implies that there exists some \( n \in \naturalset \) such that \( \orderedpair{q}{r} \in F_{n} \).
    But this would imply \( q \in n \) because \( q \in \dom F_{n} \) and \( \dom F_{n} = n \) by \cref{claim:for-all-n-in-N-dom-F-n-is-n}.
    Moreover, \( n \subset \naturalset \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N}.
    Since \( q \in n \) and \( n \) is a proper subset of \( \naturalset \), necessarily \( q \in \naturalset \).
    In other words, \( q \in \dom f\) implies \( q \in \naturalset \).
    Thus, \( \dom f \subseteq \naturalset \).
    Since \( \dom f \) and \( \naturalset \) are subsets of each other, they must be equal sets by \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, we consider the case where \( \dom F = j + 1 \) for some \( j \in \naturalset \) such that \( \orderedpair{F_{j}}{j} \notin \dom G \), in which
    case we can show that \( \dom f = j \).
    Let \( m \) be an arbitrary element of \( \dom f \); that is, \( \orderedpair{m}{y} \in f \) for some \( y \in S \).
    By the definition of unions, it follows that \( \orderedpair{m}{y} \in F_{i} \) for some \( i \in \dom F \).
    Moreover, \( m \in i \) because \( m \in \dom F_{i} \) and \( \dom F_{i} = i \) by \cref{claim:for-all-n-in-N-dom-F-n-is-n}.
    Since \( \dom F = j + 1 \), it follows that either \( i \in j \) or \( i = j \).
    But for either case, \( m \in j \) is implied.
    In other words, \( m \in \dom f \) implies \( m \in j \); that is, \( \dom f \subseteq j \).
    Conversely, let \( q \) be an arbitrary element of \( j \).
    Observe that \( q \in \dom F_{j} \) must follow because \( \dom F_{j} = j \) by \cref{claim:for-all-n-in-N-dom-F-n-is-n}.
    Therefore, there must exists some \( y \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{q}{y} \in F_{j} \).
    Since \( f = \Union F \), it follows from the definition of union that \( \orderedpair{q}{y} \in f \) and thus \( q \in \dom f \).
    From this, we infer that \( j \subseteq \dom f \).
    Since \( j \) and \( \dom f \) are subsets of each other, they are necessarily equal sets.
    In other words, there exists a \( k \in \naturalset \) such that \( \dom f = k \).

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:for-all-n-in-N-if-n-in-dom-f-then-n-must-also-be-in-dom-F}
        For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( n \in \dom f \) implies \( n \in \dom F \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( n^{\prime} \in \dom f \).
    Recall that \( \dom F \) is either \( \naturalset \) or an element of \( \naturalset \).
    If \( \dom F = \naturalset \), then \( n^{\prime} \in \dom F \) is trivially true since \( n^{\prime} \in \naturalset \).
    It remains to prove that \( n^{\prime} \in \dom F \) is also true when \( \dom F \in \naturalset \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \dom f \) by assumption, there exists a \( y^{\prime} \in S \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    But \( f = \Union F \), therefore \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in F_{m} \) for some \( m \in \dom F \).
    Since \( m \) and \( \dom F \) are both elements of \( \naturalset\), \( m \in \dom F \) implies \( m < \dom F \), which in turn implies \( m \subset \dom F \)
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-less-than-relation-of-natural-numbers-is-equivalent-to-proper-subset-relation}.
    Moreover, we know from \cref{claim:for-all-n-in-N-dom-F-n-is-n} that \( \dom F_{m} = m \) and thus \( n^{\prime} \in m \) because
    \( n^{\prime} \in \dom F_{m} \).
    But \( m \) is a subset of \( \dom F \), so if \( n^{\prime} \in m \), then \( n^{\prime} \) must also belong to \( \dom F \).
    Evidently, \( n^{\prime} \in \dom F \) follows in every possible scenario.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, n^{\prime} \in \dom f \ \text{implies} \ n^{\prime} \in \dom F
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the claim follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:for-all-n-in-dom-f-f-restricted-to-n-equals-the-value-of-F-at-n}
        For all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( n \in \dom f \) implies \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} = F_{n} \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Let \( P(n) \) be a predicate defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq n \in \dom f \ \text{implies} \ \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} = F_{n}
    \end{equation*}
    We must prove that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \), which can be done by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction}.
    The proof for the base case is straightforward since \( \emptyset \in \dom f \) and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{\emptyset} = \emptyset = \tuple{} = F_{0} \)
    by \eqref{eq:values-of-F-2}.
    Now let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Clearly, either \( n^{\prime} \in \dom f \) or \( n^{\prime} \notin \dom f \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( n^{\prime} \in \dom f \), then necessarily \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} = F_{n^{\prime}} \)
    because \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true by assumption.
    Note that \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) may or may not be an element of \( \dom f \).
    If \( n^{\prime} + 1 \notin \dom f \), then \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) is vacuously true.
    If \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom f \), then necessarily \( n^{\prime} \subset n^{\prime} + 1 \subset \dom f \)
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-less-than-relation-of-natural-numbers-is-equivalent-to-proper-subset-relation,thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-subset-of-N}
    since \( n^{\prime} \) and \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) are elements of \( \naturalset \) and \( \dom f  \) is either \( \naturalset \) or an element of \( \naturalset \).
    Since \( f \) is a function and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} = F_{n^{\prime}} \), we have
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:f-restricted-to-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form-2}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime} + 1} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{f(n^{\prime})} \mright\}
        = F_{n^{\prime}} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    for some \( y^{\prime} \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom f \), \( n^{\prime} + 1 \) must also belong to \( \dom F \)
    as demonstrated in \cref{claim:for-all-n-in-N-if-n-in-dom-f-then-n-must-also-be-in-dom-F}.
    In other words, \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) is defined and
    \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = G(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}) \) by \eqref{eq:values-of-F-2};
    that is, \( \orderedpair{\orderedpair{F_{n^{\prime}}}{n^{\prime}}}{F_{n^{\prime} + 1}} \in G \).
    This means that \( \psi_{2}(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime}, F_{n^{\prime} + 1}) \) must be true and thus
    \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\} \).
    Observe that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \in F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).
    Since \( f = \Union F \) and \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \), necessarily \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \in f \).
    But \( f \) is a function as demonstrated in \cref{claim:f-is-a-function}, which means that \( y^{\prime} \) and \( g(F_{n^{\prime}}) \)
    must be equal since both \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \) and \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \) are elements of \( f \).
    Since \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\} \)
    and \( y^{\prime} = g(F_{n^{\prime}}) \), we conclude that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:F-at-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form}
        F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    Comparing \eqref{eq:f-restricted-to-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form-2} and \eqref{eq:F-at-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form},
    it is easy to see that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \).
    In other words, \( P(n^{\prime} + 1) \) follows from \( P(n^{\prime}) \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the inductive step follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:the-value-of-f-at-n-is-the-value-of-g-at-f-restricted-to-n-for-all-n-in-dom-f}
        For all \( n \in \dom f \), \( f(n) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} ) \)
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Let \( n^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \dom f \).
    Therefore, \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) for some \( y^{\prime} \in S \).
    Since \( f = \Union F \), there exists some \( m \in \dom F \) such that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in F_{m} \).
    Note that \( \dom F_{m} = m \) by \cref{claim:for-all-n-in-N-dom-F-n-is-n}, so \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in F_{m} \)
    implies \( n^{\prime} \in m \).
    It follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-m-less-than-n-implies-m-plus-one-equals-n-or-m-plus-one-less-than-n}
    that either \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m \) or \( n^{\prime} + 1 = m \) is true.
    If \( n^{\prime} + 1 = m \), then obviously \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \) since \( m \in \dom F \).
    If \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m \), then we must consider two possibilities: \( \dom F = \naturalset \) or
    \( \dom F \in \naturalset \).
    If \( \dom F = \naturalset \), clearly \( m \in \naturalset \) because \( m \in \dom F \).
    Given that \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m \), \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-the-set-of-all-elements-of-N-that-is-less-than-itself}
    implies that \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \naturalset \) and thus \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \).
    If \( \dom F \in \naturalset \), then \( m \) must be a proper subset of \( \dom F \)
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-less-than-relation-of-natural-numbers-is-equivalent-to-proper-subset-relation} because \( m < \dom F \).
    Once again, this implies that \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \) because \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in m \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Evidently, \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \) is true in all possible scenarios.
    In other words, \( F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) is defined.
    By \eqref{eq:values-of-F-2}, it follows that
    \begin{equation*}
        F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = G(F_{n^{\prime}}, n^{\prime})
    \end{equation*}
    Applying the definition of \( G \) then yields
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:F-at-n-prime-plus-one-union-form}
        F_{n^{\prime} + 1} = F_{n^{\prime}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    We then infer from \eqref{eq:F-at-n-prime-plus-one-in-union-form} and the definition of union that \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \in f \) since
    \( \orderedpair{n^{\prime}}{g(F_{n^{\prime}})} \in F_{n^{\prime} + 1} \) and \( n^{\prime} + 1 \in \dom F \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        f(n^{\prime}) = g(F_{n^{\prime}})
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( n^{\prime} \in \dom f \), necessarily \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} = F_{n^{\prime}} \)
    as demonstrated in \cref{claim:for-all-n-in-dom-f-f-restricted-to-n-equals-the-value-of-F-at-n}.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, f(n^{\prime}) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n^{\prime}} )
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( n^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, the claim follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Cardinality}

\begin{dfn}[Cardinality]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-cardinality-of-finite-sets}
    The \keyword{cardinality} of a finite set \( S \) (denoted as \( \abs{S} \)) is the unique natural number \( n \) such that
    a bijection exists between \( n \) and \( S \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    The concept of cardinality is essentially the mathematical definition of the intuitive notion of counting.
    When we say that a set \( S \) contains \( 5 \) objects, what we're really saying is that a bijection
    exists between the natural number \( 5 \coloneq \mleft\{ 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 \mright\} \) and the set \( S \).
\end{rem}

\noindent
For \cref{dfn:metatheory-cardinality-of-finite-sets} to truly capture the essence of counting, it is crucial for the definition of cardinality
to satisfy the uniqueness property; that is, every finite set must have \emph{exactly one} cardinality.
After all, it would be absurd to claim that ``\( S \) contains 5 objects'' and ``\( S \) contains 6 objects'' are both true at
the same time.
The following theorems will serve as a justification for the uniqueness claim in \cref{dfn:metatheory-cardinality-of-finite-sets}.

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-no-bijection-exists-between-a-natural-number-and-its-proper-subset}
    If \( n \in \naturalset \) and \( S \) is a set such that \( S \subset n \), then no bijection exists between \( S \) and \( n \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let the predicate \( P(n) \) be defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{ \( n \in \naturalset \) and for all set \( S \) such that \( S \subset n \), no bijection exists between \( S \) and \( n \)}
    \end{equation*}

    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(0) \) is vacuously true since \( 0 = \emptyset \) and no proper subset of \( \emptyset \) exists.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Inductive Step}: \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \) for all \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( k \) be any element of \( \naturalset \) such that \( P(k) \) is true.
    Let \( S \) be any set such that \( S \subset \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \).
    Since \( k \in \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \) and every element of \( S \) is an element of \( k + 1 \), it follows that either \( k \in S \) or \( k \notin S \) is true.
    We will consider each case in turn and show that \( P(k + 1) \) is implied in all cases.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case} \( k \in S \):

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Clearly \( S \neq \emptyset \) because it contains \( k \) as an element.
    Since \( S \subset \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \), there must exists a \( \beta \in \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \) such that \( \beta \neq k \) and \( \beta \notin S \).
    Since \( \beta \in \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \) and \( \beta \neq k \), it follows that \( \beta \in k \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that a bijection \( \bijection{f}{\mleft( k + 1 \mright)}{S} \) exists between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \).
    Since \( k \in S \) and \( \ran f = S \), it follows that \( k \in \ran f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, there exists some \( j \in \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \) such that \( \orderedpair{j}{k} \in f \).
    Since \( j \in \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \), there are only two possibilities to consider: \( j = k \) or \( j \in k \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( j = k \), then \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{k} \) is clearly a bijection between \( k \) and \( S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \).
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary element of \( S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-difference-of-two-sets}, it follows that \( \alpha \in S \) and \( \alpha \neq k \).
    Recall that \( S \subseteq \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \).
    Therefore, \( \alpha \in S \) implies \( \alpha \in k \) or \( \alpha = k \).
    But it has already been established that \( \alpha \neq k \), so \( \alpha \in k \) must follow by \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}.
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in S - \mleft\{ k \mright\}  \) implies \( x \in k \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, \( S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \subseteq k\) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}.
    Recall also that \( \beta \) is an element of \( k \) such that \( \beta \notin S \).
    Therefore \( \beta \notin S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \subset k \).
    Since \( P(k) \) is true, it follows that no bijection can exist between \( k \) and \( S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \).
    But the restricted function \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{k} \) is such a bijection.
    In other words, the supposition that a bijection exists between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \) inevitably leads to a contradiction and must therefore be false.
    Thus, no bijection exists between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( j \in k \), then we proceed by defining a set \( g \coloneq \setbuilder{\orderedpair{x}{y}}{x \in k \ \text{and} \ \disjunction{\varphi(x, y)}} \) where
    \( \varphi(x, y) \) is a sequence of two predicates such that
    \begin{alignat*}{2}
        &\varphi_{0}(x, y) &&\coloneq \text{\( x = j \) and \( \orderedpair{k}{y} \in f \)}\\
        &\varphi_{1}(x, y) &&\coloneq \text{\( x \neq j \) and \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f \)}
    \end{alignat*}
    We now prove that \( g \) is a function such that \( \dom g = k \) and \( \ran g = \mleft( S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \mright) \union \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\}  \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( x^{\prime} \), \( y^{\prime} \), and \( z^{\prime} \) be arbitrary objects
    such that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in g \).
    It follows from the definition of \( g \) that \( x^{\prime} \in k \),
    \( \disjunction{\varphi(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \), and \( \disjunction{\varphi(x^{\prime}, z^{\prime})} \) are all true.
    Since \( x^{\prime} \) and \( j \) both belong to \( k \), we need to consider two possibilities: either \( x^{\prime} = j \) or \( x^{\prime} \neq j \).
    If \( x^{\prime} = j \), then neither \( \varphi_{1}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) nor \( \varphi_{1}(x^{\prime}, z^{\prime}) \) can be true.
    By \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}, it follows that both \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) and \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \)
    must be true; that is, \( \orderedpair{k}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{k}{z^{\prime}} \in f \) are true.
    Since \( f \) is a function, \( y^{\prime} \) and \( z^{\prime} \) must be equal.
    If \( x^{\prime} \neq j \), we can use a similar line of reasoning to infer that \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) and
    \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in f \). Again, \( y^{\prime} = z^{\prime} \) follows readily from the fact that \( f \) is a function.
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \ \text{and} \ \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}} \in g \ \text{implies} \ y^{\prime} = z^{\prime}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( g \) satisfies both criteria stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}, \( g \) must be a function.
    To prove that \( \dom g = k \), it suffices to prove that \( k \subseteq \dom g \) since \( \dom g \subseteq k \) is an immediate consequence of
    the definition of \( g \).
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary element of \( k \).
    Since \( j \in k \), either \( \alpha = j \) or \( \alpha \neq j \).
    If \( \alpha = j \), then \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{f(k)} \in g \) so that \( \alpha \in \dom g \).
    Similarly, if \( \alpha \neq j \), the ordered pair \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{f(\alpha)} \) belongs to \( g \) and \( \alpha \in \dom g \).
    In either case, \( \alpha \in \dom g \) follows. Thus, \( \alpha \in k \) implies \( \alpha \in \dom g \); i.e., \( k \subseteq \dom g \) as needed.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It should be clear from the definition of \( g \) that \( \ran g = \mleft( \ran f - \mleft\{ f(j) \mright\} \mright) \union \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\} \).
    Since \( \ran f = S \) and \( f(j) = k \), we thus have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:range-of-g}
        \ran g = \mleft( S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \mright) \union \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    Since \( f \) is a bijection and \( f(j) = k \), necessarily \( f(k) \neq k \);
    otherwise, \( f(j) = k = f(k)\) would imply \( j = k \), which contradicts the fact that \( j \in k \).
    Also, \( f(k) \in S \) because \( f(k) \in \ran f \) and \( \ran f = S \).
    Now let \( \xi \) be an arbitrary element of \( \ran g \).
    From \eqref{eq:range-of-g}, it follows that either \( \xi \in S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \) or \( \xi = f(k) \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \xi \in S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \), then necessarily \( \xi \in S \) and \( \xi \neq k \).
    But \( S \subset \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \), so it follows that either \( \xi \in k \) or \( \xi = k \).
    The latter is impossible since \( \xi \neq k \) has already been established.
    Therefore, \( \xi \in k \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( \xi = f(k) \), then \( \xi \in \ran f \) and thus \( \xi \in S \).
    Again, this implies that \( \xi \in \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \) because \( S \subset \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \).
    It follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-successor} that either \( \xi \in k \) or \( \xi = k \) is true.
    But it has already been proven that \( f(k) \neq k \).
    Since \( \xi = f(k) \), necessarily \( \xi \neq k \).
    Thus, \( \xi \in k \) must be true by \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Evidently, \( \xi \in k \) is true regardless of whether \( \xi \in S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \) or \( \xi = f(k) \).
    In other words, \( \xi \in \ran g \) implies \( \xi \in k \). Since \( \xi \) is arbitrary, we conclude from
    \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that \( \ran g \subseteq k \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Recall that \( \beta \notin S \) and \( \beta \in k \).
    From this, we can then infer \( f(k) \neq \beta \) and \( \beta \notin S - \mleft\{ k \mright\} \).
    By \eqref{eq:range-of-g}, it follows that \( \beta \notin \ran g \).
    In other words, \( \beta \) is an element of \( k \) that does not belong to \( \ran g \).
    Since \( \ran g \subseteq k \) and there exists an element in \( k \) that is not an element of \( \ran g \),
    necessarily \( \ran g \subset k \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Furthermore, it can be shown that \( g \) is necessarily injective.
    Let \( x^{\prime} \), \( x^{\prime\prime} \), and \( y^{\prime} \) be
    objects in the universe such that the statements
    \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \)
    are both true.
    Using the definition of \( g \), we infer that \( x^{\prime} \) and \( x^{\prime\prime} \) are both elements of \( k \)
    and \( \disjunction{\varphi(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \) and \( \disjunction{\varphi(x^{\prime\prime}, y^{\prime})} \)
    are both true.
    Recall that \( j \in k \). Thus, either \( x^{\prime} = j \) or \( x^{\prime} \neq j \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( x^{\prime} = j \), then \( \varphi_{1}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) cannot be true.
    This means that \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) must be true; otherwise, \( \disjunction{\varphi(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime})} \)
    will be false.
    Since \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is true, it follows that \( \orderedpair{k}{y^{\prime}} \in f \),
    in which case \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) cannot be true because it would imply \( x^{\prime\prime} = k \) otherwise
    due to \( f \) being injective.
    This in turn means that \( \varphi_{1}(x^{\prime\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) cannot be true and thus \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime\prime}, y^{\prime}) \)
    is true; i.e., \( x^{\prime\prime} = j = x^{\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( x^{\prime} \neq j \), then \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) cannot be true and thus
    \( \varphi_{1}(x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is necessarily true.
    This means \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Note also that \( \varphi_{0}(x^{\prime\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) cannot be true; otherwise
    \( \orderedpair{k}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) would be true and \( x^{\prime} = k \) follows from
    the fact that \( f \) is injective---but \( x^{\prime} \) cannot be equal to \( k \) because \( x^{\prime} \in \dom g \) and \( \dom g = k \).
    Thus, \( \varphi_{1}(x^{\prime\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is true and \( \orderedpair{x^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in f \) follows.
    Since \( f \) is injective, necessarily \( x^{\prime} = x^{\prime\prime} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It has been shown that \( x^{\prime} = x^{\prime\prime} \) in all possible cases.
    Since \( x^{\prime} \), \( x^{\prime\prime} \), and \( y^{\prime} \) are arbitrary, it follows that \( g \)
    is injective.
    This means that \( g \) is a bijection between \( k \) and \( \ran g \).
    But it has already been shown that \( \ran g \subset k \) and no bijection can exist between \( k \) and \( \ran g \)
    because \( P(k) \) is true.
    In other words, the supposition that a bijection \( f \) exists between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \) will lead to a contradiction.
    Therefore, no bijection can exist between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case}: \( k \notin S \)

    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \noindent
    Since \( k \in \naturalset \), it follows that either \( k = \emptyset \) or \( k \neq \emptyset \).
    If \( k = \emptyset \), then \( k + 1 = \mleft\{ \emptyset \mright\} \).
    Therefore, \( S \subset \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \) can only be true if \( S = \emptyset \).
    This means that a bijection between \( S \) and \( k + 1 \) is impossible.
    On the other hand, if \( k \neq \emptyset \), then \( k + 1 \) is necessarily non-empty whereas \( S \) can either by
    empty or non-empty.
    If \( S = \emptyset \), then once again a bijection between \( k + 1 \) (non-empty) and \( S \) (empty) cannot exist because no bijection can be formed between an empty set
    and a non-empty set.
    If \( S \neq \emptyset \), we can show that a bijection between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \) inevitably leads to a contradiction.
    We proceed as follows.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that \( S \neq \emptyset \) and that a bijection \( \bijection{f}{\mleft( k + 1 \mright)}{S} \) exists between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \).
    Restricting \( f \) to \( k \) would then yield a bijection between \( k \) and \( S - \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\} \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary element of \( S \).
    Since \( k \notin S \), it follows that \( \alpha \neq k \).
    But \( S \subseteq \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \), so \( \alpha \in S \) necessarily implies \( \alpha \in k \) or \( \alpha = k \).
    It has already been established that \( \alpha \neq k \), so \( \alpha \in k \) follows from \cref{meta-rule:disjunctive-syllogism}.
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in S \) implies \( x \in k \)}
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, \( S \subseteq k \) and, by extension, \( S - \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\} \subseteq k \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Recall that \( \ran f = S \). Again, the fact that \( k \notin S \) and \( S \subseteq \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \)
    means that \( f(k) \in \mleft( k + 1 \mright) \) and \( f(k) \neq k \) are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-successor}, we conclude that \( f(k) \in k \).
    So far, we have established the following:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt, label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( S - \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\} \subseteq k \)
        \item \( f(k) \) is an element of \( k \) that does not belong to \( S - \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\} \)
    \end{enumerate}
    
    \vspace{0.2cm}
    \noindent
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( S - \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\} \subset k \).
    Since \( P(k) \) is true, a bijection between \( S - \mleft\{ f(k) \mright\} \) and \( k \) cannot exist.
    But \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{k} \) is such a bijection, so we have arrived at a contradiction.
    By \cref{meta-rule:reductio-ad-absurdum}, our initial supposition must be false.
    Thus, no bijection can exist between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It has been shown that, in all possible scenarios, no bijection can exist between \( k + 1 \) and \( S \).
    Since \( S \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that \( P(k + 1) \) is true.
    Since \( k \) is an arbitrary natural number, the inductive step follows immediately from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-no-element-of-N-can-form-bijections-with-N-itself}
    If \( n \in \naturalset \), then no bijection exists between \( n \) and \( \naturalset \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( k \) be an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that a bijection exists between \( k \) and \( \naturalset \); that is, there exists some function \( f \)
    such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \bijection{f}{\naturalset}{k}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( k \in \naturalset \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-natural-number-is-a-proper-subset-of-N} that
    \( k \subset \naturalset \) (and thus \( k \subseteq \naturalset \)).
    We can then infer from \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-a-bijection-to-a-subset-of-the-domain-of-the-function} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \bijection{ \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{k} }{k}{ \mleft(  k - \imageSet{f}{\naturalset - k} \mright) }
    \end{equation*}
    Note that \( \naturalset - k \neq \emptyset \) since \( k + 1 \) is an element of \( \naturalset \) that does not belong to \( k \).
    Let \( i \) denote an element of \( \naturalset - k \).
    Clearly \( i \in \naturalset \), so it follows that \( i \in \dom f \).
    In other words, there exists some \( j \in \ran f \) such that \( \orderedpair{i}{j} \in f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, it follows that \( j \in \imageSet{f}{\naturalset - k} \).
    Since \( j \in \imageSet{f}{\naturalset - k} \) is true, necessarily \( j \notin k - \imageSet{f}{\naturalset - k} \).
    But \( \ran f = k \), which means that \( j \) must belong to \( k \).
    In other words, \( j \) is an element of \( k \) that does not belong to \( k - \imageSet{f}{\naturalset - k} \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \mleft( k - \imageSet{f}{\naturalset - k} \mright) \subset k
    \end{equation*}
    This contradicts \cref{thm:metatheory-no-bijection-exists-between-a-natural-number-and-its-proper-subset}, which states that
    no bijection can be formed between a natural number and its proper subset.
    Therefore, our initial supposition that a bijection exists between \( k \) and \( \naturalset \) must be false.
    Since \( k \) is arbitrary, the theorem can be established via \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-every-subset-of-a-natural-number-can-form-bijections-with-a-natural-number-less-than-or-equal-to-the-number}
    If \( n \in \naturalset \) and \( S \) is a set such that \( S \subseteq n \), then there exists a natural number \( k \leq n \) such that
    a bijection exists between \( k \) and \( S \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    If \( S = \emptyset \), then the proof is trivial since the empty function is a bijection between \( S \) and \( 0 \).
    Now we consider the case where \( S \neq \emptyset \).
    Let \( \lambda(X, y) \) be a predicate defined as follows:
    \begin{equation*}
        \lambda(X, y) \coloneq \ \text{\( X \) is a non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \) and \( y \in X \) such that for all \( z \in X \), \( y \leq z \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( \psi(X, y) \) be a sequence of predicates such that
    \begin{alignat*}{2}
        &\psi_{0}(X, y) &&\coloneq \text{\( X \in \finiteseq{S} \) and \( X \) is injective}\\
        &\psi_{1}(X, y) &&\coloneq \lambda(S - \ran X, y)
    \end{alignat*}
    Now let \( g \) be the set
    \begin{equation*}
        g \coloneq \setbuilderbig{\orderedpair{X}{y}}{\conjunction{\psi(X, y)}}
    \end{equation*}
    We begin by proving that \( g \) is a function.
    Let \( X^{\prime} \), \( y^{\prime} \), and \( y^{\prime\prime} \) be objects in the universe such that
    \( \orderedpair{X^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \) and \( \orderedpair{X^{\prime}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \in g \).
    Thus, \( \lambda(S - \ran X^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) and \( \lambda(S - \ran X^{\prime}, y^{\prime\prime}) \) are both true.
    Clearly, \( y^{\prime} \leq y^{\prime\prime} \) and \( y^{\prime\prime} \leq y^{\prime} \) are both true.
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-antisymmetric-property-of-membership-relation-of-N}, it follows that \( y^{\prime} = y^{\prime\prime} \).
    In other words, \( g \) is a function.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, we show that \( \dom g \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \) and \( \ran g \subseteq S \).
    Let \( X^{\prime} \) be an arbitrary element of \( \dom g \).
    Thus, there exists a \( y^{\prime} \) such that \( \orderedpair{X^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}} \in g \).
    But this would imply that \( \psi_{0}(X^{\prime}, y^{\prime}) \) is true.
    Thus, \( X^{\prime} \in \finiteseq{S} \).
    Since \( X^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that \( \dom g \subseteq \finiteseq{S} \).
    Let \( y^{\prime\prime} \) an arbitrary element of \( \ran g \).
    Thus, there exists an \( X^{\prime\prime} \) such that \( \orderedpair{X^{\prime\prime}}{y^{\prime\prime}} \in g \).
    It follows from the definitions of \( \psi \) and \( \lambda \) that \( y^{\prime\prime} \in S - \ran X^{\prime\prime} \).
    Clearly, \( y^{\prime\prime} \in S \).
    Therefore, \( \ran g \subseteq S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We have thus proven that \( g \) is a function on a subset of \( \finiteseq{S} \) into \( S \) where \( S \) is a non-empty set.
    This means that \cref{recursion-theorem:generalized-sequence-form} applies and there exists a unique function \( f \) such that
    \( f(n) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{n} ) \) for all \( n \in \dom f \).
    Furthermore, either \( \dom f = \naturalset \) or \( \dom f = k \) for some \( k \in \naturalset \) such that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{k} \notin \dom g \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We now prove the following useful claims.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:dom-f-cannot-be-empty}
        \( \dom f \neq \emptyset \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Suppose that \( \dom f = \emptyset \).
    This would mean that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{\emptyset} = \emptyset \) is not an element of \( \dom g \), which is only possible
    if \( S = \emptyset \).
    This contradicts our initial assumption that \( S \neq \emptyset \).
    Thus, \( \dom f \) cannot be empty.

    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:f-zero-equals-least-element-of-S}
        \( 0 \in \dom f \) and \( f(0) \) is the least element of \( S \).
    \end{claim}

    \noindent
    Since \( \dom f \) is either \( \naturalset \) or a non-empty element of \( \naturalset \),
    it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-every-nonzero-natural-number-contains-the-empty-set} that \( \emptyset \in \dom f \).
    Due to \( S \) being non-empty subset of \( \naturalset \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-well-ordering-of-natural-numbers}
    that \( S \) has a least element.
    Since \( 0 \in \dom f \), there exists some \( y^{\prime} \) such that \( \orderedpair{0}{y^{\prime}} \in f \).
    Furthermore, \( y^{\prime} = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{0} ) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{\emptyset} ) = g(\emptyset) \).
    In other words, \( \orderedpair{\emptyset}{y^{\prime}} \in g \), which means that \( \lambda(S - \ran \emptyset, y^{\prime}) \) must be true
    and thus \( y^{\prime} \) must be the least element of \( S - \ran \emptyset = S - \emptyset = S \).

    \noindent
    \begin{claim}
        \phantomsection \label{claim:for-all-n-in-dom-f-and-n-plus-one-in-dom-f-f-n-less-than-f-n-plus-one}
        For all \( n \in \dom f \), if \( n + 1 \in \dom f \), then \( f(n) < f(n + 1) \).
    \end{claim}
    \noindent
    Since \( \dom f \) is either \( \naturalset \) or an element of \( \naturalset \), we can prove the claim by induction.
    Let \( P(n) \) be a predicate defined as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        P(n) \coloneq \text{if \( n + 1 \in \dom f \), then \( f(n) < f(n + 1) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Base Case}: \( P(0) \) is true.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    If \( 0 + 1 \notin \dom f \), then \( P(0) \) is vacuously true.
    If \( 0 + 1 \in \dom f \), then \( f(1) = g( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{1} ) = g( \mleft\{ \orderedpair{0}{l} \mright\} ) \)
    where \( l \) is the least element of \( S \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-a-bijection-exists-between-two-natural-numbers-iff-they-are-equal}
    For all \( n \in \naturalset \) and \( m \in \naturalset \), a bijection exists between \( n \) and \( m \) iff \( n = m \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( n \) and \( m \) be arbitrary elements of \( \naturalset \) such that a bijection exists between \( n \) and \( m \).
    Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that \( n \neq m \).
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-linear-ordering-of-natural-numbers}, it follows that either \( n < m  \) or \( m < n \).
    If \( n < m \), then necessarily \( n \subset m \) by \cref{thm:metatheory-less-than-relation-of-natural-numbers-is-equivalent-to-proper-subset-relation}.
    But if \( n \subset m \), \cref{thm:metatheory-no-bijection-exists-between-a-natural-number-and-its-proper-subset} states that no bijection exists between
    \( n \) and \( m \), which contradicts our initial hypothesis that such a bijection exists.
    If \( m < n \), the same argument applies and leads to the same contradiction.
    This means that \( n \neq m \) cannot be true.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, n = m
    \end{equation*}
    The proof for the converse is trivial. If \( n = m \), then the identity function is clearly a bijection between \( n \) and \( m \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-a-set-can-form-a-bijection-with-at-most-one-natural-number}
    If \( S \) is a set and bijections exist between \( n \) and \( S \) and \( m \) and \( S \) where \( n, m \in \naturalset \),
    then \( n = m \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( f \) and \( g \) be bijections between \( n \) and \( S \) and \( m \) and \( S \) respectively.
    More precisely,
    \begin{align*}
        \bijection{f}{n}{S} \\
        \bijection{g}{m}{S}
    \end{align*}
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-inverse-of-bijection-is-another-bijection,thm:metatheory-the-composition-of-injective-functions-is-also-injective},
    it follows that \( \bijection{g^{-1}}{S}{m} \) and \( \bijection{\composition{f}{g}}{n}{m} \).
    Since \( \composition{f}{g} \) is a bijection between two natural numbers \( n \) and \( m \),
    \cref{thm:metatheory-a-bijection-exists-between-two-natural-numbers-iff-they-are-equal} guarantees that \( n = m \).
\end{proof}

\begin{rem}
The purpose of this section is to develop (in our metalanguage) just enough of set theory to be useable in the formulation of first-order logic.
In the next section, we will continue our development of the metatheory of logic by defining (in terms of the concepts that we have just devloped)
many of the concepts in logic.
After the essential concepts of logic have been defined, we will proceed to prove the familiar properties of first-order logic, many of which we already
know intuitively.
\end{rem}

\newpage
\subsection{First-Order Languages}
Suppose we include in our universe a non-empty, countable set \( \Sigma \) of symbolic entities---i.e., glyphs and characters that can be drawn, written, or printed
on a sheet of paper.
If we now take \( \mathcal{F} \) to be any non-empty subset of \( \finiteseq{\Sigma} \), then we say that \( \mathcal{F} \) is a \keyword{formal language} over \( \Sigma \)
and that \( \Sigma \) is the \keyword{alphabet} of \( \mathcal{F} \).
Although it is possible (at least in principle) to pick arbitrary elements of \( \finiteseq{\Sigma} \) to include in \( \mathcal{F} \), it is more common for
\( \mathcal{F} \) to be defined using a set of precise rules called the \keyword{syntax} of \( \mathcal{F} \).

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
A great number of formal languages have been invented throughout history, each of which requires a separate book to expound on.
For our purposes, we will restrict ourselves to a class of formal languages that are particularly suited to express mathematical statements: \keyword{first-order languages}.

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language}
    The alphabet of a first-order language \( \mathcal{L} \) is a set \( \Sigma \) consisting of:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item Logical Symbols:
            \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\arabic*.]
                \item Parentheses: \( () \, , \,[ \, ] \)
                \item Sentential Connectives\footnote{All the other familiar connectives can be defined in terms of \( \lnot \), \( \land \), and \( \lor \).}:
                    \( \lnot \), \( \land \), \( \lor \)
                \item Quantifiers: \( \forall \), \( \exists \)
                \item Equality(optional): \( = \)
                \item Variables: \( v_{0}, v_{1}, \dots \)
            \end{enumerate}
        \item Non-Logical Symbols:
            \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\arabic*.]
                \item Predicates: the elements of \( \ran P^{\mathcal{L}} \) where \( P^{\mathcal{L}} \) is a tuple of predicate symbols
                \item Functions: the elements of \( \ran F^{\mathcal{L}} \) where \( F^{\mathcal{L}} \) is a tuple of function symbols
            \end{enumerate}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Arity Functions]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-arity-functions}
    The tuples \( P^{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( F^{\mathcal{L}} \) are associated with the functions
    \( \function{\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}}{\dom P^{\mathcal{L}}}{\naturalset^{+} } \) and
    \( \function{\Phi^{\mathcal{L}}}{\dom F^{\mathcal{L}}}{\naturalset} \) respectively.
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    The traditional ``constant'' symbols are treated as \( 0 \)-ary function symbols in this approach.
    That is why \( \ran \Phi^{\mathcal{L}} \) can be the entirety of \( \naturalset \) (which includes 0) whereas \( 0 \) must
    be excluded from the range of \( \Pi^{\mathcal{L}} \).
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-L-symbol}
    A symbol that belongs to the alphabet of \( \mathcal{L} \) is called an \( \mathcal{L} \)-symbol.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-L-variable}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-symbol that is also a variable symbol is called an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-L-string}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-string is a finite sequence of \( \mathcal{L} \)-symbols.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-L-expression}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-string that belongs to \( \mathcal{L} \).
    If \( \varepsilon \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression, then \( \folsymbols{ \varepsilon } \) denotes the set of all
    the \( \mathcal{L} \)-symbols consituting \( \varepsilon \). Essentially, \( \folsymbols{ \varepsilon } = \ran \varepsilon \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-the-set-of-all-variable-symbols-in-a-first-order-language}
    If \( \mathcal{L} \) is a first-order language, then \( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) denotes the (countable and non-empty) set of all \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables
    associated with \( \mathcal{L} \).
    If \( \varepsilon \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression, then \( \folvars{ \varepsilon } \) denotes the set of all \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables
    appearing in \( \varepsilon \). 
    \begin{equation*}
        \folvars{ \varepsilon } = \folsymbols{ \varepsilon } \,{\intersect}\, \folvars{\mathcal{L}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    It follows then that for any \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression \( \varepsilon \), \( \folvars{ \varepsilon } \subseteq \folsymbols{ \varepsilon } \)
    and \( \folvars{ \varepsilon } \subseteq \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \).
\end{cor}

\subsection{Syntax}
The syntax of first-order languages is relatively straightforward.
In first-order languages, the most basic unit of grammar is formulas---analogous to
clauses in natural languages. But just as clauses are composed of words (verbs, nouns, etc.) in that natural language,
formulas are also composed of smaller units called \emph{terms}.

\begin{dfn}[Terms]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-terms}
    An \aref{dfn:first-order-language-L-expression}{\( \mathcal{L} \)-expression} is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term if and only if
    it is constructible in a finite number of steps via the application of the following two rules:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression such that \( \folsymbols{t} = \mleft\{ x \mright\} \) and \( x \in \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \),
             then \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term
        \item If \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms such that \( \dom \tau = \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \),
             then \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Complexity]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-complexity-of-terms}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term, then the \keyword{complexity} of \( t \) (denoted as \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} \))
    is the number of occurrences of function symbols with non-zero arity in \( t \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that the complexity of a term counts the number of \emph{occurrences} of function symbols with non-zero arity, not just the number of such function symbols occurring
    in \( t \). For instance, a term \( F(G(x), G(y)) \) has a complexity of \( 3 \) not \( 2 \) even though only two function symbols occurred in it in total.
    The function symbol \( G \) needs to be counted twice.
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:first-order-language-every-term-has-finite-complexity}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term, then \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} \in \naturalset \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term, it follows from \cref{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-terms} that
    \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression.
    By \cref{dfn:first-order-language-L-expression}, \( t \) must be an \( \mathcal{L} \)-string.
    Since \aref{dfn:first-order-language-L-string}{all \( \mathcal{L} \)-string are finite sequences of the elements of \( \Sigma \),} it follows that
    \( \function{t}{k}{\Sigma} \) for some \( k \in \naturalset \).
\end{proof}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-terms-with-zero-complexity-is-either-a-variable-or-0-ary-function-symbol}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term and \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} = 0 \), then either \( t \) comprises of a single \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable
    or \( t \) has the form \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}() \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \) such that \( \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) = 0 \).
\end{cor}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-terms-with-non-zero-complexity-must-have-a-parent-function-symbol}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term and \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} \neq 0 \), then \( t \) must have the form
    \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \) where \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms
    and \( \abs{\tau} = \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \neq 0 \).
\end{cor}

\begin{eg}
    Thus, in a first-order language \( \mathcal{L} \) with function symbols \( F \), \( G \), and \( H \) (with arities 0, 1, and 2 respectively) and variable symbols
    \( x \), \( y \), and \( z \), we have the following:
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{x} = 0 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{F()} = 0 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{G(x)} = 1 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{H(F(), y)} = 1 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{H(G(x), z)} = 2 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{H(H(x, F()), y)} = 2 \).
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}[Subterms]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-subterms}
    If \( s \) and \( t \) denote \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms,
    then we say that \( s \) is a \keyword{subterm} of \( t \) (denoted as \( s \folsubterm t \))
    if and only if the following conditions are met:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( t \) has the form \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \) where \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms
            such that \( \abs{\tau} = \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \neq 0 \)
        \item \( s \in \ran \tau \) or \( s \folsubterm r \) for some \( r \in \ran \tau \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-the-complexity-of-a-subterm-is-less-than-the-complexity-of-the-parent-term}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term and \( s \) is a subterm of \( t \), then \( \folcomplexityofterm{s} < \folcomplexityofterm{t} \).
\end{cor}

\begin{dfn}[Atoms]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-atom}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression \( \varphi \) is an \keyword{atom} of \( \mathcal{L} \) if and only if
    \( \varphi \) has either one of the following forms:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( s = t \) where \( s \) and \( t \) are \aref{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-terms}{\( \mathcal{L} \)-terms}
        \item \( P^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom P^{\mathcal{L}} \) where
            \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms such that \( \abs{\tau} = \Pi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Formulas]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-formulas}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula\footnote{\,Also called a \keyword{well-formed formula} or wff in \( \mathcal{L} \).}
    if and only if it is constructible from atoms of \( \mathcal{L} \) in a finite number of steps via the application of the following rules:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an atom of \( \mathcal{L} \), then \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \mleft( \varphi \mright) \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \mleft[ \, \varphi \, \mright] \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \lnot \, \varphi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then \( \varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then \( \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( v \) is any \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable,
            then \( \forall v \, \varphi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( v \) is any \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable,
            then \( \exists v \, \varphi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    The familiar abbreviations from propositional logic do apply here, of course. For instance, if \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are
    \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then we write \( \varphi {\implies} \psi \) to abbreviate \( \lnot \, \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \),
    \( \varphi {\iff} \psi \) to abbreviate \( \mleft( \varphi {\implies} \psi \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( \psi {\implies} \varphi \mright)  \), and so on.
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Syntactic Operations]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-syntactic-operations}
    If \( \mathcal{L} \) is a first-order language, then \( \mathcal{R}_{\ast} \) denotes an operation that can be applied to formulas of \( \mathcal{L} \)
    to construct a new formulas of \( \mathcal{L} \).
    The operation \( \mathcal{R}_{\ast} \) is also called a \keyword{syntactic operation} of \( \mathcal{L} \).
    There are exactly 7 syntactic operations in our approach:  \( \mathcal{R}_{(} \), \( \mathcal{R}_{[} \),
    \( \mathcal{R}_{\lnot} \), \( \mathcal{R}_{\land} \), \( \mathcal{R}_{\lor} \), \( \mathcal{R}_{\forall} \), and \( \mathcal{R}_{\exists} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{eg}
    If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then \( \varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \) is the formula obtained by applying
    the syntactic operation \( \mathcal{R}_{\land} \) to \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \), \( \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) is the formula obtained
    by applying the syntactic operation \( \mathcal{R}_{\lor} \) to \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \), etc.
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}[Complexity]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-complexity-of-formulas}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then the \keyword{complexity} of \( \varphi \) (denoted as \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \))
    is the total number of syntactic operations required to construct \( \varphi \) from atoms of \( \mathcal{L} \).
    More precisely:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( \psi \mright) \), \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft[ \,\psi\, \mright] \), \( \varphi \coloneq \lnot \, \psi \),
            \( \varphi \coloneq \forall x \, \psi \), or \( \varphi \coloneq \exists x \, \psi \),
            then \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} = \folcomplexityofformula{\psi} + 1 \)
        \item If \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \phi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \phi \),
            then \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} = \folcomplexityofformula{\psi} + \folcomplexityofformula{\phi} + 1 \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-every-formula-has-finite-complexity}
    It follows immediately from \cref{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-formulas} that every \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula can be constructed
    from \aref{dfn:first-order-language-atom}{atoms of \( \mathcal{L} \)} via the application of a finite number of
    \aref{dfn:first-order-language-syntactic-operations}{syntactic operations}; that is, for every \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \varphi \),
    \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \in \naturalset \).
\end{cor}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-only-atoms-can-have-zero-complexity}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} = 0 \) iff \( \varphi \) is an atom.
\end{cor}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-every-formula-with-non-zero-complexity-can-be-decomposed}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula such that \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \neq 0 \), then exactly one of the following must be true:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( \psi \mright) \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft[ \, \psi \, \mright] \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \lnot \, \psi \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \phi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \phi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \)
            where \( \psi \) and \( \phi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \phi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \phi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) where \( \psi \) and \( \phi \)
            are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \forall x \, \psi \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( x \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \exists x \, \psi \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( x \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable
    \end{enumerate}
\end{cor}

\begin{eg}
    If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are atoms of \( \mathcal{L} \) and \( x \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable, then we have the following:
    \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} = 0 \), \( \folcomplexityofformula{\forall x \, \varphi} = 1\), \(  \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi} = 1 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofformula{\lnot \, \varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi} = 2 \),
    \(  \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi} = 2 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofformula{\mleft( \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi} = 3 \), etc.
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}[Subformulas]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-subformulas}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then any contiguous subsequence of \( \varphi \) that is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
    is called a \keyword{subformula} of \( \varphi \). More precisely, if \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then
    \( \psi \) is a subformula of \( \varphi \) (denoted as \( \psi \folsubformula \varphi \)) if and only if exactly one of the following conditions is met:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( \psi \mright) \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft[ \, \psi \, \mright] \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \lnot \, \psi \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \phi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \phi \) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \phi \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \phi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \phi\) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \phi \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \forall x \, \psi \) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable \( x \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \exists x \, \psi \) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable \( x \)
        \item \( \psi \folsubformula \phi \) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \phi \) such that \( \phi \folsubformula \varphi \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-the-complexity-of-a-subformula-is-less-than-the-complexity-of-the-formula}
    If \( \psi \folsubformula \varphi \), then \( \folcomplexityofformula{\psi} < \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \).
\end{cor}

\begin{rem}
    The converse of \cref{cor:first-order-language-the-complexity-of-a-subformula-is-less-than-the-complexity-of-the-formula} is obviously false.
    Here is a simple counter-example with \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( x = y \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( z \neq x \mright)  \) and \( \psi \coloneq \mleft( x \neq y \mright) \).
    Clearly, \( \folcomplexityofformula{\psi} = 1 < 4 = \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \), but \( \psi \) is \emph{not} a subformula of \( \varphi \).
\end{rem}

\begin{notation}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( \psi \folsubformula \varphi \), then \( \folsubformulasubstitution{\varphi}{\psi}{\phi} \)
    denotes the \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula obtained after every occurrence of \( \psi \) in \( \varphi \)
    has been replaced with \( \phi \).
\end{notation}

\begin{eg}
    If \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( x = y \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( z \neq x \mright) \,\,{\lor}\,\, \mleft( x = y \mright) \),
    \( \psi \coloneq \mleft( x = y \mright) \), and
    \( \phi \coloneq x \neq y \), then \( \folsubformulasubstitution{\varphi}{\psi}{\phi} = \mleft( x \neq y \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( z \neq x \mright) \,\,{\lor}\,\, \mleft( x \neq y \mright) \).
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-variable-occurrence-in-formulas}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( v \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable, then we say that ``\( v \) occurs in \( \varphi \),'' 
    ``\( v \) is in \( \varphi \),'' or ``\( \varphi \) contains \( v \)'' if and only if \( v \in \folvars{\varphi} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Free Variables]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-recursive-definition-of-free-variables}
    If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, \( x \) and \( v \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables, and \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables,
    then the following holds:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an atom of \( \mathcal{L} \), then \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) if and only if \( x \in \folvars{\varphi} \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \lnot \, \varphi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) or \( \psi \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) or \( \psi \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \forall v \, \varphi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) and \( x \neq v \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \exists v \, \varphi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) and \( x \neq v \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    If \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \), we also say that \( x \) \keyword{occurs freely} or \keyword{occurs free} in \( \varphi \).
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}[Bound Variables]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-bound-variables}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable occurring in \( \varphi \) that is not free is called a \keyword{bound variable} of \( \varphi \). 
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that it is possible for the same variable symbol to be both free and bound in the same formula.
    For example, the variable symbol \( x \) in the formula \( \forall x \, \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( x = y \mright) \)
    is both free and bound.
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-the-set-of-all-free-variables-bound-variables-of-a-formula}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \folfreevars{\varphi} \) denotes the set of all free variables of \( \varphi \) and
    \( \folboundvars{\varphi} \) denotes the set of all bound variables of \( \varphi \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-set-of-all-free-variables-is-a-subset-of-set-of-all-variables-the-definition-of-the-set-of-all-bound-variables}
    Naturally, \( \folfreevars{\varphi} \subseteq \folvars{\varphi} \) and \( \folboundvars{\varphi} = \folvars{\varphi} - \folfreevars{\varphi} \)
    for any \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \varphi \).
\end{cor}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-free-variable-replacement-in-formulas}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( x \), \( y \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables,
    then \( \folfreevariablereplacement{\varphi}{x}{y} \) denotes the formula obtained after all free occurrences of \( x \) in \( \varphi \) has been
    replaced with \( y \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    If \( \varphi \) has only one free variable \( x \), then we can abbreviate \( \folfreevariablereplacement{\varphi}{x}{y} \) with \( \varphi(y) \).
\end{notation}

\subsection{Semantics}
\noindent
The syntactic rules of \( \mathcal{L} \) are fully defined in the previous section;
that is, given any finite string of symbols in \aref{dfn:first-order-language}{\( \mathcal{L} \)}, we
now have the information at our disposal to determine whether that string of symbols is a valid formula of \( \mathcal{L} \).
Nevertheless, our description of \( \mathcal{L} \) is far from complete. For any language to be useful, having a bunch of symbols
and a set of rules to construct valid formulas using those symbols is not enough; there also needs to be a systematic way for
\emph{meaning} to be assigned to all valid formulas---just as English words like ``cats'', ``apples'', and ``oranges'' need to have meaning
for English to have any value at all as a language.

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
The assignment of meaning to the expressions of a formal language is called the \keyword{semantics} of a formal language. This typically involves
associating the symbols in the language (and the valid expressions constructed using those symbols) with existing concepts or mental imageries.
These mental imageries can be either concrete or abstract. For instance, when a native English speaker sees the word ``apple,'' their minds
construct the image of a fruit with red skin. In other words, the semantics of a language allows the language to be \keyword{interpreted};
it provides a connection between a string of seemingly arbitrary symbols and concepts from the real world.

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
In first-order languages, meaning is assigned to formulas via a special class of \aref{dfn:general-mathematical-structures}{mathematical structures}
known as \keyword{first-order structures}.

\begin{dfn}
    A \keyword{first-order structure} is any \aref{dfn:general-mathematical-structures}{mathematical structure} \( \mathfrak{A} \) such that
    \aref{notation:individual-components-of-mathematical-structures}{\( S^{\mathfrak{A}} = \emptyset \)}
    and \aref{notation:individual-components-of-mathematical-structures}{\( G^{\mathfrak{A}} = \emptyset \)}.
    In other words, a first-order structure is any mathematical structure that is endowed with no other features
    aside from n-ary \aref{dfn:n-ary-relation-on-a-set}{relations} and \aref{dfn:n-ary-operation}{operations}.
\end{dfn}

\noindent
Of course, not every first-order structure can be used to assign meaning to \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas.
In fact, for a first-order structure to be capable of interpreting formulas of \( \mathcal{L} \),
its \aref{dfn:signature-of-a-structure}{signature} must be compatible with the arity functions
\aref{dfn:first-order-language}{\( \Pi^{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( \Phi^{\mathcal{L}} \)},
which leads us to the following definition.

\begin{dfn}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure is any first-order structure with \aref{dfn:signature-of-a-structure}{signature}
    \( \tuple{\tuple{}, \Pi^{\mathcal{L}}, \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}, \tuple{}} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    \phantomsection \label{one-to-one-correspondences-between-first-order-language-and-first-order-structure}
    Note that \( F^{\mathcal{L}} \), \( P^{\mathcal{L}} \), \( F^{\mathfrak{A}} \), and \( R^{\mathfrak{A}} \) are all bijective tuples
    because the symbols associated with \( \mathcal{L} \) and \( \mathfrak{A} \) are bound to be unique.
    As a result, \( \composition{F^{\mathfrak{A}}}{\mleft( F^{\mathcal{L}} \mright)^{-1}} \) and
    \( \composition{R^{\mathfrak{A}}}{\mleft( P^{\mathcal{L}} \mright)^{-1}} \) are both bijections.
    In other words, there exist one-to-one correspondences between the function symbols of \( \mathcal{L} \) and the n-ary operations of \( \mathfrak{A} \)
    as well as between the predicate symbols of \( \mathcal{L} \) and the n-ary relation of \( \mathfrak{A} \).
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-variable-assignment-function}
    If \( \mathfrak{A} \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure, then a function \( \mathfrak{a} \) is a \keyword{variable assignment function}
    of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \) if and only if \( \function{\mathfrak{a}}{\folvars{\mathcal{L}}}{\abs{\mathfrak{A}}} \).
    In other words, any function on \( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) into \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \) is a variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-modified-variable-assignment-function}
    Let \( \mathfrak{A} \) be an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure and \( \function{\mathfrak{a}}{\folvars{\mathcal{L}}}{\abs{\mathfrak{A}}} \) be a variable assignment function of
    \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \).
    If \( v \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable and \( e \in \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \), then \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} \) denotes
    the function \( \restrictedfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ v \mright\} } \union \big\{ \orderedpair{v}{e} \big\} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} \) is still a variable assignment function because
    \( \dom \big( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} \big) = \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \)
    and \( \ran \big( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} \big) \subseteq \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:first-order-language-existence-of-variable-assignment-function}
    For any first-order language \( \mathcal{L} \) and any \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure \( \mathfrak{A} \),
    a variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \mathfrak{A} \) be an arbitrary \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure.
    Since \( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \) are non-empty sets,
    it is easy to define a function on \( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) into \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
    E.g., given an arbitrary element \( \alpha \) of \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \),
    let \( \mathfrak{a} \) be a function such that \( \dom \mathfrak{a} = \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( \mathfrak{a}(x) = \alpha \) for all \( x \in \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \).
    By \cref{dfn:first-order-language-variable-assignment-function}, \( \mathfrak{a} \) is a variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:first-order-language-the-last-modification-will-take-effect-if-the-same-variable-symbol-is-modified-for-a-variable-assignment-function}
    If \( \mathfrak{A} \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure, \( \mathfrak{a} \) is any variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \),
    and \( x \) is any \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{x}{e_{1}}
        = \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{1}}
    \end{equation*}
    for all \( e_{0}, e_{1} \in \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( e_{0} \) and \( e_{1} \) denote arbitrary elements of \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
    According to \cref{dfn:first-order-language-variable-assignment-function}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-mapsto-e0}
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{x}{e_{1}}
        = \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \union
        \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{1}} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    But \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \) so that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-x}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(  \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }
    \end{equation}
    Since \(  \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }  \) and \( \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \) are
    \aref{thm:the-union-of-a-compatible-system-of-functions-is-a-function}{compatible functions}, \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-union-of-compatible-functions-equals-the-union-of-restrictions}
    applies and \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-x} becomes
    \begin{align*}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-x-2}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } &=
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }
        \,{\union}\,
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\\
        &= \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \,{\union}\, \emptyset \\
        &= \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }
    \end{align*}
    But \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \mleft( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} \mright) \,{\intersect}\, \mleft( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} \mright) } = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \) by
